It is induced by the continuous homomorphism $$ \deg : D i v (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R} $$ which sends a replete divisor $D = \sum_{\mathfrak{p}} \nu_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}$ to the real number $$ \deg (D) = \sum_ {\mathfrak {p}} \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}} \log \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) = \log \left(\prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}\right). $$ From the product formula (1.3), we find for any replete principal divisor $\mathrm{div}(f_i) \in \mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ that $$ \deg \left(\operatorname{div} (f)\right) = \sum_ {\mathfrak {p}} \log \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (f)} = \log \left(\prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \| f \| _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {- 1}\right) = 0. $$ Thus we obtain a well-defined continuous homomorphism $$ \deg : C H ^ {1} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R}. $$ The kernel $CH^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})^{0}$ of this map is made up from the unit group $O^{*}$ and the ideal class group $Cl_{K} \cong CH^{1}(\mathcal{O})$ of K as follows. | `FC09-C01-U004`, `FC09-C03-U006` |
| `FC09-C03-U020` | Proposition (1.11) | Chapter III, §1, source L7940 | (1.11) Proposition. Let $\Gamma = \lambda (\mathcal{O}^*)$ denote the complete lattice of units in trace-zero space $H = \{(x_{\mathfrak{p}})\in \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}\mathbb{R}\mid \sum_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}x_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0\}$ . There is an exact sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow H / \Gamma \longrightarrow C H ^ {1} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) ^ {0} \longrightarrow C H ^ {1} (\mathcal {O}) \longrightarrow 0. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U021` | Theorem (1.12) | Chapter III, §1, source L7966 | (1.12) Theorem. The group $CH^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})^{0}$ is compact. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U022` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §1, source L7976 | The correspondence between replete ideals and replete divisors is given by the two mutually inverse mappings $$ \begin{array}{c c} \operatorname{div}: J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow D i v (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}), & \quad \operatorname{div} (\prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}) = \sum_ {\mathfrak {p}} - \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p}, \\ D i v (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}), & \quad \sum_ {\mathfrak {p}} \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} \longmapsto \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {- \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}. \end{array} $$ These are topological isomorphisms once we equip $$ J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) = J (\mathcal {O}) \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} $$ with the product topology of the discrete topology on $J(\mathcal{O})$ and the canonical topology on $\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ . The image of a divisor $D = \sum_{\mathfrak{p}}\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}$ is also written as $$ \mathcal {O} (D) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {- \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}. $$ The minus sign here is motivated by classical usage in function theory. Replete principal ideals correspond to replete principal divisors in such a way that $P(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ becomes a discrete subgroup of $J(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ by (1.9), and $Pic(\bar{\mathcal{O}}) = J(\bar{\mathcal{O}})/P(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ is a locally compact Hausdorff topological group. We obtain the following extension of chap. I, (12.14). | `FC09-C01-U118`, `FC09-C03-U017` |
| `FC09-C03-U023` | Proposition (1.13) | Chapter III, §1, source L7996 | (1.13) Proposition. The mapping div : $J(\bar{\mathcal{O}}) \stackrel{\sim}{\longrightarrow} Div(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ induces a topological isomorphism $$ \operatorname{div}: P i c (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} C H ^ {1} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U024` | Corollary (1.14) | Chapter III, §1, source L8016 | (1.14) Corollary. The group $$ P i c (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) ^ {0} = \left\{\left[ a \right] \in P i c (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \mid \mathfrak {N} (a) = 1 \right\} $$ is compact. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U025` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §1, source L8024 | There, on an algebraic $\mathbb{Z}$ -scheme $X$ , one studies on the one hand the totality of all vector bundles, and on the other, that of all irreducible subschemes of $X$ . From the first, one constructs a series of groups $K_{i}(X)$ which constitute the subject of algebraic $K$ -theory. From the second is constructed a series of groups $CH^{i}(X)$ , the subject of Chow theory. Vector bundles are by definition locally free $\mathcal{O}_X$ -modules. In the special case $X = \operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O})$ this includes the fractional ideals. The irreducible subschemes and their formal linear combinations, i.e., the cycles of $X$ , are to be considered as generalizations of the primes and divisors. The isomorphism between divisor class group and ideal class group extends to the general setting as a homomorphic relation between the groups $CH^{i}(X)$ and $K_{i}(X)$ . Thus the initial controversy has been resolved into a seminal mathematical theory (for further reading, see [13]). | — |
| `FC09-C03-U026` | Definition (2.1) | Chapter III, §2, source L8100 | (2.1) Definition. The fractional ideal $$ \mathfrak {C} _ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} = ^ {*} \mathcal {O} = \left\{x \in L \mid T r (x \mathcal {O}) \subseteq \mathcal {O} \right\} $$ is called Dedekind's complementary module, or the inverse different. Its inverse, $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} = \mathfrak {C} _ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} ^ {- 1}, $$ is called the different of $\mathcal{O}\|\mathcal{O}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U027` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §2, source L8114 | As $\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{O}\|\mathcal{O}} \supseteq \mathcal{O}$ , the ideal $\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{O}\|\mathcal{O}} \subseteq \mathcal{O}$ is actually an integral ideal of $L$ . We will frequently denote it by $\mathfrak{D}_{L\|K}$ , provided the intended subrings $\mathcal{O}, \mathcal{O}$ are evident from the context. In the same way, we write $\mathfrak{C}_{L\|K}$ instead of $\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{O}\|\mathcal{O}}$ . The different behaves in the following manner under change of rings $\mathcal{O}$ and $\mathcal{O}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U028` | Proposition (2.2) | Chapter III, §2, source L8116 | (2.2) Proposition. (i) For a tower of fields $K \subseteq L \subseteq M$ , one has $\mathfrak{D}_{M\|K} = \mathfrak{D}_{M\|L}\mathfrak{D}_{L\|K}$ . (ii) For any multiplicative subset $S$ of $\mathcal{O}$ , one has $\mathfrak{D}_{S^{-1}\mathcal{O}\|S^{-1}\mathcal{O}} = S^{-1}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{O}\|\mathcal{O}}$ . (iii) If $\mathfrak{P}\|\mathfrak{p}$ are prime ideals of $\mathcal{O}$ , resp. $\mathcal{O}$ , and $\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}}\|\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ are the associated completions, then $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} _ {\mathfrak {P}} = \mathfrak {D} _ {\mathcal {O} _ {\mathfrak {P}} \| \mathcal {O} _ {\mathfrak {p}}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U029` | Corollary (2.3) | Chapter III, §2, source L8174 | (2.3) Corollary. $\mathfrak{D} = \prod_{\mathfrak{P}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{P}}$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U030` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8176 | The name “different” is explained by the following explicit description, which was Dedekind’s original way to define it. Let $\alpha \in O$ and let $f(X) \in \mathcal{O}[X]$ be the minimal polynomial of $\alpha$ . We define the different of the element $\alpha$ by $$ \delta_ {L \| K} (\alpha) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} f ^ {\prime} (\alpha) & \text {if} L = K (\alpha), \\ 0 & \text {if} L \neq K (\alpha). \end{array} \right. $$ In the special case where $\mathcal{O} = \mathcal{O}[\alpha]$ we then obtain: | — |
| `FC09-C03-U031` | Proposition (2.4) | Chapter III, §2, source L8184 | (2.4) Proposition. If $O = \mathcal{O}[\alpha]$ , then the different is the principal ideal $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {L \| K} = \left(\delta_ {L \| K} (\alpha)\right). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U032` | Theorem (2.5) | Chapter III, §2, source L8248 | (2.5) Theorem. The different $D_{L\|K}$ is the ideal generated by all differents of elements $\delta_{L\|K}(\alpha)$ for $\alpha \in O$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U033` | Theorem (2.6) | Chapter III, §2, source L8302 | (2.6) Theorem. A prime ideal $\mathfrak{P}$ of $L$ is ramified over $K$ if and only if $\mathfrak{P}\|\mathfrak{D}_{L\|K}$ . Let $\mathfrak{P}^s$ be the maximal power of $\mathfrak{P}$ dividing $\mathfrak{D}_{L\|K}$ , and let $e$ be the ramification index of $\mathfrak{P}$ over $K$ . Then one has $$ s = e - 1, \quad \text { if } \mathfrak {P} \text { is tamely ramified, } $$ $$ e \leq s \leq e - 1 + v _ {\mathfrak {P}} (e), \quad i f \mathfrak {P} i s w i l d l y r a m i f i e d. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U034` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8342 | The geometric significance of the different, and thus also the way it fits into higher dimensional algebraic geometry, is brought out by the following characterization, which is due to $E. K\ddot{A}HLER$ . For an arbitrary extension $B\|A$ of commutative rings, consider the homomorphism $$ \mu : B \otimes_ {A} B \longrightarrow B, \quad x \otimes y \longmapsto x y, $$ whose kernel we denote by I. Then $$ \Omega_ {B \| A} ^ {1} := I / I ^ {2} = I \otimes_ {B \otimes B} B $$ is a $B \otimes B$ -module, and hence in particular also a B-module, via the embedding $B \to B \otimes B$ , $b \mapsto b \otimes 1$ . It is called the module of differentials of $B\|A$ , and its elements are called Kähler differentials. If we put $$ d x = x \otimes 1 - 1 \otimes x \bmod I ^ {2}, $$ then we obtain a mapping $$ d: B \longrightarrow \Omega_ {B \| A} ^ {1} $$ satisfying $$ d (x y) = x d y + y d x, $$ $$ d a = 0 \quad \text { for } \quad a \in A. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U035` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8376 | Such a map is called a derivation of $B\|A$ . One can show that d is universal among all derivations of $B\|A$ with values in B-modules. $\Omega_{B\|A}^{1}$ consists of the linear combinations $\sum y_{i} dx_{i}$ . The link with the different is now this. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U036` | Proposition (2.7) | Chapter III, §2, source L8378 | (2.7) Proposition. The different $\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{O}\|\circ}$ is the annihilator of the $\mathcal{O}$ -module $\Omega_{\mathcal{O}\|\circ}^{1}$ , i.e., $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} = \left\{x \in \mathcal {O} \mid x d y = 0 \quad \text {for all} \quad y \in \mathcal {O} \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U037` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8386 | A most intimate connection holds between the different and the discriminant of $O\|O$ . The latter is defined as follows. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U038` | Definition (2.8) | Chapter III, §2, source L8388 | (2.8) Definition. The discriminant $d_{O\|O}$ is the ideal of O which is generated by the discriminants $d(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})$ of all the bases $\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}$ of L\|K which are contained in O. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U039` | Theorem (2.9) | Chapter III, §2, source L8392 | (2.9) Theorem. The following relation exists between the discriminant and the different: $$ \mathfrak {d} _ {L \| K} = N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {D} _ {L \| K}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U040` | Corollary (2.10) | Chapter III, §2, source L8412 | (2.10) Corollary. For a tower of fields $K \subseteq L \subseteq M$ , one has $$ \mathfrak {d} _ {M \| K} = \mathfrak {d} _ {L \| K} ^ {[ M: L ]} N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {d} _ {M \| L}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U041` | Corollary (2.11) | Chapter III, §2, source L8426 | (2.11) Corollary. $\mathfrak{d} = \prod_{\mathfrak{P}}\mathfrak{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U042` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8428 | The extension $L\|K$ is called unramified if all prime ideals $\mathfrak{p}$ of $K$ are unramified. This amounts to requiring that all primes of $K$ be unramified. In fact, the infinite primes are always to be regarded as unramified because $e_{\mathfrak{P}\| \mathfrak{p}} = 1$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U043` | Corollary (2.12) | Chapter III, §2, source L8430 | (2.12) Corollary. A prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ of $K$ is ramified in $L$ if and only if $\mathfrak{p}\|\mathfrak{d}$ . In particular, the extension $L\|K$ is unramified if the discriminant $\mathfrak{d} = (1)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U044` | Theorem (2.13) | Chapter III, §2, source L8434 | (2.13) Theorem. Let K be an algebraic number field and let S be a finite set of primes of K. Then there exist only finitely many extensions $L\|K$ of given degree n which are unramified outside of S. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U045` | Proposition (2.14) | Chapter III, §2, source L8466 | (2.14) Proposition. The discriminant of an algebraic number field $K$ of degree $n$ satisfies $$ \left\| d _ {K} \right\| ^ {1 / 2} \geq \frac {n ^ {n}}{n !} \left(\frac {\pi}{4}\right) ^ {n / 2}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U046` | Lemma (2.15) | Chapter III, §2, source L8510 | (2.15) Lemma. In Minkowski space $K_{\mathbb{R}} = \left[\prod_{\tau} \mathbb{C}\right]^+$ , the domain $$ X _ {t} = \left\{\left(z _ {\tau}\right) \in K _ {\mathbb {R}} \mid \sum_ {\tau} \| z _ {\tau} \| < t \right\} $$ has volume $$ \operatorname{vol} (X _ {t}) = 2 ^ {r} \pi^ {s} \frac {t ^ {n}}{n !} \cdot $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U047` | Hermite's Theorem (2.16) | Chapter III, §2, source L8608 | (2.16) Hermite's Theorem. There exist only finitely many number fields with bounded discriminant. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U048` | Theorem (2.18) | Chapter III, §2, source L8620 | (2.18) Theorem. The field $\mathbb{Q}$ does not admit any unramified extensions. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U049` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §2, source L8622 | If $\mathfrak{p}$ is a prime ideal of $K$ , then for any such curve $X$ , one may define the “reduction mod $\mathfrak{p}$ ”. This is a curve defined over the residue class field of $\mathfrak{p}$ . One says that $X$ has good reduction at the prime $\mathfrak{p}$ if its reduction mod $\mathfrak{p}$ is again a smooth curve. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U050` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L8710 | In order to mimic this state of affairs in number theory, let us recall the explanations of chap. I, §14 and chap. III, §1. We endow the places p of an algebraic number field K with the rôle of points of a space X which should be conceived of as the analogue of a compact Riemann surface. The elements $f \in K^{*}$ will be given the rôle of “meromorphic functions” on this space X. The order of the pole, resp. zero of f at the point $p \in X$ , for $p \nmid \infty$ , is defined to be the integer $v_{\mathfrak{p}}(f)$ , and for $p\|_{\infty}$ it is the real number $v_{\mathfrak{p}}(f) = -\log \|\tau f\|$ . In this way we associate to each $f \in K^{*}$ the replete divisor $$ \operatorname{div} (f) = \sum_ {\mathfrak {p}} v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (f) \mathfrak {p} \in D i v (\bar {\mathcal {O}}). $$ More precisely, for a given divisor $D = \sum_{\mathfrak{p}} \nu_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}$ , we are interested in the replete ideal $$ \mathcal {O} (D) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {- \nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} $$ and the set $$ \begin{array}{r l} H ^ {0} (\mathcal {O} (D)) & = \left\{f \in K ^ {*} \big \| \operatorname{div} (f) \geq - D \right\} \\ & = \left\{f \in \mathcal {O} (D) _ {\mathrm{f}} \big \| 0 \neq \| f \| _ {\mathfrak {p}} \leq \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \text {for} \mathfrak {p} \| \infty \right\}, \end{array} $$ where the relation $D' \geq D$ between divisors $D' = \sum_{p} v_{p}' p$ and $D = \sum_{p} v_{p} p$ is simply defined to mean $v_{p}' \geq v_{p}$ for all p. Note that $H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(D))$ is no longer a vector space. An analogue of $H^{1}(X, \mathcal{O}(D))$ is completely missing. Instead of attacking directly the problem of measuring the size of $H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(D))$ , we proceed as in the function-theoretic model by looking at the “Euler-Poincaré characteristic” of the replete ideal $\mathcal{O}(D)$ . Before defining this, we want to establish the relation between the Minkowski space $K_{R} = \left[\prod_{\tau} C\right]^{+}$ , $\tau \in \operatorname{Hom}(K, \mathbb{C})$ , and the product $\prod_{p\| \infty} K_{p}$ . The reader will allow us to explain this simple situation in the following sketch. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U051` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L8730 | We have the correspondences $$ \rho \colon K \to \mathbb {R} \quad \longmapsto \quad \mathfrak {p} \quad \text { real prime }, \rho = \rho_ {\mathfrak {p}}: K _ {\mathfrak {p}} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbb {R}, $$ $\sigma, \overline{\sigma}: K \to \mathbb{C} \quad \longmapsto \quad \mathfrak{p} \quad \text{complex prime}, \sigma = \sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}: K_{\mathfrak{p}} \stackrel{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb{C}$ . There are the following isomorphisms $$ K \otimes_ {\mathbb {Q}} \mathbb {R} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} K _ {\mathbb {R}}, \quad a \otimes x \longmapsto ((\tau a) x) _ {\tau}, $$ $$ K \otimes_ {\mathbb {Q}} \mathbb {R} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} K _ {\mathfrak {p}}, \quad a \otimes x \longmapsto ((\tau_ {\mathfrak {p}} a) x) _ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty}, $$ $\tau_{p}$ being the canonical embedding $K \rightarrow K_{p}$ (see chap. II, (8.3)). They fit into the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{r c l r c l} K \otimes \mathbb {R} & \cong & K _ {\mathbb {R}} & = & \prod_ {\rho} \mathbb {R} & \times & \prod_ {\sigma} [ \mathbb {C} \times \mathbb {C} ] ^ {+} \\ \Big \\| & & \uparrow & & \uparrow \Pi_ {\rho_ {\mathfrak {p}}} & & \uparrow \Pi_ {[ \sigma_ {\mathfrak {p}} \times \overline {{\sigma}} _ {\mathfrak {p}} ]} \\ K \otimes \mathbb {R} & \cong & \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} K _ {\mathfrak {p}}. = & & \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \text {real}} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} & \times & \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \text {complex}}, \end{array} $$ where the arrow on the right is given by $a \mapsto (\sigma a, \overline{\sigma} a)$ . Via this isomorphism, we identify $K_{\mathbb{R}}$ with $\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty} K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ : $$ K _ {\mathbb {R}} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} K _ {\mathfrak {p}}. $$ The scalar product $\langle x, y \rangle = \sum_{\tau} x_{\tau} \bar{y}_{\tau}$ on $K_{\mathbb{R}}$ is then transformed into $$ \langle x, y \rangle = \sum_ {\mathfrak {p} \text {real}} x _ {\mathfrak {p}} y _ {\mathfrak {p}} + \sum_ {\mathfrak {p} \text {complex}} (x _ {\mathfrak {p}} \bar {y} _ {\mathfrak {p}} + \bar {x} _ {\mathfrak {p}} y _ {\mathfrak {p}}). $$ The Haar measure $\mu$ on $K_{\mathbb{R}}$ which is determined by $\langle x, y \rangle$ becomes the product measure $$ \mu = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mu_ {\mathfrak {p}}, $$ where $$ \begin{array}{l} \mu_ {\mathfrak {p}} = \text { Lebesgue measure on } K _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \mathbb {R}, \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \text { real }, \\ \mu_ {\mathfrak {p}} = 2 \text { Lebesgue measure on } K _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \mathbb {C}, \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \text { complex }. \end{array} $$ Indeed, the system $1 / \sqrt{2}$ , $i / \sqrt{2}$ is an orthonormal basis with respect to the scalar product $x\overline{y} + \overline{x}y$ on $K_{\mathfrak{p}} = \mathbb{C}$ . Hence the square $Q = \{z = x + iy \mid 0 \leq x, y \leq 1 / \sqrt{2}\}$ has volume $\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(Q) = 1$ , but Lebesgue volume $1/2$ . Finally, the logarithm map $$ \ell : \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {C} ^ {*} \right] ^ {+} \longrightarrow \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {R} \right] ^ {+}, \quad x \longmapsto (\log \| x _ {\tau} \|) _ {\tau} $$ studied in Minkowski theory is transformed into the mapping $$ \ell : \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \longrightarrow \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R}, \quad x \longmapsto \left(\log \| x _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| _ {\mathfrak {p}}\right), $$ for one has the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} K _ {\mathbb {R}} ^ {*} & \xrightarrow {\ell} & [ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {R} ] ^ {+} \\ \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow \\ \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} & \xrightarrow {\ell} & \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R}, \end{array} $$ where the arrow on the right, $$ \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {R} \right] ^ {+} = \prod_ {\rho} \mathbb {R} \times \prod_ {\sigma} [ \mathbb {R} \times \mathbb {R} ] ^ {+} \longrightarrow \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R}, $$ is defined by $x \mapsto x$ for $\rho \leftrightarrow p$ , and by $(x, x) \mapsto 2x$ for $\sigma \leftrightarrow p$ . This isomorphism takes the trace map $x \mapsto \sum_{\tau} x_{\tau}$ on $\left[\prod_{\tau} R\right]^{+}$ into the trace map $x \mapsto \sum_{p \mid \infty} x_{p}$ on $\prod_{p \mid \infty} R$ , and hence the trace-zero space $H = \left\{ x \in \left[\prod_{\tau} R\right]^+ \mid \sum_{\tau} x_{\tau} = 0 \right\}$ into the trace-zero space $$ H = \left\{x \in \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} \mid \sum_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} x _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 0 \right\}. $$ In this way we have translated all necessary invariants of the Minkowski space $K_{R}$ to the product $\prod_{p\|\infty} K_{p}$ . | `FC09-C02-U099` |
| `FC09-C03-U052` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L8812 | To a given replete ideal $$ \mathfrak {a} = \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{f}} \cdot \mathfrak {a} _ {\infty} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \nmid \infty} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \mid \infty} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} $$ we now associate the following complete lattice $ja$ in $K_{\mathbb{R}}$ . The fractional ideal $\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{f}} \subseteq K$ is mapped by the embedding $j: K \to K_{\mathbb{R}}$ onto a complete lattice $j\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{f}}$ of $K_{\mathbb{R}} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ . By componentwise multiplication, $\mathfrak{a}_{\infty} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}} = (\dots ,e^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}},\dots)_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty}$ yields an isomorphism $$ \mathfrak {a} _ {\infty}: K _ {\mathbb {R}} \to K _ {\mathbb {R}}, (x _ {\mathfrak {p}}) _ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mapsto (e ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} x _ {\mathfrak {p}}) _ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty}, $$ with determinant $$ \det \left(a _ {\infty}\right) = \prod_ {p \mid \infty} e ^ {\nu_ {p} f _ {p}} = \prod_ {p \mid \infty} \mathfrak {N} (p) ^ {\nu_ {p}} = \mathfrak {N} \left(a _ {\infty}\right).\tag{*} $$ The image of the lattice $ja_{f}$ under this map is a complete lattice $$ j \mathfrak {a} := \mathfrak {a} _ {\infty} j \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{f}}. $$ Let $\text{vol}(\mathfrak{a})$ denote the volume of a fundamental mesh of $j\mathfrak{a}$ with respect to the canonical measure. By (\*), we then have $$ \operatorname{vol} (a) = \mathfrak {N} (a _ {\infty}) \operatorname{vol} (a _ {f}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U053` | Definition (3.1) | Chapter III, §3, source L8842 | (3.1) Definition. If $\mathfrak{a}$ is a replete ideal of $K$ , then the real number $$ \chi (\mathfrak {a}) = - \log \operatorname{vol} (\mathfrak {a}) $$ will be called the Euler-Minkowski characteristic of a. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U054` | Proposition (3.2) | Chapter III, §3, source L8852 | (3.2) Proposition. The Euler-Minkowski characteristic $\chi(\mathfrak{a})$ only depends on the class of $\mathfrak{a}$ in $Pic(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) = J(\overline{\mathcal{O}})/P(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U055` | Proposition (3.3) | Chapter III, §3, source L8882 | (3.3) Proposition. For every replete ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $K$ one has $$ \operatorname{vol} (\mathfrak {a}) = \sqrt {\| d _ {K} \|} \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U056` | Proposition (3.4) | Chapter III, §3, source L8914 | (3.4) Proposition. For every replete ideal of K we have the formula $$ \chi (\mathfrak {a}) = \deg (\mathfrak {a}) + \chi (\mathcal {O}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U057` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L8926 | There is no immediate analogue of $H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})$ in arithmetic. However, there is an analogue of $H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})$ . For each replete ideal $a=\prod_{p}p^{\nu_{p}}$ of the number field K, we define $$ H ^ {0} (\mathfrak {a}) = \left\{f \in K ^ {*} \mid v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (f) \geq v _ {\mathfrak {p}} \text { for all } \mathfrak {p} \right\}. $$ This is a finite set because $jH^0(\mathfrak{a})$ lies in the part of the lattice $j\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{f}} \subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}$ which is bounded by the conditions $\|f\|_{\mathfrak{p}} \leq e^{-\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}f_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ , $\mathfrak{p}\|\infty$ . As the analogue of the dimension, we put $\ell(\mathfrak{a}) = 0$ if $H^0(\mathfrak{a}) = \emptyset$ , and in all other cases $$ \ell (\mathfrak {a}) := \log \frac {\# H ^ {0} (\mathfrak {a})}{\operatorname{vol} (W)}, $$ where the normalizing factor $\operatorname{vol}(W)$ is the volume of the set $$ W = \left\{\left(z _ {\tau}\right) \in K _ {\mathbb {R}} = \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {C} \right] ^ {+} \mid \| z _ {\tau} \| \leq 1 \right\}. $$ This volume is given explicitly by | — |
| `FC09-C03-U058` | Definition (3.5) | Chapter III, §3, source L8958 | (3.5) Definition. The genus of a number field $K$ is defined to be the real number $\\| x(K) - \sqrt{1 - x}$ $$ g = \ell (\mathcal {O}) - \chi (\mathcal {O}) = \log \frac {\# \mu (K) \sqrt {\| d _ {K} \|}}{2 ^ {r} (2 \pi) ^ {s}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U059` | Proposition (3.6) | Chapter III, §3, source L8966 | (3.6) Proposition. For every replete ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $K$ one has $$ \chi (\mathfrak {a}) = \deg (\mathfrak {a}) + \ell (\mathcal {O}) - g. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U060` | Theorem (3.7) | Chapter III, §3, source L8980 | (3.7) Theorem (S. LANG). For replete ideals $\mathfrak{a} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}} \in J(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , one has $$ \# H ^ {0} (\mathfrak {a} ^ {- 1}) = \frac {2 ^ {r} (2 \pi) ^ {s}}{\sqrt {\| d _ {K} \|}} \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}) + O \left(\mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}) ^ {1 - \frac {1}{n}}\right) $$ if $\mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{a})\to \infty$ . Here, as usual, $O(t)$ denotes a function such that $O(t) / t$ remains bounded as $t\to \infty$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U061` | Lemma (3.8) | Chapter III, §3, source L8990 | (3.8) Lemma. Let $\mathfrak{a}_1, \ldots, \mathfrak{a}_h$ be fractional ideals representing the classes of the finite ideal class group $Pic(\mathcal{O})$ . Let $c$ be a positive constant and $$ \mathfrak {A} _ {i} = \left\{\mathfrak {a} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \mid \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{f}} = \mathfrak {a} _ {i}, \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \leq c \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}) ^ {f _ {\mathfrak {p}} / n} f o r \mathfrak {p} \| \infty \right\}. $$ Then the constant $c$ can be chosen in such a way that $$ J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) = \bigcup_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \mathfrak {A} _ {i} P (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U062` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L9168 | We call the number $$ i (D) = \ell (D) - \chi (D) $$ the index of specialty of $D$ and get the | — |
| `FC09-C03-U063` | Theorem (3.9) | Chapter III, §3, source L9176 | (3.9) Theorem (Riemann-Roch). For every replete divisor $D \in Div(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ we have the formula $$ \ell (D) = \deg (D) + \ell (\mathcal {O}) - g + i (D). $$ The index of specialty $i(D)$ satisfies $$ i (D) = O \left(e ^ {- \frac {1}{n} \deg (D)}\right), $$ in particular, $i(D)\to 0$ for $\deg (D)\to \infty$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U064` | Definition (3.10) | Chapter III, §3, source L9212 | (3.10) Definition. The fractional ideal $$ \omega_ {K} = \mathfrak {C} _ {K \| \mathbb {Q}} \cong \operatorname{Hom} _ {\mathbb {Z}} (\mathcal {O}, \mathbb {Z}) $$ is called the canonical module of the number field K. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U065` | Proposition (3.11) | Chapter III, §3, source L9222 | (3.11) Proposition. The canonical modules of L and K satisfy the relation $$ \omega_ {L} = \mathfrak {C} _ {L \| K} \omega_ {K}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U066` | Proposition (3.12) | Chapter III, §3, source L9234 | (3.12) Proposition. $\deg \omega_K = -2\chi (\mathcal{O}) = 2g - 2\ell (\mathcal{O}).$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U067` | Proposition (3.13) | Chapter III, §3, source L9248 | (3.13) Proposition. Let $L\|K$ be a finite extension and $g_{L}$ , resp. $g_{K}$ , the genus of $L$ , resp. $K$ . Then one has $$ g _ {L} - \ell (\mathcal {O} _ {L}) = [ L: K ] \big (g _ {K} - \ell (\mathcal {O} _ {K}) \big) + \frac {1}{2} \deg \mathfrak {C} _ {L \| K}. $$ In particular, in the case of an unramified extension $L\|K$ : $$ \chi (\mathcal {O} _ {L}) = [ L: K ] \chi (\mathcal {O} _ {K}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U068` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §3, source L9300 | Distinguishing the invariants of Riemann-Roch theory with respect to the Minkowski measure by a tilde, we get the relations $$ \widetilde {\chi} (\mathfrak {a}) = \chi (\mathfrak {a}) + \log 2 ^ {s}, \quad \widetilde {\ell} (\mathfrak {a}) = \ell (\mathfrak {a}) + \log 2 ^ {s} $$ (the latter in case that $H^0(\mathfrak{a}) \neq \emptyset$ ), whereas the genus remains unchanged. Substituting this into the Riemann-Hurwitz formula (3.13) preserves its shape only if one enriches $\mathfrak{C}_{L\|K}$ into a replete ideal in which all infinite primes $\mathfrak{P}$ such that $L_{\mathfrak{P}} \neq K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ occur. This forces us to consider the extension $\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R}$ as ramified, to put $\tilde{e}_{\mathfrak{P}\| \mathfrak{p}} = [L_{\mathfrak{P}} : K_{\mathfrak{p}}]$ , $\widetilde{f}_{\mathfrak{P}\| \mathfrak{p}} = 1$ , and in particular $$ \tilde {e} _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \left[ K _ {\mathfrak {p}}: \mathbb {R} \right], \quad \widetilde {f} _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 1. $$ The following modifications ensue from this. For an infinite prime $\mathfrak{p}$ one has to define $$ \tilde {v} _ {\mathfrak {p}} (a) = - \tilde {e} _ {\mathfrak {p}} \log \| \tau a \|, \quad \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu} = e ^ {\nu / \tilde {e} _ {\mathfrak {p}}}, \quad \widetilde {\mathfrak {N}} (\mathfrak {p}) = e. $$ The absolute norm as well as the degree of a replete ideal a remain unaltered: $$ \widetilde {\mathfrak {N}} (\mathfrak {a}) = \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}), \quad \widetilde {\deg} (\mathfrak {a}) = - \log \widetilde {\mathfrak {N}} (\mathfrak {a}) = \deg (\mathfrak {a}). $$ The canonical module $\omega_{K}$ however has to be changed: $$ \tilde {\omega} _ {K} = \omega_ {K} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \text { complex }} \mathfrak {p} ^ {2 \log 2}, $$ in order for the equation $$ \deg \tilde {\omega} _ {K} = - 2 \widetilde {\chi} (\mathcal {O}) = 2 g - 2 \tilde {\ell} (\mathcal {O}) $$ to hold. By the same token, the ideal $\mathfrak{C}_{L\|K}$ has to be replaced by the replete ideal $$ \tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{L\|K} = \mathfrak{C}_{L\|K}\prod_{\substack{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}\|\infty \\ e_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}}\neq 1}}\mathfrak{P}^{2\log 2} $$ so that $$ \tilde {\omega} _ {L} = \tilde {\mathfrak {C}} _ {L \| K} i _ {L \| K} (\tilde {\omega} _ {K}). $$ In the same way as in (3.13), this yields the Riemann-Hurwitz formula $$ g _ {L} - \tilde {\ell} (\mathcal {O} _ {L}) = [ L: K ] \big (g _ {K} - \tilde {\ell} (\mathcal {O} _ {K}) \big) + \frac {1}{2} \deg \tilde {\mathfrak {C}} _ {L \| K}. $$ | `FC09-C03-U067` |
| `FC09-C03-U069` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L9354 | In view of this sensitivity to the chosen metric on Minkowski space $K_{R}$ , the mathematician UWE JANNSEN proposes as analogues of the function fields not just number fields K by themselves but number fields equipped with a metric of the type $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {K} = \sum_ {\tau} \alpha_ {\tau} x _ {\tau} \overline {{{{y}}}} _ {\tau}, $$ $\alpha_{\tau}>0,\alpha_{\tau}=\alpha_{\overline{\tau}}$ , on $K_{R}$ . Let these new objects be called metrized number fields. This idea does indeed do justice to the situation in question in a very precise manner, and it is of fundamental importance for algebraic number theory. We denote metrized number fields $(K,\langle,\rangle_{K})$ as $\tilde{K}$ and attach to them the following invariants. Let $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {K} = \sum_ {\tau} \alpha_ {\tau} x _ {\tau} \overline {{{{y}}}} _ {\tau}. $$ Let $\mathfrak{p} = \mathfrak{p}_{\tau}$ be the infinite prime corresponding to $\tau: K \to \mathbb{C}$ . We then put $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} = \alpha_{\tau}$ . At the same time, we also use the letter $\mathfrak{p}$ for the positive real number $$ \mathfrak {p} = e ^ {\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \in \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, $$ which we interpret as the replete ideal $(1) \times (1, \cdots, 1, e^{\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}}, 1, \cdots, 1) \in J(\mathcal{O}) \times \prod_{\mathfrak{p} \| \infty} \mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ . We put $$ e _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 1 / \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} \quad \text { and } \quad f _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} [ K _ {\mathfrak {p}}: \mathbb {R} ], $$ and we define the valuation $v_{\mathfrak{p}}$ of $K^{*}$ associated to $\mathfrak{p}$ by $$ v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (a) = - e _ {\mathfrak {p}} \log \| \tau a \|. $$ Further, we put $$ \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) = e ^ {f _ {\mathfrak {p}}} \quad \text { and } \quad \| a \| _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {- v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (a)}, $$ so that again $\|a\|_{\mathfrak{p}} = \|\tau a\|$ if $\mathfrak{p}$ is real, and $\|a\|_{\mathfrak{p}} = \|\tau a\|^2$ if $\mathfrak{p}$ is complex. For every replete ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $K$ , there is a unique representation $\mathfrak{a} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ , which gives the absolute norm $\mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{a}) = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ , and the degree $$ \deg_ {\tilde {K}} (\mathfrak {a}) = - \log \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U070` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L9396 | The canonical module of $\widetilde{K}$ is defined to be the replete ideal $$ \omega_ {\tilde {K}} = \omega_ {K} \cdot \omega_ {\infty} \in J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) = J (\mathcal {O}) \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, $$ where $\omega_{K}$ is the inverse of the different $\mathfrak{D}_{K\|\mathbb{Q}}$ of $K\|\mathbb{Q}$ , and $$ \omega_ {\infty} = \left(\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {- 1}\right) _ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \in \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U071` | Proposition (3.14) | Chapter III, §3, source L9434 | (3.14) Proposition. The metrized fields $(\mathbb{Q}, \alpha xy)$ are the only metrized number fields of genus 0. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U072` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L9454 | An extension of metrized number fields is a pair $\widetilde{K} = (K, \langle , \rangle_K)$ , $\widetilde{L} = (L, \langle , \rangle_L)$ , such that $K \subseteq L$ and the metrics $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {K} = \sum_ {\tau} \alpha_ {\tau} x _ {\tau} \overline {{{y}}} _ {\tau}, \quad \langle x, y \rangle_ {L} = \sum_ {\sigma} \beta_ {\sigma} x _ {\sigma} \overline {{{y}}} _ {\sigma} $$ satisfy the relation $\alpha_{\tau} \geq \beta_{\sigma}$ whenever $\tau = \sigma\|_K$ . If $\mathfrak{P}\| \mathfrak{p}$ are infinite primes of $L\|K$ , $\mathfrak{P}$ belonging to $\sigma$ and $\mathfrak{p}$ to $\tau = \sigma\|_K$ , we define the ramification index and inertia degree by $$ e _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} = \alpha_ {\tau} / \beta_ {\sigma} \quad \text { and } \quad f _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} = \beta_ {\sigma} / \alpha_ {\tau} [ L _ {\mathfrak {P}}: K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ]. $$ Thus the fundamental identity $$ \sum_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} e _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} f _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} = [ L: K ] $$ is preserved. Also P is unramified if and only if $\alpha_{\tau} = \beta_{\sigma}$ . For “replete prime ideals” $p = e^{\alpha_{\tau}}$ , $P = e^{\beta_{\sigma}}$ , we put $$ i _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {p}) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {P} ^ {e _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}}}, \quad N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {P}) = \mathfrak {p} ^ {f _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U073` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §3, source L9478 | Finally we define the different of $\widetilde{L}\|\widetilde{K}$ to be the replete ideal $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {\tilde {L} \| \tilde {K}} = \mathfrak {D} _ {L \| K} \cdot \mathfrak {D} _ {\infty} \in J (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}} _ {L}) = J (\mathcal {O} _ {L}) \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, $$ where $\mathfrak{D}_{L\|K}$ is the different of $L\|K$ and $$ \mathfrak {D} _ {\infty} = (\beta_ {\mathfrak {P}} / \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}) _ {\mathfrak {P} \| \infty} \in \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \infty} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, $$ where $\beta_{\mathfrak{P}} = \beta_{\sigma}$ and $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} = \alpha_{\tau}$ ( $\mathfrak{P}$ belongs to $\sigma$ and $\mathfrak{p}$ to $\tau = \sigma\|_K$ ). With this convention, we obtain the general Riemann-Hurwitz formula | — |
| `FC09-C03-U074` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §4, source L9526 | The field $K$ is embedded in $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ via $$ K \longrightarrow K \otimes_ {\mathbb {Q}} \mathbb {C}, \quad a \longmapsto a \otimes 1, $$ and we identify it with its image. In the interpretation (2), the image of $a \in K$ appears as the tuple $\bigoplus_{\sigma} \sigma a$ of conjugates of a, and in the interpretation (3) as the function $x(\sigma) = \sigma a$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U075` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §4, source L9534 | We denote the generator of the Galois group $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ by $F_{\infty}$ , or simply by $F$ . This underlines the fact that it has a position analogous to the Frobenius automorphism $F_{p} \in G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\|\mathbb{F}_{p})$ , in accordance with our decision of §1 to view the extension $\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R}$ as unramified. $F$ induces an involution $F$ on $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ which, in the representation $K_{\mathbb{C}} = \operatorname{Hom}(X(\mathbb{C}), \mathbb{C})$ for $x: X(\mathbb{C}) \to \mathbb{C}$ , is given by $$ (F x) (\sigma) = \overline {{x (\overline {{\sigma}})}}. $$ F is an automorphism of the R-algebra $K_{C}$ . It is called the Frobenius correspondence. Sometimes we also consider, besides F, the involution $z \mapsto \bar{z}$ on $K_{C}$ which is given by $$ \bar {z} (\sigma) = \overline {{z (\sigma)}}. $$ We call it the conjugation. Finally, we call an element $x \in K_{C}$ , that is, a function $x : X(\mathbb{C}) \to \mathbb{C}$ , positive (written x > 0) if it takes real values, and if $x(\sigma) > 0$ for all $\sigma \in X(\mathbb{C})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U076` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §4, source L9548 | By convention every O-module considered in the sequel will be supposed to be finitely generated. For every such O-module M, we put $$ M _ {\mathbb {C}} = M \otimes_ {\mathbb {Z}} \mathbb {C}. $$ This is a module over the ring $K_{C} = O \otimes_{Z} C$ , and viewing O as a subring of $K_{C}$ – as we agreed above – we may also write $$ M _ {\mathbb {C}} = M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} K _ {\mathbb {C}} $$ as $M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C} = M\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C})$ . The involution $x\mapsto Fx$ on $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ induces the involution | — |
| `FC09-C03-U077` | Definition (4.1) | Chapter III, §4, source L9572 | (4.1) Definition. A hermitian metric on the $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ -module $M_{\mathbb{C}}$ is a sesquilinear mapping $$ \langle , \rangle_ {M}: M _ {\mathbb {C}} \times M _ {\mathbb {C}} \longrightarrow K _ {\mathbb {C}}, $$ i.e., a $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ -linear form $\langle x, y \rangle_M$ in the first variable satisfying $$ \overline {{\langle x , y \rangle}} _ {M} = \langle y, x \rangle_ {M}, $$ such that one has $\langle x, x \rangle_M > 0$ for $x \neq 0$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U078` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9586 | The metric $\langle ,\rangle_{M}$ is called $F$ -invariant if we have furthermore $$ F \langle x, y \rangle_ {M} = \langle F x, F y \rangle_ {M}. $$ This notion may be immediately reduced to the usual notion of a hermitian metric if we view the $K_{C}$ -module $M_{C}$ , by means of the decomposition $K_{C} = \bigoplus_{\sigma} C$ , as a direct sum $$ M _ {\mathbb {C}} = M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} K _ {\mathbb {C}} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} M _ {\sigma} $$ of $\mathbb{C}$ -vector spaces $$ M _ {\sigma} = M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}, \sigma} \mathbb {C}. $$ The hermitian metric $\langle\ ,\rangle_{M}$ then splits into the direct sum | — |
| `FC09-C03-U079` | Definition (4.2) | Chapter III, §4, source L9616 | (4.2) Definition. A metrized O-module is a finitely generated O-module M with an F -invariant hermitian metric on $M_{C}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U080` | Example / comparison | Chapter III, §4, source L9618 | Example 1: Every fractional ideal $\mathfrak{a} \subseteq K$ of $\mathcal{O}$ , in particular $\mathcal{O}$ itself, may be equipped with the trivial metric $$ \langle x, y \rangle = x \overline {{y}} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} x _ {\sigma} \overline {{y}} _ {\sigma} $$ on $\mathfrak{a} \otimes_{\mathbb{Z}} \mathbb{C} = K \otimes_{\mathbb{Q}} \mathbb{C} = K_{\mathbb{C}}$ . All the $F$ -invariant hermitian metrics on $\mathfrak{a}$ are obtained as $$ \alpha \langle x, y \rangle = \alpha x \overline {{{y}}} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma} \alpha (\sigma) x _ {\sigma} \overline {{{y}}} _ {\sigma}, $$ where $\alpha \in K_{\mathbb{C}}$ varies over the functions $\alpha : X(\mathbb{C}) \to \mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ such that $\alpha(\overline{\sigma}) = \alpha(\sigma)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U081` | Example / comparison | Chapter III, §4, source L9632 | Example 2: Let $L\|K$ be a finite extension and $\mathfrak{A}$ a fractional ideal of $L$ , which we view as an $\mathcal{O}$ -module $M$ . If $Y(\mathbb{C}) = \operatorname{Hom}(L, \mathbb{C})$ , we have the restriction map $Y(\mathbb{C}) \to X(\mathbb{C})$ , $\tau \mapsto \tau\|_K$ , and we write $\tau\|\sigma$ if $\sigma = \tau\|_K$ . For the complexification $M_{\mathbb{C}} = \mathfrak{A} \otimes_{\mathbb{Z}} \mathbb{C} = L_{\mathbb{C}}$ , we obtain the decomposition $$ M _ {\mathbb {C}} = \bigoplus_ {\tau \in Y (\mathbb {C})} \mathbb {C} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} M _ {\sigma}, $$ where $M_{\sigma} = \bigoplus_{\tau\|_{\sigma}} C$ . M is turned into a metrized O-module by fixing the standard metrics $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {M _ {\sigma}} = \sum_ {\tau \| \sigma} x _ {\tau} \overline {{{{y}}}} _ {\tau} $$ on the $[L:K]$ -dimensional $\mathbb{C}$ -vector spaces $M_{\sigma}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U082` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9646 | If $M$ and $M'$ are metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules, then so is their direct sum $M \oplus M'$ , the tensor product $M \otimes M'$ , the dual $\check{M} = \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(M, \mathcal{O})$ and the $n$ -th exterior power $\bigwedge^{n} M$ . In fact, we have that $$ (M \oplus M ^ {\prime}) _ {\mathbb {C}} = M _ {\mathbb {C}} \oplus M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime}, (M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} M ^ {\prime}) _ {\mathbb {C}} = M _ {\mathbb {C}} \otimes_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime}, $$ $$ \check {M} _ {\mathbb {C}} = \operatorname{Hom} _ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} (M _ {\mathbb {C}}, K _ {\mathbb {C}}), \quad (\bigwedge^ {n} M) _ {\mathbb {C}} = \bigwedge_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} ^ {n} M _ {\mathbb {C}}, $$ and the metrics on these $K_{\mathbb{C}}$ -modules are given by $$ \langle x \oplus x ^ {\prime}, y \oplus y ^ {\prime} \rangle_ {M \oplus M ^ {\prime}} = \langle x, y \rangle_ {M} + \langle x ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime} \rangle_ {M ^ {\prime}}, \quad \text {resp.} $$ $$ \langle x \otimes x ^ {\prime}, y \otimes y ^ {\prime} \rangle_ {M \otimes M ^ {\prime}} = \langle x, y \rangle_ {M} \cdot \langle x ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime} \rangle_ {M ^ {\prime}}, \quad \text {resp.} $$ $$ \langle \check {x}, \check {y} \rangle_ {\check {M}} = \overline {{\langle x , y \rangle}} _ {M}, \qquad \mathrm{resp.} $$ $$ \left\langle x _ {1} \wedge \dots \wedge x _ {n}, y _ {1} \wedge \dots \wedge y _ {n} \right\rangle_ {\wedge^ {n} M} = \det \bigl (\langle x _ {i}, y _ {j} \rangle_ {M} \bigr). $$ Here $\check{x}$ , in the case of the module $\check{M}_{\mathbb{C}}$ , denotes the homomorphism $\check{x} = \langle ,x\rangle_{M}:M_{\mathbb{C}}\to K_{\mathbb{C}}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U083` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9676 | Among all $\mathcal{O}$ -modules $M$ the projective ones play a special rôle. They are defined by the condition that for every exact sequence of $\mathcal{O}$ -modules $F' \to F \to F''$ the sequence $$ \operatorname{Hom} _ {\mathcal {O}} (M, F ^ {\prime}) \longrightarrow \operatorname{Hom} _ {\mathcal {O}} (M, F) \longrightarrow \operatorname{Hom} _ {\mathcal {O}} (M, F ^ {\prime \prime}) $$ is also exact. This is equivalent to any of the following conditions (the last two, because o is a Dedekind domain). For the proof, we refer the reader to standard textbooks of commutative algebra (see for instance [90], chap. IV, §3, or [16], chap. 7, §4). | — |
| `FC09-C03-U084` | Proposition (4.3) | Chapter III, §4, source L9684 | (4.3) Proposition. For any finitely generated $\mathcal{O}$ -module $M$ the following conditions are equivalent: (i) $M$ is projective, (ii) $M$ is a direct summand of a finitely generated free $\mathcal{O}$ -module, (iii) $M$ is locally free, i.e., $M \otimes_{\mathcal{O}} \mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is a free $\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ -module for any prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ , (iv) $M$ is torsion free, i.e., the map $M \to M$ , $x \mapsto ax$ , is injective for all nonzero $a \in \mathcal{O}$ , (v) $M \cong \mathfrak{a} \oplus \mathcal{O}^n$ for some ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $\mathcal{O}$ and some integer $n \geq 0$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U085` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9696 | In order to distinguish them from projective O-modules, we will henceforth call arbitrary finitely generated O-modules coherent. The rank of a coherent O-module M is defined to be the dimension $$ \operatorname{rk} (M) = \dim_ {K} (M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} K). $$ The projective $\mathcal{O}$ -modules $L$ of rank 1 are called invertible $\mathcal{O}$ -modules, because for them $L \otimes_{\mathcal{O}} \check{L} \to \mathcal{O}$ , $a \otimes \check{a} \mapsto \check{a}(a)$ , is an isomorphism. The invertible $\mathcal{O}$ -modules are either fractional ideals of $K$ , or isomorphic to a fractional ideal as $\mathcal{O}$ -modules. Indeed, if $L$ is projective of rank 1 and $\alpha \in L$ , $\alpha \neq 0$ , then, by (4.3), (iv), mapping $$ L \longrightarrow L \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} K = K (\alpha \otimes 1), \quad x \longmapsto f (x) (\alpha \otimes 1), $$ gives an injective $\mathcal{O}$ -module homomorphism $L \to K$ , $x \mapsto f(x)$ , onto a fractional ideal $\mathfrak{a} \subseteq K$ . | `FC09-C03-U084` |
| `FC09-C03-U086` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9710 | To see the connection with the Riemann-Roch theory of the last section, which we are about to generalize, we observe that every replete ideal $$ \mathfrak {a} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \nmid \infty} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| \infty} \mathfrak {p} ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}} = \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{f}} \mathfrak {a} _ {\infty} $$ of $K$ defines an invertible, metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module. In fact, the identity $\mathfrak{a}_{\infty} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty} \mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ yields the function $$ \alpha : X (\mathbb {C}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R} _ {+}, \quad \alpha (\sigma) = e ^ {2 \nu_ {\mathfrak {p} \sigma}}, $$ where $p_{\sigma}$ denotes as before the infinite place defined by $\sigma: K \to C$ . Since $p_{\overline{\sigma}} = p_{\sigma}$ , one has $\alpha(\overline{\sigma}) = \alpha(\sigma)$ , and we obtain on the complexification $$ \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{fC}} = \mathfrak {a} _ {\mathrm{f}} \otimes_ {\mathbb {Z}} \mathbb {C} = K _ {\mathbb {C}} $$ the F-invariant hermitian metric $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {a} = \alpha x \overline {{y}} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} e ^ {2 \nu_ {\mathfrak {p} \sigma}} x _ {\sigma} \overline {{y}} _ {\sigma} $$ (see example 1, p. 227). We denote the metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module thus obtained by $L(\mathfrak{a})$ . The ordinary fractional ideals, i.e., the replete ideals $\mathfrak{a}$ such that $\mathfrak{a}_{\infty} = 1$ , and in particular $\mathcal{O}$ itself, are thus equipped with the trivial metric $\langle x, y \rangle_{\mathfrak{a}} = \langle x, y \rangle = x\overline{y}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U087` | Definition (4.4) | Chapter III, §4, source L9738 | (4.4) Definition. Two metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules $M$ and $M'$ are called isometric if there exists an isomorphism $$ f: M \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} $$ of $\mathcal{O}$ -modules which induces an isometry $f_{\mathbb{C}}: M_{\mathbb{C}} \to M_{\mathbb{C}}'$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U088` | Proposition (4.5) | Chapter III, §4, source L9746 | (4.5) Proposition. (i) Two replete ideals $\mathfrak{a}$ and $\mathfrak{b}$ define isometric metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules $L(\mathfrak{a})$ and $L(\mathfrak{b})$ if and only if they differ by a replete principal ideal $[a]$ : $\mathfrak{a} = \mathfrak{b}[a]$ . (ii) Every invertible metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module is isometric to an $\mathcal{O}$ -module $L(\mathfrak{a})$ . (iii) $L(\mathfrak{ab}) \cong L(\mathfrak{a}) \otimes_{\mathcal{O}} L(\mathfrak{b}), L(\mathfrak{a}^{-1}) = \check{L}(\mathfrak{a})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U089` | Definition (4.6) | Chapter III, §4, source L9836 | (4.6) Definition. A short exact sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} \stackrel {\alpha} {\longrightarrow} M \stackrel {\beta} {\longrightarrow} M ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ of metrized O-modules is by definition a short exact sequence of the underlying O-modules which splits isometrically, i.e., in the sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime} \stackrel {\alpha_ {\mathbb {C}}} {\longrightarrow} M _ {\mathbb {C}} \stackrel {\beta_ {\mathbb {C}}} {\longrightarrow} M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0, $$ $M_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}$ is mapped isometrically onto $\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}M_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}$ , and the orthogonal complement $(\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}M_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime})^{\perp}$ is mapped isometrically onto $M_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime\prime}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U090` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §4, source L9850 | The homomorphisms $\alpha$ , $\beta$ in a short exact sequence of metrized O-modules are called an admissible monomorphism, resp. epimorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U091` | Proposition (4.7) | Chapter III, §4, source L9858 | (4.7) Proposition. If $0 \to M' \xrightarrow{\alpha} M \xrightarrow{\beta} M'' \to 0$ is a short exact sequence of projective metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules, we have a canonical isometry $$ \det M ^ {\prime} \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \det M ^ {\prime \prime} \cong \det M. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U092` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §5, source L9932 | We will now manufacture two abelian groups from the collection of all metrized O-modules, resp. the collection of all projective metrized O-modules. We denote by $\{M\}$ the isometry class of a metrized O-module M and form the free abelian group $$ F _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) = \bigoplus_ {\{M \}} \mathbb {Z} \{M \}, \quad \text {resp.} \quad F ^ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) = \bigoplus_ {\{M \}} \mathbb {Z} \{M \}, $$ on the isometry classes of projective, resp. coherent, metrized o-modules. In this group, we consider the subgroup $$ R _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \subseteq F _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}), \quad \text { resp. } \quad R ^ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \subseteq F ^ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}), $$ generated by all elements $\{M'\} - \{M\} + \{M''\}$ which arise from a short exact sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ of projective, resp. coherent, metrized O-modules. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U093` | Definition (5.1) | Chapter III, §5, source L9952 | (5.1) Definition. The quotient groups $$ K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) = F _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) / R _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}), \quad r e s p. \quad K ^ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) = F ^ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) / R ^ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) $$ are called the replete (or compactified) Grothendieck groups of $\mathcal{O}$ . If $M$ is a metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module, then $[M]$ denotes the class it defines in $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , resp. $K^0 (\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U094` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §5, source L10004 | Associating to the class $[M]$ of a projective O-module M in $K_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ its class in $K^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ (which again is denoted by $[M]$ ), defines a homomorphism $$ K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K ^ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}). $$ It is called the Poincaré homomorphism. We will show next that the Poincaré homomorphism is an isomorphism. The proof is based on the following two lemmas. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U095` | Lemma (5.2) | Chapter III, §5, source L10012 | (5.2) Lemma. All coherent metrized O-modules M admit a “metrized projective resolution”, i.e., a short exact sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow E \longrightarrow F \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow 0 $$ of metrized O-modules in which E and F are projective. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U096` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §5, source L10034 | In a diagram of metrized projective resolutions of M $$ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} 0 \longrightarrow & E & \longrightarrow & F & \longrightarrow & M \\ & \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow & & \Big \\| \\ 0 \longrightarrow & E ^ {\prime} & \longrightarrow & F ^ {\prime} & \longrightarrow & M \\ \end{array} \longrightarrow 0 $$ the resolution in the top line will be called dominant if the vertical arrows are admissible epimorphisms. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U097` | Lemma (5.3) | Chapter III, §5, source L10042 | (5.3) Lemma. Let $$ 0 \longrightarrow E ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow F ^ {\prime} \stackrel {f ^ {\prime}} {\longrightarrow} M \longrightarrow 0, \quad 0 \longrightarrow E ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow F ^ {\prime \prime} \stackrel {f ^ {\prime \prime}} {\longrightarrow} M \longrightarrow 0 $$ be two metrized projective resolutions of the metrized O-module M. Then, taking the O-module $$ F = F ^ {\prime} \times_ {M} F ^ {\prime \prime} = \left\{(x ^ {\prime}, x ^ {\prime \prime}) \in F ^ {\prime} \times F ^ {\prime \prime} \mid f ^ {\prime} (x ^ {\prime}) = f ^ {\prime \prime} (x ^ {\prime \prime}) \right\} $$ and the mapping $f: F \to M, (x', x'') \mapsto f'(x') = f''(x'')$ , one obtains a third metrized projective resolution $$ 0 \longrightarrow E \longrightarrow F \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow 0 $$ with kernel $E = E' \times E''$ which dominates both given ones. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U098` | Theorem (5.4) | Chapter III, §5, source L10128 | (5.4) Theorem. The Poincaré homomorphism $$ K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K ^ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) $$ is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U099` | Proposition (5.5) | Chapter III, §5, source L10202 | (5.5) Proposition. Associating to a replete ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $K$ the metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module $L(\mathfrak{a})$ yields a homomorphism $$ P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) ^ {*}, \quad [ \mathfrak {a} ] \longmapsto [ L (\mathfrak {a}) ], $$ into the unit group of the ring $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U100` | Proposition (5.6) | Chapter III, §5, source L10218 | (5.6) Proposition. $K_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ is generated as an additive group by the elements [a]. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U101` | Proposition (5.7) | Chapter III, §5, source L10234 | (5.7) Proposition. For any two replete ideals $\mathfrak{a}$ and $\mathfrak{b}$ of $K$ we have in $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ the equation $$ ([ a ] - 1) ([ b ] - 1) = 0. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U102` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §6, source L10412 | The Grothendieck ring $K_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ is equipped with a canonical surjective homomorphism $$ \operatorname{rk}: K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {Z}. $$ Indeed, the rule which associates to every isometry class $\{M\}$ of projective metrized O-modules the rank $$ \operatorname{rk} \{M \} = \dim_ {K} (M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} K) $$ extends by linearity to a ring homomorphism $F_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) \to \mathbb{Z}$ . For a short exact sequence $0 \to M' \to M \to M'' \to 0$ of metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules one has $\operatorname{rk}(M) = \operatorname{rk}(M') + \operatorname{rk}(M'')$ , and so $\operatorname{rk}(\{M'\} - \{M\} + \{M''\}) = 0$ . Thus $\operatorname{rk}$ is zero on the ideal $R_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ and induces therefore a homomorphism $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) \to \mathbb{Z}$ . It is called the augmentation of $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ and its kernel $I = \ker(\operatorname{rk})$ is called the augmentation ideal. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U103` | Proposition (6.1) | Chapter III, §6, source L10426 | (6.1) Proposition. The ideal I, resp. $I^{2}$ , is generated as an additive group by the elements $[a]-1$ , resp. $([a]-1)([b]-1)$ , where a, b vary over the replete ideals of K. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U104` | Corollary (6.2) | Chapter III, §6, source L10450 | (6.2) Corollary. $I^2 = 0$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U105` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §6, source L10452 | We now define $$ \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{{{\mathcal {O}}}}}) = \mathbb {Z} \oplus I $$ and turn this additive group into a ring by putting $xy = 0$ for $x, y \in I$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U106` | Definition (6.3) | Chapter III, §6, source L10460 | (6.3) Definition. The additive homomorphism $$ c _ {1}: K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \longrightarrow I, \quad c _ {1} (\xi) = \xi - \mathrm{rk} (\xi) $$ is called the first Chern class. The mapping $$ \operatorname{ch}: K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \to \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}), \quad \operatorname{ch} (\xi) = \operatorname{rk} (\xi) + c _ {1} (\xi), $$ is called the Chern character of $K_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U107` | Proposition (6.4) | Chapter III, §6, source L10474 | (6.4) Proposition. The Chern character $$ \operatorname{ch}: K _ {0} (\overline {{{{\mathcal {O}}}}}) \longrightarrow \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{{{\mathcal {O}}}}}) $$ is an isomorphism of rings. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U108` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §6, source L10496 | We obtain a complete and explicit description of the Chern character by taking into account another homomorphism, as well as the homomorphism $\operatorname{rk}: K_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}}) \to \mathbb{Z}$ , namely $$ \det: K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) $$ which is induced by taking determinants det M of projective o-modules M as follows (see §4). det M is an invertible metrized o-module, and therefore of the form $L(\mathfrak{a})$ for some replete ideal a, which is well determined up to isomorphism. Denoting by [det M] the class of a in $Pic(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , the linear extension of the map $\{M\}\mapsto[\det M]$ gives a homomorphism $$ \det: F _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}). $$ It maps the subgroup $R_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ to 1, because it is generated by the elements $\{M'\}-\{M\}+\{M''\}$ which arise from short exact sequences $$ 0 \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ of projective metrized o-modules and which, by (4.7), satisfy $$ \begin{array}{c} \det \{M \} = [ \det M ] = [ \det M ^ {\prime} \otimes \det M ^ {\prime \prime} ] \\ = [ \det M ^ {\prime} ] [ \det M ^ {\prime \prime} ] = \det \{M ^ {\prime} \} \det \{M ^ {\prime \prime} \}. \end{array} $$ Thus we get an induced homomorphism $\det: K_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) \to \text{Pic}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . It satisfies the following proposition. | `FC09-C03-U091` |
| `FC09-C03-U109` | Proposition (6.5) | Chapter III, §6, source L10522 | (6.5) Proposition. (i) The canonical homomorphism $$ P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) ^ {*} $$ is injective. (ii) The restriction of det to $I$ , $$ \det: I \longrightarrow P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}), $$ is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U110` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §6, source L10554 | From the isomorphism $\det: I \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Pic}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , we now obtain an isomorphism $$ \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \stackrel {\sim} {\longrightarrow} \mathbb {Z} \oplus P i c (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) $$ and the composite $$ K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \xrightarrow {\mathrm{ch}} \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \xrightarrow {\mathrm{id} \oplus \det} \mathbb {Z} \oplus P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) $$ will again be called the Chern character of $K_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ . Observing that $\det(c_{1}(\xi)) = \det(\xi - \mathrm{rk}(\xi) \cdot 1) = \det(\xi)$ , this yields the explicit description of the Grothendieck group $K_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})$ : | — |
| `FC09-C03-U111` | Theorem (6.6) | Chapter III, §6, source L10568 | (6.6) Theorem. The Chern character gives an isomorphism $$ \operatorname{ch}: K _ {0} (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbb {Z} \oplus P i c (\bar {\mathcal {O}}), \quad \operatorname{ch} (\xi) = \operatorname{rk} (\xi) \oplus \det (\xi). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U112` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §7, source L10578 | We now consider a finite extension $L\|K$ of algebraic number fields and study the relations between the Grothendieck groups of $L$ and $K$ . Let $\mathcal{O}$ , resp. $\mathcal{O}$ , be the ring of integers of $K$ , resp. $L$ , and write $X(\mathbb{C}) = \operatorname{Hom}(K, \mathbb{C})$ , $Y(\mathbb{C}) = \operatorname{Hom}(L, \mathbb{C})$ . The inclusion $i: \mathcal{O} \to \mathcal{O}$ and the surjection $Y(\mathbb{C}) \to X(\mathbb{C})$ , $\sigma \mapsto \sigma\|_K$ , give two canonical homomorphisms $$ i ^ {*}: K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \quad \text { and } \quad i _ {*}: K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}), $$ defined as follows. If $M$ is a projective metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module, then $M \otimes_{\mathcal{O}} \mathcal{O}$ is a projective $\mathcal{O}$ -module. As $$ (M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O}) _ {\mathbb {C}} = M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} \otimes_ {\mathbb {Z}} \mathbb {C} = M _ {\mathbb {C}} \otimes_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} L _ {\mathbb {C}}, $$ the hermitian metric on the $K_{C}$ -module $M_{C}$ extends canonically to an F-invariant metric of the $L_{C}$ -module $(M \otimes_{\circ} O)_{\mathbb{C}}$ . Therefore $M \otimes_{\circ} O$ is automatically a metrized O-module, which we denote by $i^{*}M$ . If $$ 0 \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ is a short exact sequence of projective metrized O-modules, then $$ 0 \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime} \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} \longrightarrow M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} \longrightarrow M ^ {\prime \prime} \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} \longrightarrow 0 $$ is a short exact sequence of metrized O-modules, because O is a projective O-module and the metrics in the sequence $$ 0 \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ simply extend $L_{C}$ -sesquilinearly to metrics in the sequence of $L_{C}$ -modules $$ 0 \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime} \otimes_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} L _ {\mathbb {C}} \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} \otimes_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} L _ {\mathbb {C}} \longrightarrow M _ {\mathbb {C}} ^ {\prime \prime} \otimes_ {K _ {\mathbb {C}}} L _ {\mathbb {C}} \longrightarrow 0. $$ This is why mapping, in the usual way (i.e., via the representation $K_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})=F_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})/R_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ ), $$ M \longmapsto [ i ^ {*} M ] = [ M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} \mathcal {O} ] $$ gives a well-defined homomorphism $$ i ^ {*}: K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}). $$ The reader may verify for himself that this is in fact a ring homomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U113` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §7, source L10630 | On the other hand, if M is a projective metrized O-module, then M is automatically also a projective O-module. For the complexification $M_{C} = M \otimes_{Z} C$ we have the decomposition $$ M _ {\mathbb {C}} = \bigoplus_ {\tau \in Y (\mathbb {C})} M _ {\tau} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} \bigoplus_ {\tau \| \sigma} M _ {\tau} = \bigoplus_ {\sigma \in X (\mathbb {C})} M _ {\sigma}, $$ where $M_{\tau} = M\otimes_{\mathcal{O},\tau}\mathbb{C}$ and $$ M _ {\sigma} = M \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}, \sigma} \mathbb {C} = \bigoplus_ {\tau \| \sigma} M _ {\tau}. $$ The C-vector spaces $M_{\tau}$ carry hermitian metrics $\langle, \rangle_{M_{\tau}}$ , and we define the metric $\langle, \rangle_{M_{\sigma}}$ on the C-vector space $M_{\sigma}$ to be the orthogonal sum $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {M _ {\sigma}} = \sum_ {\tau \| \sigma} \langle x _ {\tau}, y _ {\tau} \rangle_ {M _ {\tau}}. $$ This gives a hermitian metric on the $K_{C}$ -module $M_{C}$ , whose F-invariance is clearly guaranteed by the F-invariance of the original metric $\langle,\rangle_{M}$ . We denote the metrized O-module M thus constructed by $i_{*}M$ . If $0 \rightarrow M' \rightarrow M \rightarrow M'' \rightarrow 0$ is a short exact sequence of projective metrized O-modules, then $$ 0 \longrightarrow i _ {*} M ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow i _ {*} M \longrightarrow i _ {*} M ^ {\prime \prime} \longrightarrow 0 $$ is clearly an exact sequence of projective metrized O-modules. As before, this is why the correspondence $$ M \longmapsto [ i _ {*} M ] $$ gives us a well-defined (additive) homomorphism $$ i _ {*}: K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U114` | Proposition (7.1) | Chapter III, §7, source L10668 | (7.1) Proposition (Projection Formula). The diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c c c} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \times & K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \longrightarrow & K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \\ i _ {*} \Big \downarrow & & \Big \uparrow i ^ {*} & & \Big \downarrow i _ {*} \\ K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \times & K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \longrightarrow & K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \end{array} $$ is commutative, where the horizontal arrows are multiplication. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U115` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §7, source L10702 | The Riemann-Roch problem in Grothendieck's perspective is the task of computing the Chern character $\operatorname{ch}(i_*M)$ for a projective metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module $M$ in terms of $\operatorname{ch}(M)$ . By (6.6), this amounts to computing $\det(i_*M)$ in terms of $\det M$ . But $\det M$ is an invertible metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module and is therefore isometric by (4.5) to the metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module $L(\mathfrak{A})$ of a replete ideal $\mathfrak{A}$ of $L$ . $N_{L\|K}(\mathfrak{A})$ is then a replete ideal of $K$ , and we put $$ N _ {L \| K} (\det M) := L \left(N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {A})\right). $$ This is an invertible metrized O-module which is well determined by M up to isometry. With this notation we first establish the following theorem. | `FC09-C03-U111`, `FC09-C03-U088` |
| `FC09-C03-U116` | Theorem (7.2) | Chapter III, §7, source L10710 | (7.2) Theorem. For any projective metrized O-module M one has: $$ \operatorname{rk} (i _ {*} M) = \operatorname{rk} (M) \operatorname{rk} (\mathcal {O}), $$ $$ \det (i _ {*} M) \cong N _ {L \| K} (\det M) \otimes_ {\mathcal {O}} (\det i _ {*} \mathcal {O}) ^ {\operatorname{rk} (M)}. $$ Here we have $\operatorname{rk}(\mathcal{O}) = [L:K]$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U117` | Corollary (7.3) | Chapter III, §7, source L10818 | (7.3) Corollary. For every class $\xi \in K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , one has the formulas $$ \operatorname{rk} (i _ {*} \xi) = [ L: K ] \operatorname{rk} (\xi), $$ $$ \det (i _ {*} \xi) = [ \det i _ {*} \mathcal {O} ] ^ {\mathrm{rk} (\xi)} N _ {L \| K} (\det \xi). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U118` | Proposition (7.4) | Chapter III, §7, source L10830 | (7.4) Proposition. There is a canonical isomorphism $$ (\det i _ {*} \mathcal {O}) ^ {\otimes 2} \cong \mathfrak {d} _ {L \| K} $$ of metrized o-modules. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U119` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §7, source L10926 | We now set out to rewrite the results obtained in (7.2) and (7.4) in the language of GROTHENDIECK's general formalism. For the homomorphism $i_{*}$ there is the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{rk}} & \mathbb {Z} \\ i _ {*} \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow_ {[ L: K ]} \\ K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{rk}} & \mathbb {Z}, \end{array} $$ because $[L:K]$ times the rank of an O-module M is its rank as O-module. Therefore $i_{*}$ induces a homomorphism $$ i _ {*}: I (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow I (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) $$ between the kernels of both rank homomorphisms, so that there is a homomorphism $$ i _ {*}: \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \longrightarrow \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}). $$ It is called the Gysin map. (7.3) immediately gives the following explicit description of it. | `FC09-C03-U116`, `FC09-C03-U118`, `FC09-C03-U117` |
| `FC09-C03-U120` | Corollary (7.5) | Chapter III, §7, source L10946 | (7.5) Corollary. The diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow [ \sim ]{\operatorname{id} \oplus \det} & \mathbb {Z} \oplus P i c (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \\ i _ {*} \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow_ {[ L: K ] \oplus N _ {L \| K}} \\ \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow [ \sim ]{\operatorname{id} \oplus \det} & \mathbb {Z} \oplus P i c (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \end{array} $$ is commutative. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U121` | Definition (7.6) | Chapter III, §7, source L10970 | (7.6) Definition. The Todd class of $O\|O$ is defined to be the element $$ \operatorname{Td} (\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}) = 1 - \frac {1}{2} c _ {1} \left(\left[ \Omega_ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} ^ {1} \right]\right) = 1 - \frac {1}{2} \left[ \Omega_ {\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}} ^ {1} \right] \in \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \otimes \mathbb {Z} \left[ \frac {1}{2} \right]. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U122` | Theorem (7.7) | Chapter III, §7, source L10990 | (7.7) Theorem (Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch). The diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow {\operatorname{Td} (\mathcal {O} \| \mathcal {O}) \mathrm{ch}} & \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \\ i _ {*} \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow i _ {*} \\ K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{ch}} & \operatorname{gr} K _ {0} (\overline {{\mathcal {O}}}) \end{array} $$ is commutative. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U123` | Proposition (8.1) | Chapter III, §8, source L11058 | (8.1) Proposition. The degree map $$ \deg_ {K}: P i c (\bar {\mathcal {O}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R}, \quad \deg_ {K} ([ \mathfrak {a} ]) = - \log \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}), $$ extends uniquely to a homomorphism $$ \chi_ {K}: K _ {0} (\overline {{{{\mathcal {O}}}}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R} $$ on $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , and thereby on $K^0 (\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . It is given by $$ \chi_ {K} = \deg \circ \det $$ and called the Euler-Minkowski characteristic over $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U124` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §8, source L11086 | Via the Poincaré isomorphism $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) \xrightarrow{\sim} K^0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ , we transfer the maps det and $\chi_K$ to the Grothendieck group $K^0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ of coherent metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -modules. Then proposition (8.1) is equally valid for $K^0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ as for $K_0(\overline{\mathcal{O}})$ . We define in what follows $\chi_K(M) = \chi_K([M])$ for a metrized $\mathcal{O}$ -module $M$ . If $L\|K$ is an extension of algebraic number fields and $i: \mathcal{O} \to \mathcal{O}$ the inclusion of the maximal orders of $K$ , resp. $L$ , then applying $\deg_K$ to the formula (7.2) and using $$ \deg_ {L} (\mathfrak {A}) = - \log \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {A}) = - \log \mathfrak {N} \left(N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {A})\right) = \deg_ {K} \left(N _ {L \| K} (\mathfrak {A})\right) $$ | `FC09-C03-U123`, `FC09-C03-U116` |
| `FC09-C03-U125` | Theorem (8.2) | Chapter III, §8, source L11094 | (8.2) Theorem. For every coherent $\mathcal{O}$ -module $M$ , the Riemann-Roch formula $$ \chi_ {K} (i _ {*} M) = \deg_ {L} (\det M) + \operatorname{rk} (M) \chi_ {K} (i _ {*} \mathcal {O}) $$ is valid, and in particular, for an invertible metrized O-module M, we have $$ \chi_ {K} (i _ {*} M) = \deg_ {L} (M) + \chi_ {K} (i _ {*} \mathcal {O}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C03-U126` | Convention / notation | Chapter III, §8, source L11126 | It will be denoted by $\operatorname{vol}(M)$ for short. It does not depend on the choice of Z-basis $\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}$ because a different choice is linked to the original one by a matrix with integer coefficients which also has an inverse with integer coefficients, hence has determinant of absolute value 1. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U127` | Definition / construction | Chapter III, §8, source L11134 | In the present case, where the base field is Q, the degree map $$ \deg : P i c (\overline {{\mathbb {Z}}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R} $$ is an isomorphism (see § 1, exercise 3), and we call the unique homomorphism arising from this, $$ \chi = \deg \circ \det: K ^ {0} (\overline {{\mathbb {Z}}}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {R}, $$ the Euler-Minkowski characteristic. It is computed explicitly as follows. | — |
| `FC09-C03-U128` | Proposition (8.3) | Chapter III, §8, source L11148 | (8.3) Proposition. For a coherent metrized $\mathbb{Z}$ -module $M$ , one has $$ \chi (M) = \log \# M _ {\mathrm{tor}} - \log \operatorname{vol} (M / M _ {\mathrm{tor}}). $$ In this formula $M_{tor}$ denotes the torsion subgroup of M and $M/M_{tor}$ the projective metrized Z-module which receives its metric from M via $M \otimes R = M/M_{tor} \otimes R$ . | — |
| `FC09-C03-U129` | Proposition (8.4) | Chapter III, §8, source L11206 | (8.4) Proposition. $\chi(a) = \chi(i_*L(a)).$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U001` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §1, source L11264 | Every field k is equipped with a distinguished Galois extension: the separable closure $\bar{k}\|k$ . Its Galois group $G_{k}=G(\bar{k}\|k)$ is called the absolute Galois group of k. As a rule, this extension will have infinite degree. It does, however, have the advantage of collecting all finite Galois extensions of k. This is why it is reasonable to try to give it a prominent place in Galois theory. But such an attempt faces the difficulty that the main theorem of Galois theory does not remain true for infinite extensions. Let us explain this in the following | — |
| `FC09-C04-U002` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §1, source L11266 | Example: The absolute Galois group $G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}} = G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p} \| \mathbb{F}_{p})$ of the field $F_{p}$ with p elements contains the Frobenius automorphism $\varphi$ which is given by $$ x ^ {\varphi} = x ^ {p} \quad \text { for all } \quad x \in \overline {{\mathbb {F}}} _ {p}. $$ The subgroup $(\varphi)=\{\varphi^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}$ has the same fixed field $F_{p}$ as the whole of $G_{F_{p}}$ . But contrary to what we are used to in finite Galois theory, we find $(\varphi)\neq G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}$ . In order to check this, let us construct an element $\psi\in G_{F_{p}}$ which does not belong to $(\varphi)$ . We choose a sequence $\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ of integers satisfying $$ a _ {n} \equiv a _ {m} \bmod m $$ whenever $m\|n$ , but such that there is no integer $a$ satisfying $a_{n} \equiv a \mod n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ . An example of such a sequence is given by $a_{n} = n'x_{n}$ , where we write $n = n'p^{v_{p}(n)}$ , $(n', p) = 1$ , and $1 = n'x_{n} + p^{v_{p}(n)}y_{n}$ . Now put $$ \psi_ {n} = \varphi^ {a _ {n}} \| _ {\mathbb {F} _ {p ^ {n}}} \in G (\mathbb {F} _ {p ^ {n}} \| \mathbb {F} _ {p}). $$ If $\mathbb{F}_{p^m} \subseteq \mathbb{F}_{p^n}$ , then $m\|n$ , so that $a_n \equiv a_m \mod m$ , and therefore $$ \psi_ {n} \| _ {\mathbb {F} _ {p ^ {m}}} = \varphi^ {a _ {n}} \| _ {\mathbb {F} _ {p ^ {m}}} = \varphi^ {a _ {m}} \| _ {\mathbb {F} _ {p ^ {m}}} = \psi_ {m}. $$ Observe that $\varphi\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p^m}}$ has order $m$ . Therefore the $\psi_n$ define an automorphism $\psi$ of $\overline{\mathbb{F}}_p = \bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty} \mathbb{F}_{p^n}$ . Now $\psi$ cannot belong to ( $\varphi$ ) because $\psi = \varphi^a$ , for $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ , would imply $\psi\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p^n}} = \varphi^{a_n}\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p^n}} = \varphi^a\|_{\mathbb{F}_{p^n}}$ and hence $a_n \equiv a \mod n$ for all $n$ , which is what we ruled out by construction. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U003` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §1, source L11292 | The example does not mean, however, that we have to chuck the main theorem of Galois theory altogether in the case of infinite extensions. We just have to amend it using the observation that the Galois group $G = G(\Omega\|k)$ of any Galois extension $\Omega\|k$ carries a canonical topology. This topology is called the Krull topology and is obtained as follows. For every $\sigma \in G$ we take the cosets $$ \sigma G (\Omega \| K) $$ as a basis of neighbourhoods of $\sigma$ , with K\|k ranging over finite Galois subextensions of $\Omega\|k$ . The multiplication and the inverse map $$ G \times G \longrightarrow G, (\sigma , \tau) \longmapsto \sigma \tau , \quad \text { and } \quad G \longrightarrow G, \sigma \longmapsto \sigma^ {- 1}, $$ are continuous maps, since the preimage of a fundamental open neighbourhood $\sigma\tau G(\Omega\|K)$ , resp. $\sigma^{-1}G(\Omega\|K)$ , contains the open neighbourhood $\sigma G(\Omega\|K)\times\tau G(\Omega\|K)$ , resp. $\sigma G(\Omega\|K)$ . Thus G is a topological group which satisfies the following | — |
| `FC09-C04-U004` | Proposition (1.1) | Chapter IV, §1, source L11306 | (1.1) Proposition. For every (finite or infinite) Galois extension $\Omega\|k$ the Galois group $G = G(\Omega\|k)$ is compact Hausdorff with respect to the Krull topology. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U005` | Theorem (1.2) | Chapter IV, §1, source L11332 | (1.2) Theorem. Let $\Omega\|k$ be a (finite or infinite) Galois extension. Then the assignment $$ K \mapsto G (\Omega \| K) $$ is a 1–1-correspondence between the subextensions $K\|k$ of $\Omega\|k$ and the closed subgroups of $G(\Omega\|k)$ . The open subgroups of $G(\Omega\|k)$ correspond precisely to the finite subextensions of $\Omega\|k$ . | — |
