| `FC09-C04-U006` | Definition (1.3) | Chapter IV, §1, source L11360 | (1.3) Definition. A profinite group is a topological group G which is Hausdorff and compact, and which admits a basis of neighbourhoods of $1 \in G$ consisting of normal subgroups. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U007` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §2, source L11400 | Writing $i \leq j$ if $X_{j} \subseteq X_{i}$ (resp. $X_{i} \subseteq X_{j}$ ) makes the indexing set I into a directed system, i.e., an ordered set in which, for every pair i, j, there exists a k such that $i \leq k$ and $j \leq k$ . In the case at hand, such a k is given by $X_{k} = X_{i} \cap X_{j}$ (resp. $X_{k} = X_{i} \cup X_{j}$ ). For $i \leq j$ we denote the inclusion $X_{j} \hookrightarrow X_{i}$ (resp. $X_{i} \hookrightarrow X_{j}$ ) by $f_{ij}$ and obtain a system $\{X_{i}, f_{ij}\}$ of sets and maps. The operations of intersection and union are now generalized by replacing the inclusions $f_{ij}$ with arbitrary maps. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U008` | Definition (2.1) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11402 | (2.1) Definition. Let $I$ be a directed system. A projective, resp. inductive system over $I$ is a family $\{X_i, f_{ij} \mid i, j \in I, i \leq j\}$ of topological spaces $X_i$ and continuous maps $$ f _ {i j}: X _ {j} \longrightarrow X _ {i}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad f _ {i j}: X _ {i} \longrightarrow X _ {j}, $$ such that one has $f_{ii} = \mathrm{id}_{X_i}$ and $$ f _ {i k} = f _ {i j} \circ f _ {j k}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad f _ {i k} = f _ {j k} \circ f _ {i j}, $$ when $i \leq j \leq k$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U009` | Definition (2.2) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11418 | (2.2) Definition. The projective limit $$ X = \varprojlim_ {i \in I} X _ {i} $$ of the projective system $\{X_i, f_{ij}\}$ is defined to be the subset $$ X = \left\{(x _ {i}) _ {i \in I} \in \prod_ {i \in I} X _ {i} \mid f _ {i j} (x _ {j}) = x _ {i} \quad \text { for } \quad i \leq j \right\} $$ of the product $\prod_{i\in I}X_i$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U010` | Proposition (2.3) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11440 | (2.3) Proposition. The projective limit $X = \varprojlim_{i} X_i$ of nonempty compact spaces $X_i$ is itself nonempty and compact. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U011` | Definition (2.4) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11444 | (2.4) Definition. The inductive limit $$ X = \varinjlim_ {i \in I} X _ {i} $$ of an inductive system $\{X_i, f_{ij}\}$ is defined to be the quotient $$ X = \left(\coprod_ {i \in I} X _ {i}\right) / \sim $$ of the disjoint union $\coprod_{i\in I}X_i$ , where we consider two elements $x_{i}\in X_{i}$ and $x_{j}\in X_{j}$ equivalent if there exists a $k\geq i,j$ such that $$ f _ {i k} (x _ {i}) = f _ {j k} (x _ {j}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U012` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §2, source L11462 | In the applications, the projective and inductive systems $\{X_{i}, f_{ij}\}$ that occur will not just be systems of topological spaces and continuous maps, but the $X_{i}$ will usually be topological groups, rings or modules, etc., and the $f_{ij}$ will be continuous homomorphisms. In what follows, we will deal explicitly only with projective and inductive systems $\{G_{i}, g_{ij}\}$ of topological groups. But since everything works exactly the same way for systems of rings or modules, these cases may be thought of tacitly as being treated as well. Let $\{G_i, g_{ij}\}$ be a projective, resp. inductive system of topological groups. Then the projective, resp. inductive limit $$ G = \varprojlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad G = \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} $$ is a topological group as well. The multiplication in the projective limit is induced by the componentwise multiplication in the product $\prod_{i\in I}G_{i}$ . In the case of the inductive limit, given two equivalence classes $x,y\in G=\varliminf_{i\in I}G_{i}$ , one has to choose representatives $x_{k}$ and $y_{k}$ in the same $G_{k}$ in order to define $$ x y = \text { equivalence class of } x _ {k} y _ {k}. $$ We leave it to the reader to check that this definition is independent of the choice of representatives, and that the operation thus defined makes G into a group. The projections $p_{i} : \prod_{i \in I} G_{i} \to G_{i}$ , resp. the inclusions $\iota_{i} : G_{i} \to \coprod_{i \in I} G_{i}$ , induce a family of continuous homomorphisms $$ g _ {i}: G \longrightarrow G _ {i}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad g _ {i}: G _ {i} \longrightarrow G $$ such that $g_{i} = g_{ij} \circ g_{j}$ , resp. $g_{i} = g_{j} \circ g_{ij}$ , for $i \leq j$ . This family has the following universal property. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U013` | Proposition (2.5) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11488 | (2.5) Proposition. If $H$ is a topological group and $$ h _ {i}: H \longrightarrow G _ {i}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad h _ {i}: G _ {i} \longrightarrow H $$ is a family of continuous homomorphisms such that $$ h _ {i} = g _ {i j} \circ h _ {j}, \quad r e s p. \quad h _ {i} = h _ {j} \circ g _ {i j} $$ for $i \leq j$ , then there exists a unique continuous homomorphism $$ h: H \longrightarrow G = \varprojlim_ {i} G _ {i}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad h: G = \varinjlim_ {i} G _ {i} \longrightarrow H $$ satisfying $h_i = g_i \circ h$ , resp. $h_i = h \circ g_i$ , for all $i \in I$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U014` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §2, source L11508 | The easy proof is left to the reader. A morphism between two projective, resp. inductive systems $\{G_{i}, g_{ij}\}$ and $\{G_{i}^{\prime}, g_{ij}^{\prime}\}$ of topological groups is a family of continuous homomorphisms $f_{i}: G_{i} \to G_{i}^{\prime}, i \in I$ , such that the diagrams $$ \begin{array}{c c} G _ {j} \xrightarrow {f _ {j}} G _ {j} ^ {\prime} & \\ g _ {i j} \Biggl \downarrow & \Biggl \downarrow g _ {i j} ^ {\prime}, \\ G _ {i} \xrightarrow {f _ {i}} G _ {i} ^ {\prime} & \text {resp.} \end{array} \qquad \begin{array}{c c} G _ {j} \xrightarrow {f _ {j}} G _ {j} ^ {\prime} \\ g _ {i j} \Biggl \uparrow & \Biggl \uparrow g _ {i j} ^ {\prime} \\ G _ {i} \xrightarrow {f _ {i}} G _ {i} ^ {\prime} & \end{array} $$ commute for $i \leq j$ . Such a family $(f_i)_{i \in I}$ defines a mapping $$ f: \prod_ {i \in I} G _ {i} \longrightarrow \prod_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad f: \coprod_ {i \in I} G _ {i} \longrightarrow \coprod_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime}, $$ which induces a homomorphism $$ f: \varprojlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} \longrightarrow \varprojlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad f: \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} \longrightarrow \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime}. $$ In this way $\varprojlim$ , resp. $\varinjlim$ , becomes a functor. A particularly important property of this functor is its so-called “exactness”. For the inductive limit $\varinjlim$ , exactness holds without restrictions. In other words, one has the | — |
| `FC09-C04-U015` | Proposition (2.6) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11528 | (2.6) Proposition. Let $\alpha : \{G_i', g_{ij}'\} \to \{G_i, g_{ij}\}$ and $\beta : \{G_i, g_{ij}\} \to \{G_i'', g_{ij}''\}$ be morphisms between inductive systems of topological groups such that the sequence $$ G _ {i} ^ {\prime} \xrightarrow {\alpha_ {i}} G _ {i} \xrightarrow {\beta_ {i}} G _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} $$ is exact for every $i \in I$ . Then the induced sequence $$ \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime} \stackrel {{\alpha}} {{\longrightarrow}} \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} \stackrel {{\beta}} {{\longrightarrow}} \varinjlim_ {i \in I} G _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} $$ is also exact. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U016` | Proposition (2.7) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11558 | (2.7) Proposition. Let $\alpha : \{G_i', g_{ij}'\} \to \{G_i, g_{ij}\}$ and $\beta : \{G_i, g_{ij}\} \to \{G_i'', g_{ij}''\}$ be morphisms between projective systems of compact topological groups such that the sequence $$ G _ {i} ^ {\prime} \xrightarrow {\alpha_ {i}} G _ {i} \xrightarrow {\beta_ {i}} G _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} $$ is exact for every $i \in I$ . Then $$ \varprojlim_ {i} G _ {i} ^ {\prime} \stackrel {{\alpha}} {{\longrightarrow}} \varprojlim_ {i} G _ {i} \stackrel {{\beta}} {{\longrightarrow}} \varprojlim_ {i} G _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} $$ is again an exact sequence of compact topological groups. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U017` | Proposition (2.8) | Chapter IV, §2, source L11576 | (2.8) Proposition. If $G$ is a profinite group, and if $N$ varies over the open normal subgroups of $G$ , then one has, algebraically as well as topologically, that $$ G \cong \varprojlim_ {N} G / N. $$ If conversely $\{G_i, g_{ij}\}$ is a projective system of finite (or even profinite) groups, then $$ G = \varprojlim_ {i} G _ {i} $$ is a profinite group. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U018` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11614 | Example 1: The Galois group $G = G(\Omega \| k)$ of a Galois extension $\Omega \| k$ is a profinite group with respect to the Krull topology. This was already stated in §1. If $K \| k$ varies over the finite Galois subextensions of $\Omega \| k$ , then, by definition of the Krull topology, $G(\Omega \| K)$ varies over the open normal subgroups of $G$ . In view of the identity $G(K \| k) = G(\Omega \| k) / G(\Omega \| K)$ and of (2.8), we therefore obtain the Galois group $G(\Omega \| k)$ as the projective limit $$ G (\Omega \| k) \cong \varprojlim G (K \| k) $$ of the finite Galois groups $G(K\|k)$ . | `FC09-C04-U017` |
| `FC09-C04-U019` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11622 | Example 2: If $p$ is a prime number, then the rings $\mathbb{Z} / p^n\mathbb{Z}$ , $n \in \mathbb{N}$ , form a projective system with respect to the projections $\mathbb{Z} / p^n\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z} / p^m\mathbb{Z}$ , for $n \geq m$ . The projective limit $$ \mathbb {Z} _ {p} = \varprojlim_ {n} \mathbb {Z} / p ^ {n} \mathbb {Z} $$ is the ring of $p$ -adic integers (see chap. II, §1). | — |
| `FC09-C04-U020` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11630 | Example 3: Let $\mathcal{O}$ be the valuation ring in a $\mathfrak{p}$ -adic number field $K$ and $\mathfrak{p}$ its maximal ideal. The ideals $\mathfrak{p}^n$ , $n \in \mathbb{N}$ , make up a basis of neighbourhoods of the zero element 0 in $\mathcal{O}$ . $\mathcal{O}$ is Hausdorff and compact, and so is a profinite ring. The rings $\mathcal{O} / \mathfrak{p}^n$ , $n \in \mathbb{N}$ , are finite and we have a topological isomorphism $$ \mathcal {O} \cong \varprojlim_ {n} \mathcal {O} / \mathfrak {p} ^ {n}, \quad a \longmapsto \prod_ {n \in \mathbb {N}} (a \bmod \mathfrak {p} ^ {n}). $$ The group of units $U = \mathcal{O}^*$ is closed in $\mathcal{O}$ , hence Hausdorff and compact, and the subgroups $U^{(n)} = 1 + \mathfrak{p}^n$ form a basis of neighbourhoods of $1 \in U$ . Thus $$ U \cong \varprojlim_ {n} U / U ^ {(n)} $$ is also a profinite group. In fact, we have seen all this already in chap. II, §4. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U021` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11644 | Example 4: The rings $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ , $n \in \mathbb{N}$ , form a projective system with respect to the projections $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}$ , $n\|m$ , where the ordering on $\mathbb{N}$ is now given by divisibility, $n\|m$ . The projective limit $$ \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} = \varprojlim_ {n} \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z} $$ was originally called the Prüfer ring, whereas nowadays it has become customary to refer to it by the somewhat curt abbreviation “zed-hat” (or “zee-hat”). This ring is going to occupy quite an important position in what follows. It contains Z as a dense subring. The groups $n\widehat{Z}, n \in N$ , are precisely the open subgroups of $\widehat{Z}$ , and it is easy to verify that $$ \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} / n \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} \cong \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z}. $$ Taking, for each natural number n, the prime factorization $n = \prod_{p} p^{\nu_{p}}$ , the Chinese remainder theorem implies the decomposition $$ \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z} \cong \prod_ {p} \mathbb {Z} / p ^ {\nu_ {p}} \mathbb {Z}, $$ and passing to the projective limit, $$ \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} \cong \prod_ {p} \mathbb {Z} _ {p}. $$ This takes the natural embedding of $\mathbb{Z}$ into $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ to the diagonal embedding $\mathbb{Z} \to \prod_p \mathbb{Z}_p$ , $a \mapsto (a, a, a, \ldots)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U022` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11670 | Example 5: For the field $F_{q}$ with q elements, we get isomorphisms $$ G (\mathbb {F} _ {q ^ {n}} \| \mathbb {F} _ {q}) \cong \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z}, $$ one for every $n \in N$ , by mapping the Frobenius automorphism $\varphi_{n}$ to 1 mod $nZ$ . Passing to the projective limit gives an isomorphism $$ G (\overline {{{\mathbb {F}}}} _ {q} \| \mathbb {F} _ {q}) \cong \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ which sends the Frobenius automorphism $\varphi \in G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_q\|\mathbb{F}_q)$ to $1\in \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ , and the subgroup $(\varphi) = \{\varphi^n \|n\in \mathbb{Z}\}$ onto the dense (but not closed) subgroup $\mathbb{Z}$ of $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ . Given this, it is now clear, in the example at the beginning of this chapter, how we were able to construct an element $\psi \in G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_q\|\mathbb{F}_q)$ which did not belong to $(\varphi)$ . In fact, looking at it via the isomorphism $G(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_q\|\mathbb{F}_q)\cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ , what we did amounted to writing down the element $$ (\ldots , 0, 0, 1 _ {p}, 0, 0, \ldots) \in \prod_ {\ell} \mathbb {Z} _ {\ell} = \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}, $$ which does not belong to $\mathbb{Z}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U023` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11690 | Example 6: Let $\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q}$ be the extension obtained by adjoining all roots of unity. Its Galois group $G(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q})$ is then canonically isomorphic (as a topological group) to the group of units $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^* \cong \prod_p \mathbb{Z}_p^*$ of $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ , $$ G (\widetilde {\mathbb {Q}} \| \mathbb {Q}) \cong \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} ^ {*}. $$ This isomorphism is obtained by passing to the projective limit from the canonical isomorphisms $$ G (\mathbb {Q} (\mu_ {n}) \| \mathbb {Q}) \cong (\mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z}) ^ {*}, $$ where $\mu_{n}$ denotes the group of n-th roots of unity. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U024` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11704 | Example 7: The groups $\mathbb{Z}_p$ and $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ are (additive) special cases of the class of procyclic groups. These are profinite groups $G$ which are topologically generated by a single element $\sigma$ ; i.e., $G$ is the closure $(\overline{\sigma})$ of the subgroup $(\sigma) = \{\sigma^n \mid n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ . The open subgroups of a procyclic group $G = (\overline{\sigma})$ are all of the form $G^n$ . Indeed, $G^n$ is closed, being the image of the continuous map $G \to G$ , $x \mapsto x^n$ , and the quotient group $G / G^n$ is finite, because it contains the finite group $\{\sigma^\nu \bmod G^n \mid 0 \leq \nu < n\}$ as a dense subgroup, and is therefore equal to it. Conversely, if $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ of index $n$ , then $G^n \subseteq H \subseteq G$ and $n = (G:H) \leq (G:G^n) \leq n$ , so that $H = G^n$ . Every procyclic group $G$ is a quotient of the group $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ . In fact, if $G = (\overline{\sigma})$ , then we have for every $n$ the surjective homomorphism $$ \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z} \rightarrow G / G ^ {n}, \quad 1 \bmod n \mathbb {Z} \mapsto \sigma \bmod G ^ {n}, $$ and in view of (2.7), passing to the projective limit yields a continuous surjection $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\to G$ . | `FC09-C04-U016` |
| `FC09-C04-U025` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11714 | Example 8: Let A be an abelian torsion group. Then the Pontryagin dual $$ \chi (A) = \operatorname{Hom} (A, \mathbb {Q} / \mathbb {Z}) $$ is a profinite group. For one has $$ A = \bigcup_ {i} A _ {i}, $$ where $A_{i}$ varies over the finite subgroups of A, and thus $$ \chi (A) = \varprojlim_ {i} \chi (A _ {i}) $$ with finite groups $\chi(A_i)$ . If for instance, $$ A = \mathbb {Q} / \mathbb {Z} = \bigcup_ {n \in \mathbb {N}} \frac {1}{n} \mathbb {Z} / \mathbb {Z}, $$ then $\chi (\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z} / \mathbb{Z}) = \mathbb{Z} / n\mathbb{Z}$ , so that $$ \chi (\mathbb {Q} / \mathbb {Z}) \cong \varprojlim_ {n} \mathbb {Z} / n \mathbb {Z} = \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U026` | Example / comparison | Chapter IV, §2, source L11744 | Example 9: If $G$ is any group and $N$ varies over all normal subgroups of finite index, then the profinite group $$ \widehat {G} = \varprojlim_ {N} G / N $$ is called the profinite completion of $G$ . The profinite completion of $\mathbb{Z}$ , for example, is the group $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}} = \varprojlim_{n} \mathbb{Z} / n\mathbb{Z}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U027` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §3, source L11796 | We denote the closed subgroups of G by $G_{K}$ , and call these indices K “fields”; K will be called the fixed field of $G_{K}$ . The field k such that $G_{k} = G$ is called the base field, and $\bar{k}$ denotes the field satisfying $G_{\bar{k}} = \{1\}$ . The field belonging to the closure $(\overline{\sigma})$ of the cyclic group $(\sigma) = \{\sigma^{k} \mid k \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ generated by an element $\sigma \in G$ is simply called the fixed field of $\sigma$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U028` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L11798 | We write formally $K \subseteq L$ or $L\|K$ if $G_{L} \subseteq G_{K}$ , and we call the pair $L\|K$ a field extension. $L\|K$ is called a finite extension, if $G_{L}$ is open, i.e., of finite index in $G_{K}$ , and this index $$ [ L: K ] := (G _ {K}: G _ {L}) $$ will be called the degree of $L\|K$ . $L\|K$ is said to be normal or Galois if $G_{L}$ is a normal subgroup of $G_{K}$ . If this is the case, we define the Galois group of $L\|K$ by $$ G (L \| K) = G _ {K} / G _ {L}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U029` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L11810 | If $N \supseteq L \supseteq K$ are Galois extensions of $K$ , we define the restriction of an element $\sigma \in G(N\|K)$ to $L$ by $$ \sigma \mid_ {L} = \sigma \bmod G (N \| L) \in G (L \| K). $$ This gives a homomorphism $$ G (N \| K) \longrightarrow G (L \| K), \quad \sigma \longmapsto \sigma \| _ {L}, $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U030` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §3, source L11822 | The extension L\|K is called cyclic, abelian, solvable, etc., if the Galois group $G(L\|K)$ has these properties. We put $$ K = \bigcap_ {i} K _ {i} \quad \left(" \text { intersection }"\right) $$ if $G_{K}$ is topologically generated by the subgroups $G_{K_i}$ , and $$ K = \prod_ {i} K _ {i} \quad \left(" \text { composite }"\right) $$ if $G_K = \bigcap_i G_{K_i}$ . If $G_{K'} = \sigma^{-1}G_K\sigma$ for $\sigma \in G$ , we write $K' = K^\sigma$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U031` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L11862 | For every extension $L\|K$ we have $A_K \subseteq A_L$ , and if $L\|K$ is finite, then we have the norm map $$ N _ {L \| K}: A _ {L} \longrightarrow A _ {K}, \quad N _ {L \| K} (a) = \prod_ {\sigma} a ^ {\sigma}, $$ where $\sigma$ varies over a system of representatives of $G_{L} \backslash G_{K}$ . If $L\|K$ is Galois, then $A_{L}$ is a $G(L\|K)$ -module and one has $$ A _ {L} ^ {G (L \| K)} = A _ {K}. $$ At the center of class field theory there is the norm residue group $$ H ^ {0} \big (G (L \| K), A _ {L} \big) = A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L}. $$ We also consider the group $$ H ^ {- 1} \big (G (L \| K), A _ {L} \big) = _ {N _ {L \| K}} A _ {L} / I _ {G (L \| K)} A _ {L}, $$ where $$ { } _ { N _ { L \| K } } A _ { L } = \left\{ a \in A _ { L } \mid N _ { L \| K } ( a ) = 1 \right\} $$ is the “norm-one group” and $I_{G(L\|K)}A_{L}$ is the subgroup of $N_{L\|K}A_{L}$ which is generated by all elements $$ a ^ {\sigma - 1} := a ^ {\sigma} a ^ {- 1}, $$ with $a \in A_L$ , and $\sigma \in G(L\|K)$ . If $G(L\|K)$ is cyclic and $\sigma$ is a generator, then $I_{G(L\|K)}A_L$ is simply the group $$ A _ {L} ^ {\sigma - 1} = \left\{a ^ {\sigma - 1} \mid a \in A _ {L} \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U032` | Axiom (3.1) | Chapter IV, §3, source L11908 | (3.1) Axiom. One has $H^{-1}(G(L\|K), A_{L}) = 1$ for all finite cyclic extensions L\|K. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U033` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L11910 | The theory we are about to develop makes reference to a surjective $G$ -homomorphism $$ \wp : A \longrightarrow A, \quad a \longmapsto a ^ {\wp}, $$ with finite cyclic kernel $\mu_{\wp}$ . The order $n = \# \mu_{\wp}$ is called the exponent of the operator $\wp$ . The case of prime interest to us is when $\wp$ is the n-th power map $a \mapsto a^{n}$ , and $\mu_{\wp} = \mu_{n} = \{\xi \in A \mid \xi^{n} = 1\}$ is the group of “n-th roots of unity” in A. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U034` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L11918 | We now fix a field $K$ such that $\mu_{\wp} \subseteq A_K$ . For every subset $B \subseteq A$ , let $K(B)$ denote the fixed field of the closed subgroup $$ H = \left\{\sigma \in G _ {K} \mid b ^ {\sigma} = b \text { for all } b \in B \right\} $$ of $G_{K}$ . If B is $G_{K}$ -invariant, then $K(B)\|K$ is obviously Galois. A Kummer extension (with respect to $\wp$ ) is by definition an extension of the form $$ K \left(\wp^ {- 1} (\Delta)\right) \| K, $$ where $\Delta\subseteq A_{K}$ . A Kummer extension $K(\wp^{-1}(\Delta))\|K$ is always Galois, and its Galois group is abelian of exponent n. Indeed, for an extension $K(\wp^{-1}(a))\|K$ , we have the injective homomorphism | — |
| `FC09-C04-U035` | Proposition (3.2) | Chapter IV, §3, source L11946 | (3.2) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is an abelian extension of exponent $n$ , then $$ L = K \left(\wp^ {- 1} (\Delta)\right) \quad \text { with } \quad \Delta = A _ {L} ^ {\wp} \cap A _ {K}. $$ If in particular, $L\|K$ is cyclic, then we find $L = K(\alpha)$ with $\alpha^{\wp} = a \in A_K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U036` | Theorem (3.3) | Chapter IV, §3, source L11958 | (3.3) Theorem. The correspondence $$ \Delta \longmapsto L = K \left(\wp^ {- 1} (\Delta)\right) $$ is a 1-1-correspondence between the groups $\Delta$ such that $A_K^\wp \subseteq \Delta \subseteq A_K$ and the abelian extensions $L\|K$ of exponent $n$ . If $\Delta$ and $L$ correspond to each other, then $A_L^\wp \cap A_K = \Delta$ , and we have a canonical isomorphism $$ \Delta / A _ {K} ^ {\wp} \cong \operatorname{Hom} \left(G (L \| K), \mu_ {\wp}\right), \quad a \bmod A _ {K} ^ {\wp} \longmapsto \chi_ {a}, $$ where the character $\chi_{a}: G(L\|K) \to \mu_{\wp^{-}}$ is given by $\chi_{a}(\sigma) = \alpha^{\sigma - 1}$ , for $\alpha \in \wp^{-1}(a)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U037` | Proposition (3.4) | Chapter IV, §3, source L12032 | (3.4) Proposition. For every cyclic finite field extension $L\|K$ , one has $H^{-1}(G(L\|K), L) = 1$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U038` | Theorem (3.5) | Chapter IV, §3, source L12040 | (3.5) Theorem (Hilbert 90). For a cyclic field extension $L\|K$ one always has $$ H ^ {- 1} \big (G (L \| K), L ^ {*} \big) = 1. $$ In other words: An element $\alpha \in L^{*}$ of norm $N_{L\|K}(\alpha) = 1$ is of the form $\alpha = \beta^{\sigma - 1}$ , where $\beta \in L^{*}$ and $\sigma$ is a generator of $G(L\|K)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U039` | Corollary (3.6) | Chapter IV, §3, source L12060 | (3.6) Corollary. Let $n$ be a natural number which is relatively prime to the characteristic of the field $K$ , and assume that $\mu_n \subseteq K$ . Then the abelian extensions $L\|K$ of exponent $n$ correspond 1-1 to the subgroups $\Delta \subseteq K^*$ which contain $K^{*n}$ , via the rule $$ \Delta \mapsto L = K \left(\sqrt [ n ]{\Delta}\right), $$ and we have $$ G (L \| K) \cong \operatorname{Hom} (\Delta / K ^ {* n}, \mu_ {n}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U040` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §3, source L12092 | The functions $f_{a}$ are called 1-coboundaries and form a subgroup $B^{1}(G,A)$ of $Z^{1}(G,A)$ . We define $$ H ^ {1} (G, A) = Z ^ {1} (G, A) / B ^ {1} (G, A) $$ and obtain as a first result about this group the | — |
| `FC09-C04-U041` | Proposition (3.7) | Chapter IV, §3, source L12100 | (3.7) Proposition. If $G$ is cyclic, then $H^1(G, A) \cong H^{-1}(G, A)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U042` | Proposition (3.8) | Chapter IV, §3, source L12124 | (3.8) Proposition. For a finite Galois field extension $L\|K$ , one has that $$ H ^ {1} \big (G (L \| K), L ^ {*} \big) = 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U043` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §4, source L12192 | The further development will now be based on a fixed choice of a surjective continuous homomorphism $$ d: G \longrightarrow \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ from the profinite group $G$ onto the procyclic group $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}} = \varprojlim \mathbb{Z} / n\mathbb{Z}$ (see §2, example 4). This homomorphism will produce a theory which is an abstract reflection of the ramification theory of $\mathfrak{p}$ -adic number fields. Indeed, in the case where $G$ is the absolute Galois group $G_{k} = G(\bar{k} \| k)$ of a $\mathfrak{p}$ -adic number field $k$ , such a surjective homomorphism $d: G \to \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ arises via the maximal unramified extension $\tilde{k} \| k$ : if $\mathbb{F}_q$ is the residue class field of $k$ , then, by chap. II, §9, p. 173 and example 5 in §2, we have canonical isomorphisms $$ G (\tilde {k} \| k) \cong G (\overline {{{\mathbb {F}}}} _ {q} \| \mathbb {F} _ {q}) \cong \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ which associate to the element $1 \in \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ the Frobenius automorphism $\varphi \in G(\tilde{k} \| k)$ . It is defined by $$ a ^ {\varphi} \equiv a ^ {q} \bmod \tilde {\mathfrak {p}} \quad \text { for } \quad a \in \tilde {\mathcal {O}}, $$ where $\tilde{O}$ , resp. $\tilde{p}$ , denote the valuation ring of $\tilde{k}$ , resp. its maximal ideal. The homomorphism $d: G \to \widehat{Z}$ in question is then given, in this concrete case, as the composite $$ G \longrightarrow G (\tilde {k} \| k) \stackrel {\sim} {\longrightarrow} \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}. $$ In the abstract situation, the initial choice of a surjective homomorphism $d: G \to \widehat{Z}$ mimics the p-adic case, but the applications of the theory are by no means confined to p-adic number fields. The kernel I of d has a certain fixed field $\tilde{k}\|k$ , and d induces an isomorphism $G(\tilde{k}\|k) \cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U044` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §4, source L12218 | More generally, for any field K we denote by $I_{K}$ the kernel of the restriction $d: G_{K} \to \widehat{Z}$ , and call it the inertia group over K. Since $$ I _ {K} = G _ {K} \cap I = G _ {K} \cap G _ {\tilde {k}} = G _ {K \tilde {k}}, $$ the fixed field $\widetilde{K}$ of $I_K$ is the composite $$ \widetilde {K} = K \widetilde {k}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U045` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §4, source L12230 | We call $\widetilde{K}\|K$ the maximal unramified extension of $K$ . We put $$ f _ {K} = \left(\widehat {\mathbb {Z}}: d (G _ {K})\right), \quad e _ {K} = (I: I _ {K}) $$ and obtain, when $f_{K}$ is finite, a surjective homomorphism $$ d _ {K} = \frac {1}{f _ {K}} d: G _ {K} \longrightarrow \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ with kernel $I_K$ , and an isomorphism $$ d _ {K}: G (\widetilde {K} \| K) \stackrel {\sim} {\longrightarrow} \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U046` | Definition (4.1) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12248 | (4.1) Definition. The element $\varphi_K \in G(\widetilde{K}\|K)$ such that $d_K(\varphi_K) = 1$ is called the Frobenius over $K$ . For a field extension $L\|K$ we define the inertia degree $f_{L\|K}$ and the ramification index $e_{L\|K}$ by $$ f _ {L \mid K} = \left(d (G _ {K}): d (G _ {L})\right) \quad \text { and } \quad e _ {L \mid K} = (I _ {K}: I _ {L}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U047` | Proposition (4.2) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12262 | (4.2) Proposition. For every extension $L\|K$ we have the "fundamental identity" $$ [ L: K ] = f _ {L \| K} e _ {L \| K}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U048` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §4, source L12282 | $L\|K$ is called unramified if $e_{L\|K} = 1$ , i.e., if $L \subseteq \widetilde{K}$ . $L\|K$ is called totally ramified if $f_{L\|K} = 1$ , i.e., if $L \cap \widetilde{K} = K$ . In the unramified case, we have the surjective homomorphism $$ G (\widetilde {K} \| K) \to G (L \| K) $$ and, if $f_{K} < \infty$ , we call the image $\varphi_{L\|K}$ of $\varphi_{K}$ the Frobenius automorphism of $L\|K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U049` | Proposition (4.3) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12304 | (4.3) Proposition. If $f_{K}$ and $f_{L}$ are finite, then $f_{L\|K} = f_{L} / f_{K}$ , and we have the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} G _ {L} & \xrightarrow {d _ {L}} & \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} \\ \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow f _ {L \| K} \\ G _ {K} & \xrightarrow {d _ {K}} & \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}. \end{array} $$ In particular, one has $\varphi_L\|_{\tilde{K}} = \varphi_K^{f_{L\|K}}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U050` | Proposition (4.4) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12320 | (4.4) Proposition. For a finite Galois extension $L\|K$ the mapping $$ \operatorname{Frob} (\widetilde {L} \| K) \longrightarrow G (L \| K), \quad \sigma \longmapsto \sigma \| _ {L}, $$ is surjective. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U051` | Proposition (4.5) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12338 | (4.5) Proposition. Let $\tilde{\sigma} \in \operatorname{Frob}(\widetilde{L}\|K)$ , and let $\Sigma$ be the fixed field of $\tilde{\sigma}$ . Then we have: $$ \text {(i)} \quad f _ {\Sigma \| K} = d _ {K} (\tilde {\sigma}), \text {(ii)} \quad [ \Sigma : K ] < \infty , \text {(iii)} \quad \widetilde {\Sigma} = \widetilde {L}, \text {(iv)} \quad \tilde {\sigma} = \varphi_ {\Sigma}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U052` | Definition (4.6) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12368 | (4.6) Definition. A henselian valuation of $A_{k}$ with respect to $d: G \to \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ is a homomorphism $$ v: A _ {k} \to \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ satisfying the following properties: (i) $v(A_{k}) = Z\supseteq \mathbb{Z}$ and $Z / nZ\cong \mathbb{Z} / n\mathbb{Z}$ for all $n\in \mathbb{N}$ , (ii) $v(N_{K\|k}A_K) = f_K Z$ for all finite extensions $K\|k$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U053` | Proposition (4.7) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12382 | (4.7) Proposition. For every field $K$ which is finite over $k$ , the formula $$ v _ {K} = \frac {1}{f _ {K}} v \circ N _ {K \| k}: A _ {K} \longrightarrow Z $$ defines a surjective homomorphism satisfying the following properties: (i) $v_{K} = v_{K^{\sigma}} \circ \sigma$ for all $\sigma \in G$ . (ii) For every finite extension $L\|K$ , one has the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} A _ {L} & \xrightarrow {v _ {L}} & \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} \\ N _ {L \| K} \Big \downarrow & & \Big \downarrow f _ {L \| K} \\ A _ {K} & \xrightarrow {v _ {K}} & \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}. \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U054` | Definition (4.8) | Chapter IV, §4, source L12410 | (4.8) Definition. A prime element of $A_K$ is an element $\pi_K \in A_K$ such that $v_K(\pi_K) = 1$ . We put $$ U _ {K} = \left\{u \in A _ {K} \mid v _ {K} (u) = 0 \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U055` | Convention / notation | Chapter IV, §5, source L12456 | In the following we introduce the convention that the letter K, whenever it occurs without embellishments or commentary to the contrary, will always denote a field of finite degree over k. We furthermore impose the following axiomatic condition, which will be systematically assumed in the sequel. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U056` | Axiom (5.1) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12458 | (5.1) Axiom. For every unramified finite extension $L\|K$ one has $$ H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), U _ {L} \big) = 1 \quad \text { for } \quad i = 0, - 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U057` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §5, source L12464 | For an infinite extension $L\|K$ we set $$ N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} = \bigcap_ {M} N _ {M \| K} A _ {M}, $$ with $M\|K$ varying over the finite subextensions of $L\|K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U058` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §5, source L12472 | Our goal is to define a canonical homomorphism $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} $$ for every finite Galois extension $L\|K$ . To this end, we pass from $L\|K$ to the extension $\widetilde{L}\|K$ and define first a mapping on the semigroup $$ \operatorname{Frob} (\widetilde {L} \| K) = \left\{\sigma \in G (\widetilde {L} \| K) \mid d _ {K} (\sigma) \in \mathbb {N} \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U059` | Definition (5.2) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12484 | (5.2) Definition. The reciprocity map $$ r _ {\tilde {L} \| K}: \operatorname{Frob} (\widetilde {L} \| K) \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {\tilde {L} \| K} A _ {\tilde {L}} $$ is defined by $$ r _ {\tilde {L} \| K} (\sigma) = N _ {\Sigma \| K} (\pi_ {\Sigma}) \bmod N _ {\tilde {L} \| K} A _ {\tilde {L}}, $$ where $\Sigma$ is the fixed field of $\sigma$ and $\pi_{\Sigma} \in A_{\Sigma}$ is a prime element. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U060` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §5, source L12520 | Now we introduce the homomorphism $$ N = N _ {\tilde {L} \| \tilde {K}}: A _ {\tilde {L}} \longrightarrow A _ {\tilde {K}} $$ and prove two lemmas for it. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U061` | Lemma (5.3) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12528 | (5.3) Lemma. Let $\varphi, \sigma \in \operatorname{Frob}(\widetilde{L}\|K)$ with $d_K(\varphi) = 1$ , $d_K(\sigma) = n$ . If $\Sigma$ is the fixed field of $\sigma$ and $a \in A_{\Sigma}$ , then $$ N _ {\Sigma \| K} (a) = (N \circ \varphi_ {n}) (a) = (\varphi_ {n} \circ N) (a). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U062` | Lemma (5.4) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12550 | (5.4) Lemma. If $x \in H_0(G(\widetilde{L}\|\widetilde{K}), U_{\widetilde{L}})$ is fixed by an element $\varphi \in G(\widetilde{L}\|K)$ such that $d_K(\varphi) = 1$ , i.e., $x^\varphi = x$ , then $$ N (x) \in N _ {\tilde {L} \| K} U _ {\tilde {L}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U063` | Proposition (5.5) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12588 | (5.5) Proposition. The reciprocity map $$ r _ {\widetilde {L} \| K}: \operatorname{Frob} (\widetilde {L} \| K) \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {\widetilde {L} \| K} A _ {\widetilde {L}} $$ is multiplicative. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U064` | Proposition (5.6) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12662 | (5.6) Proposition. For every finite Galois extension $L\|K$ , there is a canonical homomorphism $$ r _ {L \mid K}: G (L \mid K) \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {L \mid K} A _ {L} $$ given by $$ r _ {L \| K} (\sigma) = N _ {\Sigma \| K} \left(\pi_ {\Sigma}\right) \bmod N _ {L \| K} A _ {L}, $$ where $\Sigma$ is the fixed field of a preimage $\tilde{\sigma} \in \mathrm{Frob}(\widetilde{L}\|K)$ of $\sigma \in G(L\|K)$ and $\pi_{\Sigma} \in A_{\Sigma}$ is a prime element. It is called the reciprocity homomorphism of $L\|K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U065` | Proposition (5.7) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12682 | (5.7) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is an unramified extension, then the reciprocity map $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} $$ is given by $$ r _ {L \| K} (\varphi_ {L \| K}) = \pi_ {K} \bmod N _ {L \| K} A _ {L}, $$ and is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U066` | Proposition (5.8) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12706 | (5.8) Proposition. Let $L\|K$ and $L'\|K'$ be finite Galois extensions, so that $K \subseteq K'$ and $L \subseteq L'$ , and let $\sigma \in G$ . Then we have the commutative diagrams $$ \begin{array}{c c c} G (L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime}) \xrightarrow {r _ {L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime}}} A _ {K ^ {\prime}} / N _ {L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime}} A _ {L ^ {\prime}} & G (L \| K) \xrightarrow {r _ {L \| K}} A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} \\ \Big \downarrow & \Big \downarrow_ {N _ {K ^ {\prime} \| K}} & \Big \downarrow_ {\sigma^ {*}} \\ G (L \| K) \xrightarrow {r _ {L \| K}} A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} & G (L ^ {\sigma} \| K ^ {\sigma}) \xrightarrow {r _ {L ^ {\sigma} \| K ^ {\sigma}}} A _ {K ^ {\sigma}} / N _ {L ^ {\sigma} \| K ^ {\sigma}} A _ {L ^ {\sigma}} \end{array} $$ where the vertical arrows on the left are given by $\sigma'\mapsto\sigma'\|_{L}$ , resp. by the conjugation $\tau\mapsto\sigma^{-1}\tau\sigma$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U067` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §5, source L12726 | Another very interesting functorial property of the reciprocity map is obtained via the transfer (Verlagerung in German). For an arbitrary group G, let $G'$ denote the commutator subgroup and write $$ G ^ {a b} = G / G ^ {\prime} $$ for the maximal abelian quotient group. If then $H \subseteq G$ is a subgroup of finite index, we have a canonical homomorphism $$ \operatorname{Ver}: G ^ {a b} \longrightarrow H ^ {a b}, $$ which is called transfer from G to H. This homomorphism is defined as follows (see [75], chap. IV, §1). Let $R$ be a system of representatives for the left cosets of $H$ in $G$ , $G = RH$ , $1 \in R$ . If $\sigma \in G$ we write, for every $\rho \in R$ , $$ \sigma \rho = \rho^ {\prime} \sigma_ {\rho}, \quad \sigma_ {\rho} \in H, \quad \rho^ {\prime} \in R, $$ and we define $$ \operatorname{Ver} \left(\sigma \bmod G ^ {\prime}\right) = \prod_ {\rho \in R} \sigma_ {\rho} \bmod H ^ {\prime}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U068` | Proposition (5.9) | Chapter IV, §5, source L12772 | (5.9) Proposition. Let $L\|K$ be a finite Galois extension and $K'$ an intermediate field. Then we have the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} G (L \| K ^ {\prime}) ^ {a b} & \xrightarrow {r _ {L \| K ^ {\prime}}} & A _ {K ^ {\prime}} / N _ {L \| K ^ {\prime}} A _ {L} \\ \text {Ver} \Big \uparrow & & \Big \uparrow \\ G (L \| K) ^ {a b} & \xrightarrow {r _ {L \| K}} & A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L}, \end{array} $$ where the arrow on the right is induced by inclusion. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U069` | Class Field Axiom (6.1) | Chapter IV, §6, source L12872 | (6.1) Class Field Axiom. For every cyclic extension $L\|K$ one has $$ \# H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), A _ {L} \big) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} [ L: K ] & \text {for i = 0 ,} \\ 1 & \text {for i = -1 .} \end{array} \right. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U070` | Proposition (6.2) | Chapter IV, §6, source L12880 | (6.2) Proposition. For a finite unramified extension $L\|K$ , one has $$ H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), U _ {L} \big) = 1 \quad \text { for } \quad i = 0, - 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U071` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §6, source L12896 | By definition, a class field theory is a pair of homomorphisms $$ \left(d: G \to \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}, v: A \to \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}\right), $$ where A is a G-module which satisfies axiom (6.1), d is a surjective continuous homomorphism, and v is a henselian valuation. From proposition (6.2) and §5, we obtain for every finite Galois extension $L\|K$ , the reciprocity homomorphism $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) ^ {a b} \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L}. $$ | `FC09-C04-U069`, `FC09-C04-U070` |
| `FC09-C04-U072` | Theorem (6.3) | Chapter IV, §6, source L12910 | (6.3) Theorem. For every finite Galois extension $L\|K$ , the homomorphism $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) ^ {a b} \longrightarrow A _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} $$ is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U073` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §6, source L12956 | The inverse of the mapping $r_{L\|K}: G(L\|K)^{ab} \to A_K / N_{L\|K} A_L$ gives, for every finite Galois extension L\|K, a surjective homomorphism $$ (, L \| K): A _ {K} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) ^ {a b} $$ with kernel $N_{L\|K}A_L$ . This map is called the norm residue symbol of $L\|K$ . From (5.8) and (5.9) we have the | `FC09-C04-U066`, `FC09-C04-U068` |
| `FC09-C04-U074` | Proposition (6.4) | Chapter IV, §6, source L12964 | (6.4) Proposition. Let $L\|K$ and $L'\|K'$ be finite Galois extensions such that $K \subseteq K'$ and $L \subseteq L'$ , and let $\sigma \in G$ . Then we have the commutative diagrams $$ \begin{array}{c c} A _ {K ^ {\prime}} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime})} G (L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime}) ^ {a b} & A _ {K} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L \| K)} G (L \| K) ^ {a b} \\ N _ {K ^ {\prime} \| K} \Biggl \downarrow & \sigma \Biggl \downarrow \\ A _ {K} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L \| K)} G (L \| K) ^ {a b}, & A _ {K ^ {\sigma}} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L ^ {\sigma} \| K ^ {\sigma})} G (L ^ {\sigma} \| K ^ {\sigma}) ^ {a b}, \end{array} $$ and if $K' \subseteq L$ , we have the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c} A _ {K ^ {\prime}} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L \| K ^ {\prime})} G (L \| K ^ {\prime}) ^ {a b} \\ \Bigg \uparrow \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \Bigg \uparrow_ {\text {Ver}} \\ A _ {K} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L \| K)} G (L \| K) ^ {a b}. \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U075` | Proposition (6.5) | Chapter IV, §6, source L12990 | (6.5) Proposition. One has $$ (a, \widetilde {K} \| K) = \varphi_ {K} ^ {v _ {K} (a)}, \quad \text { and thus } \quad d _ {K} \circ (\mathbf {\Omega}, \widetilde {K} \| K) = v _ {K}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U076` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §6, source L13006 | For every field K, we equip the group $A_{K}$ with a topology by declaring the cosets $aN_{L\|K}A_{L}$ to be a basis of neighbourhoods of $a \in A_{K}$ , where $L\|K$ varies over all finite Galois extensions of K. We call this topology the norm topology of $A_{K}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U077` | Proposition (6.6) | Chapter IV, §6, source L13008 | (6.6) Proposition. (i) The open subgroups of $A_K$ are precisely the closed subgroups of finite index. (ii) The valuation $v_{K}: A_{K} \to \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ is continuous. (iii) If $L\|K$ is a finite extension, then $N_{L\|K}: A_L \to A_K$ is continuous. (iv) $A_{K}$ is Hausdorff if and only if the group $$ A _ {K} ^ {0} = \bigcap_ {L} N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} $$ of universal norms is trivial. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U078` | Theorem (6.7) | Chapter IV, §6, source L13050 | (6.7) Theorem. Associating $$ L \longmapsto \mathcal {N} _ {L} = N _ {L \| K} A _ {L} $$ sets up a 1–1-correspondence between the finite abelian extensions $L\|K$ and the open subgroups $\mathcal{N}$ of $A_{K}$ . Furthermore, one has $$ L _ {1} \subseteq L _ {2} \Longleftrightarrow \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \supseteq \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \cap \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} \cap L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}. $$ The field L corresponding to the subgroup N of $A_{K}$ is called the class field associated with N. By (6.3), it satisfies $$ G (L \| K) \cong A _ {K} / \mathcal {N}. $$ | `FC09-C04-U072` |
| `FC09-C04-U079` | Proposition (7.1) | Chapter IV, §7, source L13424 | (7.1) Proposition. If $1 \to A \to B \to C \to 1$ is an exact sequence of $G$ -modules, then we obtain an exact hexagon $$ \begin{array}{c} H ^ {0} (G, A) \xrightarrow {f _ {1}} H ^ {0} (G, B) \\ \left. \begin{array}{c} f _ {6} \\ H ^ {- 1} (G, C) \end{array} \right. \xrightarrow {f _ {5}} H ^ {- 1} (G, B) \xleftarrow {f _ {4}} H ^ {- 1} (G, A) \xleftarrow {f _ {3}} H ^ {0} (G, C) \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C04-U080` | Definition (7.2) | Chapter IV, §7, source L13434 | (7.2) Definition. The Herbrand quotient of the $G$ -module $A$ is defined to be $$ h (G, A) = \frac {\# H ^ {0} (G , A)}{\# H ^ {- 1} (G , A)}, $$ provided that both orders are finite. | — |
| `FC09-C04-U081` | Proposition (7.3) | Chapter IV, §7, source L13444 | (7.3) Proposition. If $1 \to A \to B \to C \to 1$ is an exact sequence of $G$ -modules, then one has $$ h (G, B) = h (G, A) h (G, C) $$ in the sense that, whenever two of these quotients are defined, so is the third and the identity holds. For a finite $G$ -module $A$ , one has $h(G, A) = 1$ . | — |
| `FC09-C04-U082` | Definition / construction | Chapter IV, §7, source L13474 | If G is an arbitrary group and g a subgroup, then to any g-module B, we may associate the so-called induced G-module $$ A = \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {g} (B). $$ It consists of all functions $f: G \to B$ such that $f(x\tau) = f(x)^{\tau}$ for all $\tau \in g$ . The operation of $\sigma \in G$ is given by $$ f ^ {\sigma} (x) = f (\sigma x). $$ If $g = \{1\}$ , we write $\operatorname{Ind}_G(B)$ instead of $\operatorname{Ind}_G^g (B)$ . We have a canonical $g$ -homomorphism $$ \pi : \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {g} (B) \longrightarrow B, \quad f \longmapsto f (1), $$ which maps the $g$ -submodule $$ B ^ {\prime} = \left\{f \in \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {g} (B) \mid f (x) = 1 \text { for } x \notin g \right\} $$ isomorphically onto $B$ . We identify $B'$ with $B$ . If $g$ is of finite index, we find | — |
| `FC09-C04-U083` | Proposition (7.4) | Chapter IV, §7, source L13516 | (7.4) Proposition. Let $G$ be a finite cyclic group, $g$ a subgroup and $B$ a $g$ -module. Then we have canonically $$ H ^ {i} \big (G, \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {g} (B) \big) \cong H ^ {i} (g, B) \quad f o r \quad i = 0, - 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U001` | Theorem (1.1) | Chapter V, §1, source L13636 | (1.1) Theorem. For a cyclic extension $L\|K$ of local fields, one has $$ \# H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), L ^ {*} \big) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} [ L: K ] & \text {for i = 0 ,} \\ 1 & \text {for i = -1 .} \end{array} \right. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U002` | Corollary (1.2) | Chapter V, §1, source L13686 | (1.2) Corollary. If $L\|K$ is an unramified extension of local fields, then for $i = 0, -1$ , one has $$ H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), U _ {L} \big) = 1 \quad a n d \quad H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), U _ {L} ^ {(n)} \big) = 1 \quad f o r \quad n = 1, 2, \ldots $$ In particular, $$ N _ {L \| K} U _ {L} = U _ {K} \quad a n d \quad N _ {L \| K} U _ {L} ^ {(n)} = U _ {K} ^ {(n)}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U003` | Theorem (1.3) | Chapter V, §1, source L13772 | (1.3) Theorem. For every finite Galois extension $L\|K$ of local fields we have a canonical isomorphism $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) ^ {a b} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} K ^ {*} / N _ {L \| K} L ^ {*}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U004` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §1, source L13784 | Inverting $r_{L\|K}$ gives us the local norm residue symbol $$ (, L \| K): K ^ {*} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) ^ {a b}. $$ It is surjective and has kernel $N_{L\|K}L^{*}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U005` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §1, source L13792 | In global class field theory we will have to take into account the field $R = Q_{\infty}$ along with the p-adic number fields $Q_{p}$ . It also admits a reciprocity law: for the unique non-trivial Galois extension C\|R, we define the norm residue symbol $$ (\quad , \mathbb {C} \| \mathbb {R}): \mathbb {R} ^ {*} \longrightarrow G (\mathbb {C} \| \mathbb {R}) $$ by $$ (a, \mathbb {C} \| \mathbb {R}) \sqrt {- 1} = \sqrt {- 1} ^ {\operatorname{sgn} (a)}. $$ The kernel of $(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ is the group $\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ of all positive real numbers, which is again the group of norms $N_{\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}^{*} = \{z\bar{z} \mid z \in \mathbb{C}^{*}\}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U006` | Theorem (1.4) | Chapter V, §1, source L13808 | (1.4) Theorem. The rule $$ L \longmapsto \mathcal {N} _ {L} = N _ {L \| K} L ^ {*} $$ gives a 1-1-correspondence between the finite abelian extensions of a local field $K$ and the open subgroups $\mathcal{N}$ of finite index in $K^{*}$ . Furthermore, $$ L _ {1} \subseteq L _ {2} \Longleftrightarrow \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \supseteq \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \cap \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} \cap L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U007` | Proposition (1.5) | Chapter V, §1, source L13846 | (1.5) Proposition. If $K$ contains the $n$ -th roots of unity, and if the characteristic of $K$ does not divide $n$ , then the extension $L = K(\sqrt[n]{K^*})\|K$ is finite, and one has $$ N _ {L \| K} L ^ {*} = K ^ {* n} \quad \text { and } \quad G (L \| K) \cong K ^ {*} / K ^ {* n}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U008` | Convention / notation | Chapter V, §1, source L13852 | Theorem (1.4) is called the existence theorem, because its essential statement is that, for every open subgroup N of finite index in $K^{*}$ , there exists an abelian extension $L\|K$ such that $N_{L\|K}L^{*} = N$ . This is the “class field” of N. (Incidentally, when $\text{char}(K) = 0$ , every subgroup of finite index is automatically open – see chap. II, (5.7).) Every open subgroup of $K^{*}$ contains some higher unit group $U_{K}^{(n)}$ , as these form a basis of neighbourhoods of 1 in $K^{*}$ . We put $U_{K}^{(0)} = U_{K}$ and define: | `FC09-C02-U065`, `FC09-C05-U006` |
| `FC09-C05-U009` | Definition (1.6) | Chapter V, §1, source L13854 | (1.6) Definition. Let $L\|K$ be a finite abelian extension, and $n$ the smallest number $\geq 0$ such that $U_K^{(n)} \subseteq N_{L\|K}L^*$ . Then the ideal $$ \mathfrak {f} = \mathfrak {p} _ {K} ^ {n} $$ is called the conductor of $L\|K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U010` | Proposition (1.7) | Chapter V, §1, source L13862 | (1.7) Proposition. A finite abelian extension $L\|K$ is unramified if and only if its conductor is $f = 1$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U011` | Proposition (1.8) | Chapter V, §1, source L13868 | (1.8) Proposition. The group of norms of the extension $\mathbb{Q}_p(\mu_{p^n})\|\mathbb{Q}_p$ is the group $(p)\times U_{\mathbb{Q}_p}^{(n)}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U012` | Corollary (1.9) | Chapter V, §1, source L13892 | (1.9) Corollary. Every finite abelian extension of $L\|\mathbb{Q}_p$ is contained in a field $\mathbb{Q}_p(\zeta)$ , where $\zeta$ is a root of unity. In other words: The maximal abelian extension $Q_{p}^{ab}\|Q_{p}$ is generated by adjoining all roots of unity. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U013` | Theorem (1.10) | Chapter V, §1, source L13906 | (1.10) Theorem. Every finite abelian extension $L\|\mathbb{Q}$ is contained in a field $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta)$ generated by a root of unity $\zeta$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U014` | Convention / notation | Chapter V, §2, source L14024 | We have to invoke a transcendental method which makes use of the completion $\widehat{K}$ of the maximal unramified extension $\widetilde{K}$ of a local field K. We extend the Frobenius $\varphi \in G(\widetilde{K}\|K)$ to $\widehat{K}$ by continuity. First we prove the | — |
| `FC09-C05-U015` | Lemma (2.1) | Chapter V, §2, source L14026 | (2.1) Lemma. For every $c \in \mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}$ , resp. every $c \in U_{\widehat{K}}$ , the equation $$ x ^ {\varphi} - x = c, \quad r e s p. \quad x ^ {\varphi - 1} = c, $$ admits a solution in $\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}$ , resp. in $U_{\widehat{K}}$ . If $x^{\varphi} = x$ for $x \in \mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}$ , then $x \in \mathcal{O}_K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U016` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §2, source L14062 | For a power series $F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}$ , let $F^{\varphi}$ be the power series in $O_{\widehat{K}}X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}$ which arises from F by applying $\varphi$ to the coefficients of F. A Lubin-Tate series for a prime element $\pi$ of K is by definition a power series $e(X)\in\mathcal{O}_{K}X$ with the properties $$ e (X) \equiv \pi X \bmod \deg 2 \quad \text { and } \quad e (X) \equiv X ^ {q} \bmod \pi , $$ where $q = q_{K}$ denotes, as always, the number of elements in the residue class field of K. The totality of all Lubin-Tate series is denoted by $E_{\pi}$ . In $E_{\pi}$ there are in particular the polynomials $$ e (X) = u X ^ {q} + \pi (a _ {q - 1} X ^ {q - 1} + \dots + a _ {2} X ^ {2}) + \pi X, $$ where $u, a_{i} \in O_{K}$ and $u \equiv 1 \mod \pi$ . These are called the Lubin-Tate polynomials. The simplest one among them is the polynomial $X^{q} + \pi X$ . In the case $K = Q_{p}$ for example, $e(X) = (1 + X)^{p} - 1$ is a Lubin-Tate polynomial for the prime element p. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U017` | Proposition (2.2) | Chapter V, §2, source L14076 | (2.2) Proposition. Let $\pi$ and $\overline{\pi}$ be prime elements of $\widehat{K}$ , and let $e(X) \in \mathcal{E}_{\pi}$ , $\overline{e}(X) \in \mathcal{E}_{\overline{\pi}}$ be Lubin-Tate series. Let $L(X_1, \ldots, X_n) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i X_i$ be a linear form with coefficients $a_i \in \mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}$ such that $$ \pi L (X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}) = \overline {{\pi}} L ^ {\varphi} (X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}). $$ Then there is a uniquely determined power series $F(X_{1},\ldots ,X_{n}) \in \mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}X_1,\dots ,X_n$ satisfying $$ F (X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}) \equiv L (X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}) \bmod \deg 2, $$ $$ e \left(F \left(X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}\right)\right) = F ^ {\varphi} \left(\bar {e} \left(X _ {1}\right), \dots , \bar {e} \left(X _ {n}\right)\right). $$ If the coefficients of $e, \bar{e}, L$ lie in a complete subring $\mathcal{O}$ of $\mathcal{O}_{\hat{K}}$ such that $\mathcal{O}^{\varphi} = \mathcal{O}$ , then $F$ has coefficients in $\mathcal{O}$ as well. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U018` | Corollary (2.3) | Chapter V, §2, source L14154 | (2.3) Corollary. Let $\pi$ and $\overline{\pi}$ be prime elements of $K$ , and let $e \in \mathcal{E}_{\pi}$ , $\bar{e} \in \mathcal{E}_{\overline{\pi}}$ be Lubin-Tate series with coefficients in $\mathcal{O}_K$ . Let $\pi = u\overline{\pi}$ , $u \in U_K$ , and $u = \varepsilon^{\varphi - 1}$ , $\varepsilon \in U_{\widehat{K}}$ . Then there is a uniquely determined power series $\theta(X) \in \mathcal{O}_{\widehat{K}}X$ such that $\theta(X) \equiv \varepsilon X \mod \deg 2$ and $$ e \circ \theta = \theta^ {\varphi} \circ \bar {e}. $$ Furthermore, there is a uniquely determined power series $[u](X) \in \mathcal{O}_KX$ such that $[u](X) \equiv uX \bmod \deg 2$ and $$ \bar {e} \circ [ u ] = [ u ] \circ \bar {e}. $$ They satisfy $$ \theta^ {\varphi} = \theta \circ [ u ]. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U019` | Theorem (2.4) | Chapter V, §2, source L14176 | (2.4) Theorem. Let $a = up^{v_p(a)} \in \mathbb{Q}_p^*$ , and let $\zeta$ be a primitive $p^n$ -th root of unity. Then one has $$ \left(a, \mathbb {Q} _ {p} (\zeta) \| \mathbb {Q} _ {p}\right) \zeta = \zeta^ {u ^ {- 1}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U020` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §3, source L14290 | (bilinear in the multiplicative sense). This pairing is called the Hilbert symbol. Its relation to the norm residue symbol is described explicitly in the following proposition. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U021` | Proposition (3.1) | Chapter V, §3, source L14292 | (3.1) Proposition. For $a, b \in K^{*}$ , the Hilbert symbol $\left(\frac{a,b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) \in \mu_{n}$ is given by $$ \left(a, K (\sqrt [ n ]{b}) \| K\right) \sqrt [ n ]{b} = \left(\frac {a , b}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) \sqrt [ n ]{b}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U022` | Proposition (3.2) | Chapter V, §3, source L14308 | (3.2) Proposition. (i) $\left(\frac{aa', b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = \left(\frac{a, b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left(\frac{a', b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right),$ (ii) $\left(\frac{a, bb'}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = \left(\frac{a, b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\left(\frac{a, b'}{\mathfrak{p}}\right),$ (iii) $\left(\frac{a,b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = 1\Longleftrightarrow a$ is a norm from the extension $K(\sqrt[n]{b})\|K,$ (iv) $\left(\frac{a,b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = \left(\frac{b,a}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)^{-1},$ (v) $\left(\frac{a,1 - a}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = 1$ and $\left(\frac{a, - a}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = 1,$ (vi) If $\left(\frac{a,b}{\mathfrak{p}}\right) = 1$ for all $b\in K^{*}$ , then $a\in K^{*n}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U023` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §3, source L14356 | Next we determine the Hilbert symbol explicitly in the case where K is a local field $(\neq\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C})$ whose residue characteristic p does not divide n. We call this the case of the tame Hilbert symbol. Since $\mu_{n}\subseteq\mu_{q-1}$ one has $n\|q-1$ in that case. First we establish the | — |
| `FC09-C05-U024` | Lemma (3.3) | Chapter V, §3, source L14358 | (3.3) Lemma. Let $(n, p) = 1$ and $x \in K^{*}$ . The extension $K(\sqrt[n]{x})\|K$ is unramified if and only if $x \in U_{K}K^{*n}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U025` | Proposition (3.4) | Chapter V, §3, source L14370 | (3.4) Proposition. If $(n, p) = 1$ and $a, b \in K^{*}$ , then $$ \left(\frac {a , b}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \omega \left((- 1) ^ {\alpha \beta} \frac {b ^ {\alpha}}{a ^ {\beta}}\right) ^ {(q - 1) / n}, $$ where $\alpha = v_{K}(a),\beta = v_{K}(b)$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U026` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §3, source L14416 | We call it the Legendre symbol, or the n-th power residue symbol. Both names are justified by the | — |
| `FC09-C05-U027` | Proposition (3.5) | Chapter V, §3, source L14418 | (3.5) Proposition. Let $(n, p) = 1$ and $u \in U_K$ . Then one has $$ \left(\frac {u}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad u \text { is an } n \text {-th power mod } \mathfrak {p} _ {K}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U028` | Theorem (3.6) | Chapter V, §3, source L14440 | (3.6) Theorem. Let $n = 2$ . For $a, b \in \mathbb{Q}_p^*$ we write $$ a = p ^ {\alpha} a ^ {\prime}, \quad b = p ^ {\beta} b ^ {\prime}, \quad a ^ {\prime}, b ^ {\prime} \in U _ {\mathbb {Q} _ {p}}. $$ If $p \neq 2$ , then $$ \left(\frac {a , b}{p}\right) = (- 1) ^ {\frac {p - 1}{2} \alpha \beta} \left(\frac {a ^ {\prime}}{p}\right) ^ {\beta} \left(\frac {b ^ {\prime}}{p}\right) ^ {\alpha}. $$ In particular, one has $\left(\frac{p,p}{p}\right) = (-1)^{(p - 1) / 2}$ and $\left(\frac{p,u}{p}\right) = \left(\frac{u}{p}\right)$ , if $u$ is a unit. If $p = 2$ and $a,b\in U_{\mathbb{Q}_2}$ , then $$ \left(\frac {2 , a}{2}\right) = (- 1) ^ {(a ^ {2} - 1) / 8}, $$ $$ \left(\frac {a , b}{2}\right) = \left(\frac {b , a}{2}\right) = (- 1) ^ {\frac {a - 1}{2} \frac {b - 1}{2}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U029` | Convention / notation | Chapter V, §3, source L14480 | So let $\mu_{p^{\nu}} \subseteq K$ , choose a prime element $\pi$ of $K$ , and let $W$ be the ring of integers of the maximal unramified subextension $T$ of $K\|\mathbb{Q}_p$ (i.e., the ring of Witt vectors over the residue class field of $K$ ). Then every element $x \in K$ can be written in the form $$ x = f (\pi), $$ with a Laurent series $f(X) \in W((X))$ . For an arbitrary Laurent series $f(X) = \sum_{i\geq -m}a_iX^i\in W((X))$ , let $f^{\mathcal{P}}(X)$ denote the series $$ f ^ {\mathcal {P}} (X) = \sum_ {i} a _ {i} ^ {\varphi} X ^ {i p}, $$ where $\varphi$ is the Frobenius automorphism of W. Further, let $\operatorname{Res}(fdX) \in W$ denote the residue of the differential fdX, $$ d \log f := \frac {f ^ {\prime}}{f} d X, $$ and $$ \log f := \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\infty} (- 1) ^ {i + 1} \frac {(f - 1) ^ {i}}{i}, $$ if $f \in 1 + pWX$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U030` | Theorem (3.7) | Chapter V, §3, source L14528 | (3.7) Theorem. If $x, y \in K^*$ and $f, g \in W((X))^*$ such that $f(\pi) = x$ and $g(\pi) = y$ , then $$ \left(\frac {x , y}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \zeta^ {w (x, y)} $$ where $$ w (x, y) = T r _ {W \| \mathbb {Z} _ {p}} \operatorname{Res} h \cdot \left(\frac {1}{p} \log \frac {f ^ {p}}{f ^ {\mathcal {P}}} d \log g - \frac {1}{p} \log \frac {g ^ {p}}{g ^ {\mathcal {P}}} \frac {1}{p} d \log f ^ {\mathcal {P}}\right) \bmod p ^ {\nu}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U031` | Proposition (3.8) | Chapter V, §3, source L14550 | (3.8) Proposition. For $a \in U_{\Phi_{\nu}}^{(2p^{\nu - 1})}$ and $b \in \Phi_{\nu}^{*}$ one has $$ \left(\frac {a , b}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \zeta^ {S (\zeta \log a D \log b) / p ^ {\nu}},\tag{1} $$ where $D \log b$ denotes the formal logarithmic derivative in $\pi$ of an arbitrary representation of b as an integral power series in $\pi$ with coefficients in $Z_{p}$ . For $a \in U_{\Phi_v}^{(1)}$ one has furthermore the two supplementary theorems (2) $$ \left(\frac {\zeta , a}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \zeta^ {S (\log a) / p ^ {\nu}},\tag{3} $$ $$ \left(\frac {a , \pi}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \zeta^ {S ((\zeta / \pi) \log a) / p ^ {\nu}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U032` | Convention / notation | Chapter V, §3, source L14576 | one may also interpret the $\zeta$ -exponent in the formulae (1)-(3) as the $(p-1)$ -st coefficient of a $\pi$ -adic expansion of $\log a D \log b$ . In this way it appears as a formal residue $\mathrm{Res}_{\pi} \frac{1}{\pi^p} \log a D \log b$ . As to the supplementary theorems, one has to define also $D \log \zeta = -\zeta^{-1}$ , $D \log \pi = \pi^{-1}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U033` | Definition (4.1) | Chapter V, §4, source L14648 | (4.1) Definiton. A (1-dimensional, commutative) formal group over a ring $\mathcal{O}$ is a formal power series $F(X,Y)\in \mathcal{O}X,Y$ with the following properties: (i) $F(X,Y)\equiv X + Y\bmod \deg 2,$ $$ \begin{array}{l l} \text {(ii)} & F (X, Y) = F (Y, X) \\ \text {(iii)} & F (X, F (Y, Z)) = F (F (X, Y), Z) \end{array} \qquad \begin{array}{l l} \text {``commutativity''}, \\ \text {``associativity''.} \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U034` | Definition (4.2) | Chapter V, §4, source L14696 | (4.2) Definition. A homomorphism $f: F \to G$ between two formal groups is a power series $f(X) = a_{1}X + a_{2}X^{2} + \cdots \in \mathcal{O}X$ such that $$ f (F (X, Y)) = G \big (f (X), f (Y) \big). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U035` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §4, source L14702 | In example 3, for instance, the power series f is a homomorphism of the formal group F to the additive group $G_{a}$ . It is called the logarithm of F. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U036` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §4, source L14704 | A homomorphism $f: F \to G$ is an isomorphism if $a_1 = f'(0)$ is a unit, i.e., if there is a homomorphism $g = f^{-1}: G \to F$ such that $$ f (g (X)) = g (f (X)) = X. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U037` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §4, source L14710 | If the power series $f(X) = a_{1}X + a_{2}X^{2} + \cdots$ satisfies the equation $f(F(X,Y)) = G(f(X), f(Y))$ , but its coefficients belong to an extension ring $O'$ , then we call this a homomorphism defined over $O'$ . The following proposition is immediately evident. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U038` | Proposition (4.3) | Chapter V, §4, source L14712 | (4.3) Proposition. The homomorphisms $f: F \to F$ of a formal group $F$ over $\mathcal{O}$ form a ring $\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{O}}(F)$ in which addition and multiplication are defined by $$ (f \underset {F} {+} g) (X) = F \big (f (X), g (X) \big), \quad (f \circ g) (X) = f \big (g (X) \big). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U039` | Definition (4.4) | Chapter V, §4, source L14718 | (4.4) Definition. A formal $\mathcal{O}$ -module is a formal group $F$ over $\mathcal{O}$ together with a ring homomorphism $$ \mathcal {O} \longrightarrow \operatorname{End} _ {\mathcal {O}} (F), \quad a \longmapsto [ a ] _ {F} (X), $$ such that $[a]_F(X) \equiv aX \mod \deg 2$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U040` | Definition (4.5) | Chapter V, §4, source L14734 | (4.5) Definition. A Lubin-Tate module over $O_{K}$ for the prime element $\pi$ is a formal $O_{K}$ -module F such that $$ [ \pi ] _ {F} (X) \equiv X ^ {q} \bmod \pi . $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U041` | Example / comparison | Chapter V, §4, source L14742 | Example: The formal multiplicative group $\mathbb{G}_m$ is a formal $\mathbb{Z}_p$ -module with respect to the mapping $$ \mathbb {Z} _ {p} \rightarrow \operatorname{End} _ {\mathbb {Z} _ {p}} (\mathbb {G} _ {m}), \quad a \mapsto [ a ] _ {\mathbb {G} _ {m}} (X) = (1 + X) ^ {a} - 1 = \sum_ {\nu = 1} ^ {\infty} \binom {a} {\nu} X ^ {\nu}. $$ $\mathbb{G}_m$ is a Lubin-Tate module for the prime element $p$ because $$ [ p ] _ {\mathbb {G} _ {m}} (X) = (1 + X) ^ {p} - 1 \equiv X ^ {p} \bmod p. $$ The following theorem gives a complete and explicit overall view of the totality of all Lubin-Tate modules. Let $e(X)$ , $\bar{e}(X) \in \mathcal{O}_KX$ be Lubin-Tate series for the prime element $\pi$ of K, and let $$ F _ {e} (X, Y) \in \mathcal {O} _ {K} [ [ X, Y ] ] \quad \text { and } \quad [ a ] _ {e, \bar {e}} (X) \in \mathcal {O} _ {K} [ [ X ] ] $$ $(a \in \mathcal{O}_{K})$ be the power series (uniquely determined according to (2.2)) such that $$ F _ {e} (X, Y) \equiv X + Y \bmod {\deg 2}, \quad e \big (F _ {e} (X, Y) \big) = F _ {e} \big (e (X), e (Y) \big), $$ $$ [ a ] _ {e, \bar {e}} (X) \equiv a X \bmod \deg 2, \quad e ([ a ] _ {e, \bar {e}} (X)) = [ a ] _ {e, \bar {e}} (\bar {e} (X)). $$ If $e(X) = \bar{e}(X)$ we simply write $[a]_{e,\bar{e}}(X) = [a]_e(X)$ . | `FC09-C05-U017` |
| `FC09-C05-U042` | Theorem (4.6) | Chapter V, §4, source L14772 | (4.6) Theorem. (i) The Lubin-Tate modules for $\pi$ are precisely the series $F_{e}(X,Y)$ , with the formal $\mathcal{O}_K$ -module structure given by $$ \mathcal {O} _ {K} \longrightarrow \operatorname{End} _ {\mathcal {O} _ {K}} (F _ {e}), \quad a \longmapsto [ a ] _ {e} (X). $$ (ii) For every $a \in \mathcal{O}_K$ the power series $[a]_{e,\bar{e}}(X)$ is a homomorphism $$ [ a ] _ {e, \bar {e}}: F _ {\bar {e}} \longrightarrow F _ {e} $$ of formal O-modules, and it is an isomorphism if a is a unit. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U043` | Proposition (5.1) | Chapter V, §5, source L14850 | (5.1) Proposition. Let $F$ be a formal $\mathcal{O}_{K}$ -module. Then the set $\overline{\mathfrak{p}}$ with the operations $$ x + _ {F} y = F (x, y) \quad \text { and } \quad a \cdot x = [ a ] _ {F} (x), $$ $x, y \in \overline{\mathfrak{p}}, a \in \mathcal{O}_K$ , is an $\mathcal{O}_K$ -module in the usual sense. We denote it by $\overline{\mathfrak{p}}_F$ . If $f: F \to G$ is a homomorphism (isomorphism) of formal $\mathcal{O}_K$ -modules, then $$ f: \overline {{{\mathfrak {p}}}} _ {F} \longrightarrow \overline {{{\mathfrak {p}}}} _ {G}, \quad x \longmapsto f (x), $$ is a homomorphism (isomorphism) of ordinary $O_{K}$ -modules. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U044` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §5, source L14868 | We now consider a Lubin-Tate module $F$ for the prime element $\pi$ of $\mathcal{O}_K$ . We define the group of $\pi^n$ -division points by $$ F (n) = \left\{\lambda \in \overline {{{\mathfrak {p}}}} _ {F} \mid \pi^ {n} \cdot \lambda = 0 \right\} = \ker \left([ \pi^ {n} ] _ {F}\right). $$ This is an $O_{K}$ -module, and an $O_{K}/\pi^{n}O_{K}$ -module because it is killed by $\pi^{n}O_{K}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U045` | Proposition (5.2) | Chapter V, §5, source L14876 | (5.2) Proposition. $F(n)$ is a free $\mathcal{O}_K / \pi^n\mathcal{O}_K$ -module of rank 1. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U046` | Corollary (5.3) | Chapter V, §5, source L14886 | (5.3) Corollary. Associating $a \mapsto [a]_F$ we obtain canonical isomorphisms $$ \mathcal {O} _ {K} / \pi^ {n} \mathcal {O} _ {K} \longrightarrow \operatorname{End} _ {\mathcal {O} _ {K}} (F (n)) \quad \text { and } \quad U _ {K} / U _ {K} ^ {(n)} \longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut} _ {\mathcal {O} _ {K}} (F (n)). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U047` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §5, source L14894 | Given a Lubin-Tate module $F$ for the prime element $\pi$ , we now define the field of $\pi^n$ -division points by $$ L _ {n} = K (F (n)). $$ Since $F(n) \subseteq F(n + 1)$ we get a tower of fields $$ K \subseteq L _ {1} \subseteq L _ {2} \subseteq \dots \subseteq L _ {\pi} := \bigcup_ {n = 1} ^ {\infty} L _ {n}. $$ These fields are also called the Lubin-Tate extensions. They only depend on the prime element $\pi$ , not on the Lubin-Tate module $F$ . For if $G$ is another Lubin-Tate module for $\pi$ , then by (4.6), there is an isomorphism $f: F \to G$ , $f \in \mathcal{O}_KX$ such that $G(n) = f(F(n)) \subseteq K(F(n))$ , and hence $K(G(n)) = K(F(n))$ . If $F$ is the Lubin-Tate module $F_e$ belonging to a Lubin-Tate polynomial $e(X) \in \mathcal{E}_{\pi}$ , then $e(X) = [\pi]_F(X)$ and $L_n\|K$ is the splitting field of the $n$ -fold iteration $$ e ^ {n} (X) = (e \circ \dots \circ e) (X) = [ \pi^ {n} ] _ {F} (X). $$ | `FC09-C05-U042` |
| `FC09-C05-U048` | Example / comparison | Chapter V, §5, source L14912 | Example: If $\mathcal{O}_K = \mathbb{Z}_p$ and $F$ is the Lubin-Tate module $\mathbb{G}_m$ , then $$ e ^ {n} (X) = [ p ^ {n} ] _ {\mathbb {G} _ {m}} (X) = (1 + X) ^ {p ^ {n}} - 1. $$ So $\mathbb{G}_{m}(n)$ consists of the elements $\zeta-1$ , where $\zeta$ varies over the $p^{n}$ -th roots of unity. $L_{n}\|K$ is therefore the $p^{n}$ -th cyclotomic extension $\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{n}})\|\mathbb{Q}_{p}$ . The following theorem shows the complete analogy of Lubin-Tate extensions with cyclotomic fields. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U049` | Theorem (5.4) | Chapter V, §5, source L14920 | (5.4) Theorem. $L_{n}\|K$ is a totally ramified abelian extension of degree $q^{n - 1}(q - 1)$ with Galois group $$ G (L _ {n} \| K) \cong \operatorname{Aut} _ {\mathcal {O} _ {K}} (F (n)) \cong U _ {K} / U _ {K} ^ {(n)}, $$ i.e., for every $\sigma \in G(L_n\|K)$ there is a unique class $u \bmod U_K^{(n)}$ , with $u \in U_K$ such that $$ \lambda^ {\sigma} = [ u ] _ {F} (\lambda) \quad f o r \quad \lambda \in F (n). $$ Furthermore the following is true: let $F$ be the Lubin-Tate module $F_{e}$ associated to the polynomial $e(X) \in \mathcal{E}_{\pi}$ , and let $\lambda_{n} \in F(n) \setminus F(n-1)$ . Then $\lambda_{n}$ is a prime element of $L_{n}$ , i.e., $L_{n} = K(\lambda_{n})$ , and $$ \phi_ {n} (X) = \frac {e ^ {n} (X)}{e ^ {n - 1} (X)} = X ^ {q ^ {n - 1} (q - 1)} + \dots + \pi \in \mathcal {O} _ {K} [ X ] $$ is its minimal polynomial. In particular one has $N_{L_n\|K}(-\lambda_n) = \pi$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U050` | Theorem (5.5) | Chapter V, §5, source L14970 | (5.5) Theorem. For the field $L_{n}\|K$ of $\pi^n$ -division points and for $a = u\pi^{v_K(a)} \in K^*, u \in U_K$ , one has $$ (a, L _ {n} \| K) \lambda = [ u ^ {- 1} ] _ {F} (\lambda), \quad \lambda \in F (n). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U051` | Corollary (5.6) | Chapter V, §5, source L15014 | (5.6) Corollary. The field $L_{n}\|K$ of $\pi^n$ -division points is the class field relative to the group $(\pi)\times U_K^{(n)}\subseteq K^*$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U052` | Corollary (5.7) | Chapter V, §5, source L15028 | (5.7) Corollary. The maximal abelian extension of $K$ is the composite $$ K ^ {a b} = \widetilde {K} L _ {\pi}, $$ where $L_{\pi}$ is the union $\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty} L_n$ of the fields $L_n$ of $\pi^n$ -division points. | — |
| `FC09-C05-U053` | Proposition (6.1) | Chapter V, §6, source L15152 | (6.1) Proposition. Let $L_{n}\|K$ be the field of $\pi^n$ -division points of a Lubin-Tate module for the prime element $\pi$ . Then $$ G _ {i} (L _ {n} \| K) = G (L _ {n} \| L _ {k}) \quad \text { for } \quad q ^ {k - 1} \leq i \leq q ^ {k} - 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U054` | Theorem (6.2) | Chapter V, §6, source L15186 | (6.2) Theorem. If $L\|K$ is a finite abelian extension, then the norm residue symbol $$ (\quad , L \| K): K ^ {*} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) $$ maps the group $U_K^{(n)}$ onto the group $G^n (L\|K)$ , for $n\geq 0$ . | — |
| `FC09-C05-U055` | Definition / construction | Chapter V, §6, source L15216 | Higher ramification groups $G^{t}(L\|K)$ were introduced for arbitrary real numbers $t \geq -1$ . Thus we may ask for which numbers they change. We call these numbers the jumps of the filtration $\{G^{t}(L\|K)\}_{t \geq -1}$ of $G(L\|K)$ . In other words, t is a jump if for all $\varepsilon > 0$ , one has $$ G ^ {t} (L \| K) \neq G ^ {t + \varepsilon} (L \| K). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C05-U056` | Proposition (6.3) | Chapter V, §6, source L15222 | (6.3) Proposition $(H_{ASSE}-A_{RF})$ . For a finite abelian extension $L\|K$ , the jumps of the filtration $\{G^{t}(L\|K)\}_{t\geq-1}$ of $G(L\|K)$ are rational integers. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U001` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15234 | An adèle of K — this curious expression, which has the stress on the second syllable, is derived from the original term “additive idèle” — is a family $$ \alpha = (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}) $$ of elements $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} \in K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ where $\mathfrak{p}$ runs through all primes of $K$ , and $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is integral in $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ for almost all $\mathfrak{p}$ . The adèles form a ring, which is denoted by $$ \mathbb {A} _ {K} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} K _ {\mathfrak {p}}. $$ Addition and multiplication are defined componentwise. This kind of product is called the “restricted product” of the $K_{p}$ with respect to the subrings $O_{p} \subseteq K_{p}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U002` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15248 | The idèle group of K is defined to be the unit group $$ I _ {K} = \mathbb {A} _ {K} ^ {*}. $$ Thus an idèle is a family $$ \alpha = (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}) $$ of elements $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} \in K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ where $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is a unit in the ring $\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ of integers of $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , for almost all $\mathfrak{p}$ . In analogy with $\mathbb{A}_K$ , we write the idèle group as the restricted product $$ I _ {K} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} $$ with respect to the unit groups $\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ . For every finite set of primes $S$ , $I_{K}$ contains the subgroup $$ I _ {K} ^ {S} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \not \in S} U _ {\mathfrak {p}} $$ of $S$ -idèles, where $U_{\mathfrak{p}} = K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ for $\mathfrak{p}$ infinite complex, and $U_{\mathfrak{p}} = \mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ for $\mathfrak{p}$ infinite real. One clearly has $$ I _ {K} = \bigcup_ {S} I _ {K} ^ {S}, $$ if $S$ varies over all finite sets of primes of $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U003` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15280 | The inclusions $K \subseteq K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ allow us to define the diagonal embedding $$ K ^ {*} \longrightarrow I _ {K}, $$ which associates to $a \in K^{*}$ the idèle $\alpha \in I_{K}$ whose p-th component is the element a in $K_{p}$ . We thus view $K^{*}$ as a subgroup of $I_{K}$ and we call the elements of $K^{*}$ in $I_{K}$ principal idèles. The intersection $$ K ^ {S} = K ^ {*} \cap I _ {K} ^ {S} $$ consists of the numbers $a \in K^{*}$ which are units at all primes $\mathfrak{p} \notin S$ , $\mathfrak{p} \nmid \infty$ , and which are positive in $K_{\mathfrak{p}} = \mathbb{R}$ for all real infinite places $\mathfrak{p} \notin S$ . They are called $S$ -units. In particular, for the set $S_{\infty}$ of infinite places, $K^{S_{\infty}}$ is the unit group $\mathcal{O}_{K}^{*}$ of $\mathcal{O}_{K}$ . We get the following generalization of Dirichlet's unit theorem. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U004` | Proposition (1.1) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15294 | (1.1) Proposition. If $S$ contains all infinite places, then the homomorphism $$ \lambda : K ^ {S} \longrightarrow \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} \mathbb {R}, \quad \lambda (a) = \left(\log \| a \| _ {\mathfrak {p}}\right) _ {\mathfrak {p} \in S}, $$ has kernel $\mu(K)$ , and its image is a complete lattice in the $(s-1)$ -dimensional trace-zero space $H = \left\{(x_{\mathfrak{p}}) \in \prod_{\mathfrak{p} \in S} \mathbb{R} \mid \sum_{\mathfrak{p} \in S} x_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0\right\}$ , $s = \#S$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U005` | Definition (1.2) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15336 | (1.2) Definition. The elements of the subgroup $K^{*}$ of $I_{K}$ are called principal idèles and the quotient group $$ C _ {K} = I _ {K} / K ^ {*} $$ is called the idèle class group of $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U006` | Proposition (1.3) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15382 | (1.3) Proposition. $Cl_K \cong I_K / I_K^{S_\infty} K^*$ , and $\text{Pic}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}) \cong I_K / I_K^0 K^*$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U007` | Proposition (1.4) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15386 | (1.4) Proposition. $I_K = I_K^S K^*$ , i.e., $C_K = I_K^S K^* / K^*$ , if $S$ is a sufficiently big finite set of places of $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U008` | Proposition (1.5) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15400 | (1.5) Proposition. $K^{*}$ is a discrete, and therefore closed, subgroup of $I_{K}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U009` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15416 | As $K^{*}$ is closed in $I_{K}$ , the fact that $I_{K}$ is a locally compact Hausdorff topological group carries over to the idèle class group $C_{K} = I_{K}/K^{*}$ . For any idèle $\alpha = (\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}) \in I_{K}$ , its class in $C_{K}$ will be denoted by $[\alpha]$ . We define the absolute norm of $\alpha$ to be the real number $$ \mathfrak {N} (\alpha) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {v _ {\mathfrak {p}} \left(\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}\right)} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \left\| \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} \right\| _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {- 1}. $$ If $x \in K^*$ is a principal idèle, then we find by chap. III, (1.3), that $\mathfrak{N}(x) = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \|x\|_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1} = 1$ . We thus have a continuous homomorphism $$ \mathfrak {N}: C _ {K} \longrightarrow \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}. $$ | `FC09-C03-U006`, `FC09-C06-U006` |
| `FC09-C06-U010` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15432 | Here the arrow $$ C _ {K} \longrightarrow P i c (\overline {{{\mathcal {O}}}}) $$ is induced by the continuous surjective homomorphism $$ I _ {K} \longrightarrow J (\bar {\mathcal {O}}), \quad (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}) \longmapsto \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}})}, $$ with kernel $$ I _ {K} ^ {0} = \left\{\left(\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}\right) \in I _ {K} \mid \| \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} \| _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 1 \text { for all } \mathfrak {p} \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U011` | Theorem (1.6) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15452 | (1.6) Theorem. The group $C_K^0 = \{[\alpha] \in C_K \mid \mathfrak{N}([\alpha]) = 1\}$ is compact. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U012` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §1, source L15466 | The idèle class group $C_K$ plays a similar rôle for the algebraic number field $K$ as the multiplicative group $K_{\mathfrak{p}}^*$ does for a $\mathfrak{p}$ -adic number field $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ . It comes equipped with a collection of canonical subgroups which are to be viewed as analogues of the higher unit groups $U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(n)} = 1 + \mathfrak{p}^n$ of a $\mathfrak{p}$ -adic number field $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ . Instead of $\mathfrak{p}^n$ , we take any integral ideal $\mathfrak{m} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p} \nmid \infty} \mathfrak{p}^{n_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ . We may also write it as a replete ideal $$ \mathfrak {m} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {n _ {\mathfrak {p}}} $$ with $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0$ for $\mathfrak{p}\|\infty$ , and we treat it in what follows as a module of $K$ . For every place $\mathfrak{p}$ of $K$ we put $U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(0)} = U_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , and $$ U _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {(n _ {\mathfrak {p}})} := \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 + \mathfrak {p} ^ {n _ {\mathfrak {p}}}, & \text {if} \mathfrak {p} \nmid \infty , \\ \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \subset K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}, & \text {if} \mathfrak {p} \text {is real}, \\ \mathbb {C} ^ {*} = K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}, & \text {if} \mathfrak {p} \text {is complex}, \end{array} \right. $$ for $n_{\mathfrak{p}} > 0$ . Given $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} \in K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ we write $$ \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} \equiv 1 \bmod \mathfrak {p} ^ {n _ {\mathfrak {p}}} \iff \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} \in U _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {(n _ {\mathfrak {p}})}. $$ For a finite prime p and $n_{p} > 0$ this means the usual congruence; for a real place, it symbolizes positivity, and for a complex place it is the empty condition. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U013` | Definition (1.7) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15486 | (1.7) Definition. The group $$ C _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = I _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} K ^ {*} / K ^ {*}, $$ formed from the idèle group $$ I _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} U _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {(n _ {\mathfrak {p}})}, $$ is called the congruence subgroup mod m, and the quotient group $C_{K}/C_{K}^{m}$ is called the ray class group mod m. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U014` | Proposition (1.8) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15504 | (1.8) Proposition. The closed subgroups of finite index of $C_K$ are precisely those subgroups that contain a congruence subgroup $C_K^m$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U015` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §1, source L15522 | The ray class groups can be given the following purely ideal-theoretic description.
