Let $J_{K}^{m}$ be the group of all fractional ideals relatively prime to m, and let $P_{K}^{m}$ be the group of all principal ideals ( $a) \in P_{K}$ such that $$ a \equiv 1 \bmod {\mathfrak {m}} \quad \text { and } \quad a \text { totally positive. } $$ The latter condition means that, for every real embedding $K \to \mathbb{R}$ , $a$ turns out to be positive. The congruence $a \equiv 1 \mod \mathfrak{m}$ means that $a$ is the quotient $b/c$ of two integers relatively prime to $\mathfrak{m}$ such that $b \equiv c \mod \mathfrak{m}$ . This is tantamount to saying that $a \equiv 1 \mod \mathfrak{p}^{n_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ in $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , i.e., $a \in U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(n_{\mathfrak{p}})}$ for all $\mathfrak{p}\|\mathfrak{m} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p} \nmid \infty} \mathfrak{p}^{n_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ . We put $$ C l _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = J _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} / P _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U016` | Proposition (1.9) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15536 | (1.9) Proposition. The homomorphism $$ (\quad): I _ {K} \longrightarrow J _ {K}, \quad \alpha \longmapsto (\alpha) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \nmid \infty} \mathfrak {p} ^ {v _ {\mathfrak {p}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}})}, $$ induces an isomorphism $$ C _ {K} / C _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \cong C l _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U017` | Proposition (1.10) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15564 | (1.10) Proposition. For any module $\mathfrak{m} = (m)$ of the field $\mathbb{Q}$ , one has $$ C _ {\mathbb {Q}} / C _ {\mathbb {Q}} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \cong C l _ {\mathbb {Q}} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \cong (\mathbb {Z} / m \mathbb {Z}) ^ {*}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U018` | Proposition (1.11) | Chapter VI, §1, source L15586 | (1.11) Proposition. There is an exact sequence $$ 1 \longrightarrow \mathcal {O} ^ {*} / \mathcal {O} _ {+} ^ {*} \longrightarrow \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \text { real }} \mathbb {R} ^ {*} / \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} ^ {1} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} \longrightarrow 1, $$ where $\mathcal{O}_+^*$ is the group of totally positive units of $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U019` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §2, source L15717 | For an idèle $\alpha \in I_L$ , we then define $\sigma \alpha \in I_L$ to be the idèle with components $$ (\sigma \alpha) _ {\sigma \mathfrak {P}} = \sigma \alpha_ {\mathfrak {P}} \in (\sigma L) _ {\sigma \mathfrak {P}}. $$ If $L\|K$ is a Galois extension with Galois group $G = G(L\|K)$ , then every $\sigma \in G$ yields an automorphism $\sigma : I_L \to I_L$ , i.e., $I_L$ is turned into an $G$ -module. As to the fixed module $I_L^G = \{\alpha \in I_L \mid \sigma \alpha = \alpha \text{ for all } \sigma \in G\}$ , we have the | — |
| `FC09-C06-U020` | Proposition (2.1) | Chapter VI, §2, source L15725 | (2.1) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a Galois extension with Galois group $G$ , then $$ I _ {L} ^ {G} = I _ {K}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U021` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §2, source L15777 | Every $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}} \in L_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ defines an automorphism $$ \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}: L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \longrightarrow L _ {\mathfrak {p}}, \quad x \longmapsto \alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}} x, $$ of the $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ -vector space $L_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , and as in the case of a field extension, we define the norm of $\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}$ by $$ N _ {L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}) = \det (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}). $$ In this way we obtain a homomorphism $$ N _ {L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}}: L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \longrightarrow K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}. $$ It induces a norm homomorphism $$ N _ {L \| K}: I _ {L} \longrightarrow I _ {K} $$ between the idèle groups $I_{L} = \prod_{p} L_{p}^{*}$ and $I_{K} = \prod_{p} K_{p}^{*}$ . Explicitly the norm of an idèle is given by the following proposition. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U022` | Proposition (2.2) | Chapter VI, §2, source L15803 | (2.2) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a finite extension and $\alpha = (\alpha_{\mathfrak{P}}) \in I_L$ , the local components of the idèle $N_{L\|K}(\alpha)$ are given by $$ N _ {L \| K} (\alpha) _ {\mathfrak {p}} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} N _ {L _ {\mathfrak {P}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {P}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U023` | Proposition (2.3) | Chapter VI, §2, source L15817 | (2.3) Proposition. (i) For a tower of fields $K \subseteq L \subseteq M$ we have $N_{M\|K} = N_{L\|K} \circ N_{M\|L}$ . (ii) If $L\|K$ is embedded into the Galois extension $M\|K$ and if $G = G(M\|K)$ and $H = G(M\|L)$ , then one has for $\alpha \in I_L: N_{L\|K}(\alpha) = \prod_{\sigma \in G / H} \sigma \alpha$ . (iii) $N_{L\|K}(\alpha) = \alpha^{[L:K]}$ for $\alpha \in I_K$ . (iv) The norm of the principal idèle $x \in L^*$ is the principal idèle of $K$ defined by the usual norm $N_{L\|K}(x)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U024` | Proposition (2.4) | Chapter VI, §2, source L15857 | (2.4) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a finite extension, then the homomorphism $I_K \to I_L$ induces an injection of idèle class groups $$ C _ {K} \longrightarrow C _ {L}, \quad \alpha K ^ {*} \longmapsto \alpha L ^ {*}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U025` | Proposition (2.5) | Chapter VI, §2, source L15871 | (2.5) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a Galois extension and $G = G(L\|K)$ , then $C_L$ is canonically a $G$ -module and $C_L^G = C_K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U026` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §3, source L15947 | Let $L\|K$ be a finite Galois extension with Galois group G. The G-module $I_{L}$ may be described in the following simple manner, which immediately reduces us to local fields. For every place p of K we put $$ L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} L _ {\mathfrak {P}} ^ {*} \quad \text { and } \quad U _ {L, \mathfrak {p}} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {P} \| \mathfrak {p}} U _ {\mathfrak {P}}. $$ Since the automorphisms $\sigma \in G$ permute the places of $L$ above $\mathfrak{p}$ , the groups $L_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ and $U_{L,\mathfrak{p}}$ are $G$ -modules, and we have for the $G$ -module $I_L$ the decomposition $$ I _ {L} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}, $$ where the restricted product is taken with respect to the subgroups $U_{L,p} \subseteq L_{p}^{*}$ . Choose a place P of L above p, and let $G_{\mathfrak{P}} = G(L_{\mathfrak{P}} \| K_{\mathfrak{p}}) \subseteq G$ be its decomposition group. As $\sigma$ varies over a system of representatives of $G / G_{P}$ , $\sigma P$ runs through the various places of L above p, and we get $$ L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = \prod_ {\sigma} L _ {\sigma \mathfrak {P}} ^ {*} = \prod_ {\sigma} \sigma (L _ {\mathfrak {P}} ^ {*}), U _ {L, \mathfrak {p}} = \prod_ {\sigma} U _ {\sigma \mathfrak {P}} = \prod_ {\sigma} \sigma (U _ {\mathfrak {P}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U027` | Proposition (3.1) | Chapter VI, §3, source L15967 | (3.1) Proposition. $L_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ and $U_{L,\mathfrak{p}}$ are the induced $G$ -modules $$ L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {G _ {\mathfrak {P}}} (L _ {\mathfrak {P}} ^ {*}), \quad U _ {L, \mathfrak {p}} = \operatorname{Ind} _ {G} ^ {G _ {\mathfrak {P}}} (U _ {\mathfrak {P}}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U028` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §3, source L15973 | Now let S be a finite set of places of K containing the infinite places. We then define $I_{L}^{S} = I_{L}^{\overline{S}}$ , where $\overline{S}$ denotes the set of all places of L which lie above the places of S. For $I_{L}^{S}$ we have the G-module decomposition $$ I _ {L} ^ {S} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \not \in S} U _ {L, \mathfrak {p}}, $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U029` | Proposition (3.2) | Chapter VI, §3, source L15981 | (3.2) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a cyclic extension, and if $S$ contains all primes ramified in $L$ , then we have for $i = 0, -1$ that $$ H ^ {i} (G, I _ {L} ^ {S}) \cong \bigoplus_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} H ^ {i} (G _ {\mathfrak {P}}, L _ {\mathfrak {P}} ^ {*}) \quad a n d \quad H ^ {i} (G, I _ {L}) \cong \bigoplus_ {\mathfrak {p}} H ^ {i} (G _ {\mathfrak {P}}, L _ {\mathfrak {P}} ^ {*}), $$ where for each p, P is a chosen prime of L above p. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U030` | Proposition (3.3) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16013 | (3.3) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is a cyclic extension and if $S$ contains all ramified primes, then $$ h (G, I _ {L} ^ {S}) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} n _ {\mathfrak {p}}, $$ where $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = [L_{\mathfrak{P}}:K_{\mathfrak{p}}]$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U031` | Lemma (3.4) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16035 | (3.4) Lemma. Let V be an s-dimensional R-vector space, and let G be a finite group of automorphisms of V which operates as a permutation group on the elements of a basis $v_{1}, \ldots, v_{s}$ : $\sigma v_{i} = v_{\sigma(i)}$ . If $\Gamma$ is a $G$ -invariant complete lattice in $V$ , i.e., $\sigma \Gamma \subseteq \Gamma$ for all $\sigma$ , then there exists a complete sublattice in $\Gamma$ , $$ \Gamma^ {\prime} = \mathbb {Z} w _ {1} + \dots + \mathbb {Z} w _ {s}, $$ such that $\sigma w_{i} = w_{\sigma (i)}$ for all $\sigma \in G$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U032` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §3, source L16101 | Now let L\|K be a cyclic extension of degree n with Galois group $G = G(L\|K)$ , let S be a finite set of places containing the infinite places, and let $\bar{S}$ be the set of places of L that lie above the places of S. We denote the group $L^{\bar{S}}$ of $\bar{S}$ -units simply by $L^{S}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U033` | Proposition (3.5) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16103 | (3.5) Proposition. The Herbrand quotient of the $G$ -module $L^S$ satisfies $$ h (G, L ^ {S}) = \frac {1}{n} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} n _ {\mathfrak {p}}, $$ where $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = [L_{\mathfrak{P}}:K_{\mathfrak{p}}]$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U034` | Theorem (3.6) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16175 | (3.6) Theorem. If $L\|K$ is a cyclic extension of degree $n$ with Galois group $G = G(L\|K)$ , then $$ h (G, C _ {L}) = \frac {\# H ^ {0} (G , C _ {L})}{\# H ^ {- 1} (G , C _ {L})} = n. $$ In particular $(C_K:N_{L\|K}C_L)\geq n$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U035` | Corollary (3.7) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16185 | (3.7) Corollary. If $L\|K$ is cyclic of prime power degree $n = p^{\nu} (\nu > 0)$ , then there are infinitely many places of $K$ which do not split in $L$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U036` | Corollary (3.8) | Chapter VI, §3, source L16197 | (3.8) Corollary. Let $L\|K$ be a finite extension of algebraic number fields. If almost all primes of $K$ split completely in $L$ , then $L = K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U037` | Proposition (4.1) | Chapter VI, §4, source L16223 | (4.1) Proposition. One has $s \geq r$ , and there exists a set $T$ of $s - r$ primes of $K$ that do not belong to $S$ such that $$ L = K (\sqrt [ n ]{\Delta}), $$ where $\Delta$ is the kernel of the map $K^S \to \prod_{\mathfrak{p} \in T} K_{\mathfrak{p}}^* / K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*n}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U038` | Theorem (4.2) | Chapter VI, §4, source L16251 | (4.2) Theorem. Let $T$ be a set of places as in (4.1), and let $$ C _ {K} (S, T) = I _ {K} (S, T) K ^ {*} / K ^ {*}, $$ where $$ I _ {K} (S, T) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in S} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {* n} \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \in T} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \times \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \not \in S \cup T} U _ {\mathfrak {p}}. $$ Then one has $$ N _ {L \| K} C _ {L} \supseteq C _ {K} (S, T) \quad \text { and } \quad \left(C _ {K}: C _ {K} (S, T)\right) = [ L: K ]. $$ In particular, if $L\|K$ is cyclic, then $N_{L\|K}C_L = C_K(S,T)$ . | `FC09-C06-U037` |
| `FC09-C06-U039` | Lemma (4.3) | Chapter VI, §4, source L16275 | (4.3) Lemma. $I_K(S, T) \cap K^* = (K^{S \cup T})^n$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U040` | Theorem (4.4) | Chapter VI, §4, source L16333 | (4.4) Theorem (Global Class Field Axiom). If $L\|K$ is a cyclic extension of algebraic number fields, then $$ \# H ^ {i} \big (G (L \| K), C _ {L} \big) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} [ L: K ] & \text {for i = 0 ,} \\ 1 & \text {for i = -1 .} \end{array} \right. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U041` | Corollary (4.5) | Chapter VI, §4, source L16371 | (4.5) Corollary. Let $L\|K$ be a cyclic extension. An element $x \in K^*$ is a norm if and only if it is a norm locally everywhere, i.e., a norm in every completion $L_{\mathfrak{P}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{P}\|\mathfrak{p})$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U042` | Proposition (5.1) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16431 | (5.1) Proposition. Let $\Omega\|\mathbb{Q}$ be the field obtained by adjoining all roots of unity, and let $T$ be the torsion subgroup of $G(\Omega\|K)$ (i.e., the group of all elements of finite order). Then the fixed field $\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q}$ of $T$ is a $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ -extension. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U043` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §5, source L16461 | We fix an isomorphism $G(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q}) \cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ . There is no canonical choice as in the case of local fields. However, the reciprocity law will not depend on the choice. Via $G(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q}) \cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ , we obtain a continuous surjective homomorphism $$ d: G _ {\mathbb {Q}} \longrightarrow \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ of the absolute Galois group $G_{\mathbb{Q}} = G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\|\mathbb{Q})$ . With this we continue as in chap. IV, §4, choosing $k = \mathbb{Q}$ as our base field. If $K\|\mathbb{Q}$ is a finite extension, then we put $f_K = [K \cap \widetilde{\mathbb{Q}} : \mathbb{Q}]$ and get a surjective homomorphism $$ d _ {K} = \frac {1}{f _ {K}} d: G _ {K} \longrightarrow \widehat {\mathbb {Z}}, $$ which defines the $\widehat{Z}$ -extension $\widetilde{K}=K\widetilde{Q}$ of K. $\widetilde{K}\|K$ is called the cyclotomic $\widehat{Z}$ -extension of K. We denote again by $\varphi_{K}$ the element of $G(\widetilde{K}\|K)$ which is mapped to 1 by the isomorphism $G(\widetilde{K}\|K) \cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ , and by $\varphi_{L\|K}$ the restriction $\varphi_K\|_L$ if $L\|K$ is a subextension of $\widetilde{K}\|K$ . The automorphism $\varphi_{L\|K}$ must not be confused with the Frobenius automorphism corresponding to a prime ideal of $L$ (see §7). | — |
| `FC09-C06-U044` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §5, source L16483 | For an arbitrary finite abelian extension $L\|K$ , we define the homomorphism $$ [ \mathrm{,} L \| K ]: I _ {K} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) $$ by $$ [ \alpha , L \| K ] = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}, L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}), $$ where $L_{p}$ denotes the completion of L with respect to a place $P\|p$ , and $(\alpha_{p}, L_{p}\|K_{p})$ is the norm residue symbol of local class field theory. Note that almost all factors in the product are 1 because almost all extensions $L_{p}\|K_{p}$ are unramified and almost all $\alpha_{p}$ are units. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U045` | Proposition (5.2) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16497 | (5.2) Proposition. If $L\|K$ and $L'\|K'$ are two abelian extensions of finite algebraic number fields such that $K \subseteq K'$ and $L \subseteq L'$ , then we have the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c} I _ {K ^ {\prime}} \xrightarrow {[ , L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime} ]} G (L ^ {\prime} \| K ^ {\prime}) \\ N _ {K ^ {\prime} \| K} \Big \downarrow \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \Big \downarrow \\ I _ {K} \xrightarrow {[ , L \| K ]} G (L \| K). \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U046` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §5, source L16515 | If $L\|K$ is an abelian extension of infinite degree, then we define the homomorphism $$ [ \quad , L \| K ]: I _ {K} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) $$ by its restrictions $[, L\|K]_{L'} := [, L'\|K]$ to the finite subextensions $L'$ of $L\|K$ . In other words, if $\alpha \in I_K$ , then the elements $[\alpha, L'\|K]$ define, by (5.2), an element of the projective limit $\varprojlim_{L'} G(L'\|K)$ , and $[\alpha, L\|K]$ is precisely this element, once we identify $G(L\|K) = \varprojlim G(L'\|K)$ . Again one has the equation $$ [ \alpha , L \| K ] = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}, L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}), $$ where $L_{\mathfrak{p}}$ does not denote the completion of $L$ with respect to a place above $\mathfrak{p}$ , but rather the localization, i.e., the union of the completions $L_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ of all finite subextensions (see chap. II, §8). Then $L_{\mathfrak{p}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is Galois, $G(L_{\mathfrak{p}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}) \subseteq G(L\|K)$ , and the product $\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}, L_{\mathfrak{p}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}})$ converges in the profinite group to the element $[\alpha, L\|K]$ . Indeed, if $L^{\prime}\|K$ varies over the finite subextensions of $L\|K$ , then the sets $S_{L^{\prime}} = \{\mathfrak{p} \| (\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}, L_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}) \neq 1\}$ are all finite, so that we may write down the finite products | `FC09-C06-U045` |
| `FC09-C06-U047` | Proposition (5.3) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16551 | (5.3) Proposition. For every root of unity $\zeta$ and every principal idèle $a \in K^{*}$ one has $$ [ a, K (\zeta) \| K ] = 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U048` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §5, source L16579 | The homomorphism $[ \quad, \widetilde{K} \| K] : I_K \to G(\widetilde{K} \| K)$ therefore induces a homomorphism $$ [ \quad , \widetilde {K} \| K ]: C _ {K} \longrightarrow G (\widetilde {K} \| K), $$ and we consider its composite $$ v _ {K}: C _ {K} \longrightarrow \widehat {\mathbb {Z}} $$ with $d_K: G(\widetilde{K}\|K) \to \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ . The pair $(d_K, v_K)$ is then a class field theory, for we have the | — |
| `FC09-C06-U049` | Proposition (5.4) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16593 | (5.4) Proposition. The map $v_{K}: C_{K} \to \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ is surjective and is a henselian valuation with respect to $d_{K}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U050` | Theorem (5.5) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16631 | (5.5) Theorem. For every Galois extension $L\|K$ of finite algebraic number fields we have a canonical isomorphism $$ r _ {L \| K}: G (L \| K) ^ {a b} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} C _ {K} / N _ {L \| K} C _ {L}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U051` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §5, source L16637 | The inverse map of $r_{L\|K}$ yields a surjective homomorphism $$ (\quad , L \| K): C _ {K} \rightarrow G (L \| K) ^ {a b} $$ with kernel $N_{L\|K}C_{L}$ . The map $(,L\|K)$ is called the global norm residue symbol. We view it also as a homomorphism $I_{K} \to G(L\|K)^{ab}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U052` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §5, source L16645 | For every place $\mathfrak{p}$ of $K$ , we have on the one hand the embedding $G(L_{\mathfrak{p}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}) \hookrightarrow G(L\|K)$ , and on the other the canonical injection $$ \langle \quad \rangle : K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \rightarrow C _ {K}, $$ which sends $a_{\mathfrak{p}} \in K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}$ to the class of the idèle $$ \langle a _ {\mathfrak {p}} \rangle = (\dots , 1, 1, 1, a _ {\mathfrak {p}}, 1, 1, 1, \dots). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U053` | Proposition (5.6) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16659 | (5.6) Proposition. If $L\|K$ is an abelian extension and $\mathfrak{p}$ is a place of $K$ , then the diagram $$ \begin{array}{c} K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}})} G (L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}) \\ \langle \quad \rangle \Biggl \downarrow \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \Biggl \downarrow \\ C _ {K} \xrightarrow {(\quad , L \| K)} G (L \| K) \end{array} $$ is commutative. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U054` | Corollary (5.7) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16711 | (5.7) Corollary. If $L\|K$ is an abelian extension and $\alpha = (\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}) \in I_K$ , then $$ (\alpha , L \| K) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} (\alpha_ {\mathfrak {p}}, L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}). $$ In particular, for a principal idèle $a \in K^{*}$ we have the product formula $$ \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} (a, L _ {\mathfrak {p}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}) = 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U055` | Corollary (5.8) | Chapter VI, §5, source L16733 | (5.8) Corollary. For every finite abelian extension one has $$ N C _ {L} \cap K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = N _ {\mathfrak {p}} L _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U056` | Theorem (6.1) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16779 | (6.1) Theorem. The map $$ L \longmapsto \mathcal {N} _ {L} = N _ {L \| K} C _ {L} $$ is a 1-1-correspondence between the finite abelian extensions $L\|K$ and the closed subgroups of finite index in $C_K$ . Moreover one has: $$ L _ {1} \subseteq L _ {2} \Longleftrightarrow \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \supseteq \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \cap \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}, \quad \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1} \cap L _ {2}} = \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {1}} \mathcal {N} _ {L _ {2}}. $$ The field $L\|K$ corresponding to the subgroup $\mathcal{N}$ of $C_K$ is called the class field of $\mathcal{N}$ . It satisfies $$ G (L \| K) \cong C _ {K} / \mathcal {N}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U057` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §6, source L16829 | The above theorem is called the “existence theorem” of global class field theory because its main assertion is the existence, for any given closed subgroup N of finite index in $C_{K}$ , of an abelian extension $L\|K$ such that $N_{L\|K}C_{L} = N$ . This extension L is the class field for N. The existence theorem gives a clear overview of all the abelian extensions of K once we bring in the congruence subgroups $C_{K}^{m}$ of $C_{K}$ corresponding to the modules $m = \prod_{p \nmid \infty} p^{n_{p}}$ (see (1.7)). They are closed of finite index by (1.8), and they prompt the following definition. | `FC09-C06-U013`, `FC09-C06-U014` |
| `FC09-C06-U058` | Definition (6.2) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16831 | (6.2) Definition. The class field $K^{m}\|K$ for the congruence subgroup $C_{K}^{m}$ is called the ray class field mod m. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U059` | Corollary (6.3) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16847 | (6.3) Corollary. Every finite abelian extension $L\|K$ is contained in a ray class field $K^{\mathfrak{m}}\|K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U060` | Definition (6.4) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16849 | (6.4) Definition. Let $L\|K$ be a finite abelian extension, and let $\mathcal{N}_L = N_{L\|K}C_L$ . The conductor $\mathfrak{f}$ of $L\|K$ (or of $\mathcal{N}_L$ ) is the gcd of all modules $\mathfrak{m}$ such that $L \subseteq K^{\mathfrak{m}}$ (i.e., $C_K^{\mathfrak{m}} \subseteq \mathcal{N}_L$ ). | — |

For an infinite place $\mathfrak{p}$ we define $\mathfrak{f}_{\mathfrak{p}} = 1$ . Then we view $\mathfrak{f}$ as the replete ideal $\mathfrak{f} \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\|\infty} \mathfrak{p}^{0}$ and obtain the | `FC09-C05-U009`, `FC09-C06-U011` |
| `FC09-C06-U062` | Proposition (6.5) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16853 | (6.5) Proposition. If $\mathfrak{f}$ is the conductor of the abelian extension $L\|K$ , and $\mathfrak{f}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is the conductor of the local extension $L_{\mathfrak{p}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , then $$ f = \prod_ {p} f _ {p}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U063` | Corollary (6.6) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16879 | (6.6) Corollary. Let $L\|K$ be a finite abelian extension and $f$ its conductor. Then: $$ \mathfrak {p} \text { is ramified in } L \iff \mathfrak {p} \| \mathfrak {f}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U064` | Proposition (6.7) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16887 | (6.7) Proposition. Let $m$ be a natural number and $\mathfrak{m} = (m)$ . Then the ray class field mod $\mathfrak{m}$ of $\mathbb{Q}$ is the field $$ \mathbb {Q} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = \mathbb {Q} (\mu_ {m}) $$ of $m$ -th roots of unity. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U065` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §6, source L16907 | Among all abelian extensions of K, the ray class field mod 1 occupies a special place. It is called the big Hilbert class field and has Galois group $$ G (K ^ {1} \| K) \cong C l _ {K} ^ {1}. $$ By (1.11), the group $Cl_K^1$ is linked to the ordinary ideal class group by the exact sequence $$ 1 \longrightarrow \mathcal {O} ^ {*} / \mathcal {O} _ {+} ^ {*} \longrightarrow \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \text { real }} \mathbb {R} ^ {*} / \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} ^ {1} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} \longrightarrow 1. $$ The big Hilbert class field has conductor f = 1 and may therefore be characterized by (6.6) in the following way. | `FC09-C06-U018`, `FC09-C06-U063` |
| `FC09-C06-U066` | Proposition (6.8) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16921 | (6.8) Proposition. The big Hilbert class field is the maximal unramified abelian extension of K. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U067` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §6, source L16923 | Since the infinite places are always unramified, this means that all prime ideals are unramified. The Hilbert class field, or more precisely, the “small Hilbert class field”, is defined to be the maximal unramified abelian extension $H\|K$ in which all infinite places split completely, i.e., the real places stay real. It satisfies the | — |
| `FC09-C06-U068` | Proposition (6.9) | Chapter VI, §6, source L16925 | (6.9) Proposition. The Galois group of the small Hilbert class field $H\|K$ is canonically isomorphic to the ideal class group: $$ G (H \| K) \cong C l _ {K}. $$ In particular, the degree $[H : K]$ is the class number $h_{K}$ of K. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U069` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §6, source L16951 | Remark: The small Hilbert class field is in general not a ray class field in terms of the theory developed here. But it is in many other textbooks where ray class groups and ray class fields are defined differently (see for instance [107]). This other theory is obtained by equipping all number fields with the Minkowski metric $$ \langle x, y \rangle_ {K} = \sum_ {\tau} \alpha_ {\tau} x _ {\tau} \overline {{{{y}}}} _ {\tau} \quad \left(\tau \in \operatorname{Hom} (K, \mathbb {C})\right), $$ $\alpha_{\tau} = 1$ if $\tau = \overline{\tau}$ , $\alpha_{\tau} = \frac{1}{2}$ if $\tau \neq \overline{\tau}$ . A ray class group can then be attached to any replete module $$ \mathfrak {m} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathfrak {p} ^ {n _ {\mathfrak {p}}}, $$ where $n_{\mathfrak{p}} \in \mathbb{Z}$ , $n_{\mathfrak{p}} \geq 0$ , and $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0$ or $= 1$ if $\mathfrak{p} \| \infty$ . The groups $U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(n_{\mathfrak{p}})}$ attached to the metrized number field $(K, \langle , \rangle_K)$ are defined by $$ U _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {(n _ {\mathfrak {p}})} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 + \mathfrak {p} ^ {n _ {\mathfrak {p}}}, & \text { for } n _ {\mathfrak {p}} > 0, \text { and } U _ {\mathfrak {p}} \text { for } n _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 0, \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \nmid \infty , \\ \mathbb {R} ^ {*}, & \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \text { is real and } n _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 0, \\ \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, & \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \text { is real and } n _ {\mathfrak {p}} = 1, \\ \mathbb {C} ^ {*} = K _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}, & \text { if } \mathfrak {p} \text { is complex. } \end{array} \right. $$ The congruence subgroup mod $\mathfrak{m}$ of $(K, \langle , \rangle_K)$ is then the subgroup $C_K^{\mathfrak{m}} = I_K^{\mathfrak{m}}K^* / K^*$ of $C_K$ formed with the group $$ I _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} U _ {\mathfrak {p}} ^ {(n _ {\mathfrak {p}})}, $$ and the factor group $C_K / C_K^m$ is the ray class group mod m. The ray class field mod m of $(K, \langle , \rangle_K)$ is again the class field of $K$ corresponding to the group $C_K^m \subseteq C_K$ . As explained in chap. III, §3, the infinite places p have to be considered as ramified in an extension $L\|K$ if $L_{\mathfrak{p}} \neq K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ . Likewise, the conductor of an abelian extension $L\|K$ , i.e., the gcd of all modules $\mathfrak{m} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}^{n_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ such that $C_K^{\mathfrak{m}} \subseteq N_{L\|K}C_L$ , is the replete ideal $$ f = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} f _ {\mathfrak {p}}, $$ where now for an infinite place $\mathfrak{p}$ , we have $\mathfrak{f}_{\mathfrak{p}} = \mathfrak{p}^{n_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ with $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0$ if $L_{\mathfrak{p}} = K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , and $n_{\mathfrak{p}} = 1$ if $L_{\mathfrak{p}} \neq K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ . Corollary (6.6) then continues to hold: a place $\mathfrak{p}$ is ramified in $L$ if and only if $\mathfrak{p}$ occurs in the conductor $\mathfrak{f}$ . This entails the following modifications of the above theory, as far as ray class fields are concerned. The ray class field mod 1 is the small Hilbert class field. It is now the maximal abelian extension of K which is unramified at all places. The big Hilbert class field is the ray class field for the module $m = \prod_{p\|_{\infty}} p$ . In the case of the base field Q, the field $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta)$ of m-th roots of unity is the ray class field mod $mp_{\infty}$ , where $p_{\infty}$ is the infinite place. The ray class field for the module m becomes the maximal real subextension $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta + \zeta^{-1})$ , which was not a ray class field before. This is the theory one finds in the textbooks alluded to above. It corresponds to the number fields with the Minkowski metric. The theory of ray class fields according to the treatment of this book is forced upon us already by the choice of the standard metric $\langle x, y \rangle = \sum_{\tau} x_{\tau} \bar{y}_{\tau}$ on $K_{R}$ taken in chap. I, §5. It is compatible with the Riemann-Roch theory of chap. III, and has the advantage of being simpler. | `FC09-C03-U111`, `FC09-C06-U063` |
| `FC09-C06-U070` | Theorem (6.10) | Chapter VI, §6, source L17023 | (6.10) Theorem. Let $K$ be an imaginary quadratic number field and $\mathfrak{a}$ an ideal of $\mathcal{O}_K$ . Then one has: (i) The $j$ -invariant $j(\mathfrak{a})$ of $\mathbb{C} / \mathfrak{a}$ is an algebraic integer which depends only on the ideal class $\mathfrak{K}$ of $\mathfrak{a}$ , and will therefore be denoted by $j(\mathfrak{K})$ . (ii) Every $j(\mathfrak{a})$ generates the Hilbert class field over $K$ . (iii) If $\mathfrak{a}_1, \ldots, \mathfrak{a}_h$ are representatives of the ideal class group $Cl_K$ , then the numbers $j(\mathfrak{a}_i)$ are conjugate to one another over $K$ . (iv) For almost all prime ideals $\mathfrak{p}$ of $K$ one has $$ \varphi_ {\mathfrak {p}} j (\mathfrak {a}) = j \left(\mathfrak {p} ^ {- 1} \mathfrak {a}\right), $$ where $\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}} \in G(K(j(\mathfrak{a}))\|K)$ is the Frobenius automorphism of a prime ideal $\mathfrak{P}$ of $K(j(\mathfrak{a}))$ above $\mathfrak{p}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U071` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §6, source L17039 | Note that for a totally imaginary field K there is no difference between big and small Hilbert class field. In order to go beyond the Hilbert class field, i.e., the ray class field mod 1, to the ray class fields for arbitrary modules $m \neq 1$ , we form, for any lattice $\Gamma \subseteq C$ , the Weber function $$ \tau_ {\Gamma} (z) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} - 2 ^ {7} 3 ^ {5} \frac {g _ {2} g _ {3}}{\varDelta} \wp_ {\Gamma} (z), & \mathrm{if} g _ {2} g _ {3} \neq 0, \\ - 2 ^ {9} 3 ^ {6} \frac {g _ {3}}{\varDelta} \wp_ {\Gamma} ^ {3} (z), & \mathrm{if} g _ {2} = 0, \\ 2 ^ {8} 3 ^ {4} \frac {g _ {2} ^ {2}}{\varDelta} \wp_ {\Gamma} ^ {2} (z), & \mathrm{if} g _ {3} = 0. \end{array} \right. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U072` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §6, source L17045 | Let $\mathfrak{K} \in Cl_K$ be an ideal class chosen once and for all. We denote by $\mathfrak{K}^*$ the classes in the ray class group $Cl_K^\mathfrak{m} = J_K^\mathfrak{m} / P_K^\mathfrak{m}$ which under the homomorphism $$ C l _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} $$ are sent to the ideal class $(\mathfrak{m})\mathfrak{K}^{-1}$ . Let $\mathfrak{a}$ be an ideal in $\mathfrak{K}$ , and let $\mathfrak{b}$ be an integral ideal in $\mathfrak{K}^*$ . Then $\mathfrak{abm}^{-1} = (a)$ is a principal ideal. The value $\tau_{\mathfrak{a}}(a)$ only depends on the class $\mathfrak{K}^*$ , not on the choice of $\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}$ and $a$ . It will be denoted by $$ \tau (\mathfrak {K} ^ {*}) = \tau_ {a} (a). $$ With these conventions we then have the | — |
| `FC09-C06-U073` | Theorem (6.11) | Chapter VI, §6, source L17059 | (6.11) Theorem. (i) The invariants $\tau(\mathfrak{K}_1^*)$ , $\tau(\mathfrak{K}_2^*)$ , ..., for a fixed ideal class $\mathfrak{K}$ , are distinct algebraic numbers which are conjugate over the Hilbert class field $K^1 = K(j(\mathfrak{K}))$ . (ii) For an arbitrary $\mathfrak{K}^*$ , the field $K(j(\mathfrak{K}),\tau (\mathfrak{K}^{*}))$ is the ray class field mod $\mathfrak{m}$ over $K$ : $$ K ^ {\mathfrak {m}} = K \left(j (\mathfrak {K}), \tau (\mathfrak {K} ^ {*})\right). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U074` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §7, source L17155 | Let $L\|K$ be an abelian extension, and let $\mathfrak{p}$ be an unramified prime ideal of $K$ and $\mathfrak{P}$ a prime ideal of $L$ lying above $\mathfrak{p}$ . The decomposition group $G(L_{\mathfrak{P}}\|K_{\mathfrak{p}}) \subseteq G(L\|K)$ is then generated by the classical Frobenius automorphism $$ \varphi_ {\mathfrak {p}} = (\pi_ {\mathfrak {p}}, L _ {\mathfrak {P}} \| K _ {\mathfrak {p}}), $$ where $\pi_{p}$ is a prime element of $K_{p}$ . As an automorphism of L, $\varphi_{p}$ is obviously characterized by the congruence $$ \varphi_ {\mathfrak {p}} a \equiv a ^ {q} \bmod \mathfrak {P} \quad \text {for all} \quad a \in \mathcal {O} _ {L} $$ where q is the number of elements in the residue class field of p. We put $$ \varphi_ {\mathfrak {p}} =: \left(\frac {L \| K}{\mathfrak {p}}\right). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U075` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §7, source L17173 | Now let m be a module of K such that L lies in the ray class field mod m. Such a module is called an module of definition for L. Since by (6.6) each prime ideal $p \nmid m$ is unramified in L, we get a canonical homomorphism $$ \left(\frac {L \| K}{\text { }}\right): J _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) $$ from the group $J_K^{\mathfrak{m}}$ of all ideals of $K$ which are relatively prime to $\mathfrak{m}$ by putting, for any ideal $\mathfrak{a} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ : $$ \left(\frac {L \| K}{\mathfrak {a}}\right) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \left(\frac {L \| K}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}. $$ $\left(\frac{L\|K}{a}\right)$ is called the Artin symbol. If $\mathfrak{p} \in J_K^\mathfrak{m}$ is a prime ideal and $\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}$ a prime element of $K_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , then clearly $$ \left(\frac {L \| K}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = \left(\langle \pi_ {\mathfrak {p}} \rangle , L \| K\right), $$ if $\langle \pi_{\mathfrak{p}}\rangle \in C_K$ denotes the class of the idèle ( $\ldots, 1, 1, \pi_{\mathfrak{p}}, 1, 1, \ldots$ ). | `FC09-C06-U063` |
| `FC09-C06-U076` | Theorem (7.1) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17195 | (7.1) Theorem. Let $L\|K$ be an abelian extension, and let $\mathfrak{m}$ be a module of definition for it. Then the Artin symbol induces a surjective homomorphism $$ \left(\frac {L \| K}{\text { }}\right): C l _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \longrightarrow G (L \| K) $$ with kernel $H^{\mathfrak{m}} / P_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}$ , where $H^{\mathfrak{m}} = (N_{L\|K}J_{L}^{\mathfrak{m}})P_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}$ , and we have an exact commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c} 1 \longrightarrow N _ {L \| K} C _ {L} \longrightarrow C _ {K} \xrightarrow {(\text { , } L \| K)} G (L \| K) \longrightarrow 1 \\ \Big \downarrow \qquad \qquad \qquad \Big \downarrow \qquad \qquad \qquad \Big \downarrow_ {\mathrm{id}} \\ 1 \longrightarrow H ^ {\mathfrak {m}} / P _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \longrightarrow C l _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \xrightarrow {\left(\underline {{L \| K}}\right)} G (L \| K) \longrightarrow 1. \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U077` | Corollary (7.2) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17243 | (7.2) Corollary. The Artin symbol $\left(\frac{L\|K}{\mathfrak{a}}\right)$ , for $\mathfrak{a} \in J_K^\mathfrak{m}$ , only depends on the class $\mathfrak{a} \bmod P_K^\mathfrak{m}$ . It defines an isomorphism $$ \left(\frac {L \| K}{\text { }}\right): J _ {K} ^ {\mathfrak {m}} / H ^ {\mathfrak {m}} \xrightarrow {\sim} G (L \| K). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U078` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §7, source L17249 | The group $H^{\mathfrak{m}} = (N_{L\|K} J_{L}^{\mathfrak{m}}) P_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}$ is called the “ideal group defined mod m” belonging to the extension L\|K. From the existence theorem (6.1), we see that the correspondence $L \mapsto H^{m}$ is 1–1 between subextensions of the ray class field mod m and subgroups of $J_{K}^{m}$ containing $P_{K}^{m}$ . | `FC09-C06-U056` |
| `FC09-C06-U079` | Theorem (7.3) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17253 | (7.3) Theorem (Decomposition Law). Let $L\|K$ be an abelian extension of degree $n$ , and let $\mathfrak{p}$ be an unramified prime ideal. Let $\mathfrak{m}$ be a module of definition for $L\|K$ that is not divisible by $\mathfrak{p}$ (for instance the conductor), and let $H^{\mathfrak{m}}$ be the corresponding ideal group. If $f$ is the order of $\mathfrak{p} \mod H^{\mathfrak{m}}$ in the class group $J_K^{\mathfrak{m}} / H^{\mathfrak{m}}$ , i.e., the smallest positive integer such that $$ \mathfrak {p} ^ {f} \in H ^ {\mathfrak {m}}, $$ then $\mathfrak{p}$ decomposes in $L$ into a product $$ \mathfrak {p} = \mathfrak {P} _ {1} \dots \mathfrak {P} _ {r} $$ of $r = n / f$ distinct prime ideals of degree $f$ over $\mathfrak{p}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U080` | Corollary (7.4) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17281 | (7.4) Corollary. The prime ideals of $K$ which split completely in the Hilbert class field are precisely the principal prime ideals. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U081` | Theorem (7.5) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17285 | (7.5) Theorem. In the Hilbert class field every ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ of $K$ becomes a principal ideal. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U082` | Theorem (7.6) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17307 | (7.6) Theorem. Let $G$ be a finitely generated group, $G'$ its commutator subgroup, and $G''$ the commutator subgroup of $G'$ . If $(G: G') < \infty$ , then the transfer $$ \operatorname{Ver}: G / G ^ {\prime} \longrightarrow G ^ {\prime} / G ^ {\prime \prime} $$ is the trivial homomorphism. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U083` | Lemma (7.7) | Chapter VI, §7, source L17323 | (7.7) Lemma. For every subgroup $H$ of finite index in $G$ , one has a commutative diagram $$ \begin{array}{c c c} G / G ^ {\prime} & \xrightarrow {\text {Ver}} & H / H ^ {\prime} \\ \delta \Big \downarrow \cong & & \delta \Big \downarrow \cong \\ I _ {G} / I _ {G} ^ {2} & \xrightarrow {S} & (I _ {H} + I _ {G} I _ {H}) / I _ {G} I _ {H}, \end{array} $$ where the homomorphisms $\delta$ are induced by $\sigma\mapsto\delta\sigma=\sigma-1$ , and the homomorphism S is given by $$ S (x \bmod I _ {G} ^ {2}) = x \sum_ {\rho \in R} \rho \bmod I _ {G} I _ {H}, $$ for a system of representatives of the left cosets $R \ni 1$ of G/H. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U084` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §7, source L17441 | A problem which is closely related to the principal ideal theorem and which was first put forward by PHILIPP FURTWÄNGLER is the problem of the class field tower. This is the question whether the class field tower $$ K = K _ {0} \subseteq K _ {1} \subseteq K _ {2} \subseteq K _ {3} \subseteq \dots , $$ where $K_{i+1}$ is the Hilbert class field of $K_{i}$ , stops after a finite number of steps. A positive answer would have the implication that the last field in the tower had class number 1 so that in it not only the ideals of K, but in fact all its ideals become principal. This perspective naturally generated the greatest interest. But the problem, after withstanding for a long time all attempts to solve it, was finally decided in the negative by the Russian mathematicians E.S. GOLOD and I.R. ŠAFAREVIČ in 1964 (see [48], [24]). | — |
| `FC09-C06-U085` | Theorem (8.1) | Chapter VI, §8, source L17499 | (8.1) Theorem. For $a, b \in K^{*}$ one has $$ \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \left(\frac {a , b}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) = 1. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U086` | Definition (8.2) | Chapter VI, §8, source L17531 | (8.2) Definition. For every ideal $\mathfrak{b} = \prod_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid n}\mathfrak{p}^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}}$ prime to $n$ , and every number a prime to $\mathfrak{b}$ , we define the $n$ -th power residue symbol by $$ \left(\frac {a}{\mathfrak {b}}\right) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \nmid n} \left(\frac {a}{\mathfrak {p}}\right) ^ {\nu_ {\mathfrak {p}}}. $$ Here $\left(\frac{a}{\mathfrak{p}}\right)^{\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}} = 1$ when $\nu_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0$ . | — |
| `FC09-C06-U087` | Convention / notation | Chapter VI, §8, source L17539 | The power residue symbol $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)$ is obviously multiplicative in both arguments. If b is a principal ideal (b), we write for short $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)=\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)$ . We now prove the general reciprocity law for the n-th power residues. | — |
| `FC09-C06-U088` | Theorem (8.3) | Chapter VI, §8, source L17541 | (8.3) Theorem. If $a, b \in K^{*}$ are prime to each other and to $n$ , then $$ \left(\frac {a}{b}\right) \left(\frac {b}{a}\right) ^ {- 1} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p} \| n \infty} \left(\frac {a , b}{\mathfrak {p}}\right). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U089` | Gauss's Reciprocity Law (8.4) | Chapter VI, §8, source L17569 | (8.4) Gauss's Reciprocity Law. Let $K = \mathbb{Q}$ , $n = 2$ , and let $a$ and $b$ be odd, relatively prime integers. Then one has $$ \left(\frac {a}{b}\right) \left(\frac {b}{a}\right) = (- 1) ^ {\frac {a - 1}{2} \frac {b - 1}{2}} (- 1) ^ {\frac {\operatorname{sgn} a - 1}{2} \frac {\operatorname{sgn} b - 1}{2}}, $$ and for positive odd integers b, we have the two “supplementary theorems” $$ \left(\frac {- 1}{b}\right) = (- 1) ^ {\frac {b - 1}{2}}, \quad \left(\frac {2}{b}\right) = (- 1) ^ {\frac {b ^ {2} - 1}{8}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C06-U090` | Definition / construction | Chapter VI, §8, source L17587 | The symbol $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)$ is called the Jacobi symbol, or also the quadratic residue symbol (although, for b not a prime number, the condition that the symbol $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)=1$ is no longer equivalent to the condition that a is a quadratic residue modulo b). | — |
| `FC09-C07-U001` | Proposition (1.1) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17609 | (1.1) Proposition. The series $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{n^s}$ is absolutely and uniformly convergent in the domain $\operatorname{Re}(s) \geq 1 + \delta$ , for every $\delta > 0$ . It therefore represents an analytic function in the half-plane $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$ . One has Euler's identity $$ \zeta (s) = \prod_ {p} \frac {1}{1 - p ^ {- s}}, $$ where p runs through the prime numbers. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U002` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L17669 | Euler's identity expresses the law of unique prime factorization of natural numbers in a single equation. This already demonstrates the number-theoretic significance of the zeta function. It challenges us to study its properties more closely. By its definition, the function is only given on the half-plane $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$ . It does, however, admit an analytic continuation to the whole complex plane, with the point $s = 1$ removed, and it satisfies a functional equation which relates the argument $s$ to the argument $1 - s$ . These crucial facts will be proved next. The proof hinges on an integral formula for the zeta function $\zeta(s)$ which arises from the well-known gamma function. This latter is defined for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 0$ by the absolutely convergent integral $$ \Gamma (s) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {- y} y ^ {s} \frac {d y}{y} $$ and obeys the following rules (see [34], vol. I, chap. I). | — |
| `FC09-C07-U003` | Proposition (1.2) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17677 | (1.2) Proposition. (i) The gamma function is analytic and admits a meromorphic continuation to all of $\mathbb{C}$ . (ii) It is nowhere zero and has simple poles at $s = -n$ , $n = 0, 1, 2, \ldots$ , with residues $(-1)^n / n!$ . There are no poles anywhere else. (iii) It satisfies the functional equations 1) $\Gamma(s + 1) = s\Gamma(s)$ , 2) $\Gamma(s)\Gamma(1 - s) = \frac{\pi}{\sin\pi s},$ 3) $\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s + \frac{1}{2}) = \frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{2^{2s}}\Gamma(2s)$ (Legendre's duplication formula). (iv) It has the special values $\Gamma(1/2) = \sqrt{\pi}$ , $\Gamma(1) = 1$ , $\Gamma(k+1) = k!$ , $k = 0, 1, 2, \ldots$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U004` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L17721 | The function $$ Z (s) = \pi^ {- s / 2} \Gamma (s / 2) \zeta (s) $$ is called the completed zeta function. We obtain the | — |
| `FC09-C07-U005` | Proposition (1.3) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17729 | (1.3) Proposition. The completed zeta function $Z(s)$ admits the integral representation $$ Z (s) = \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} (\theta (i y) - 1) y ^ {s / 2} \frac {d y}{y}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U006` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L17735 | The proof of the functional equation for the function $Z(s)$ is based on the following general principle. For a continuous function $f: R_{+}^{*} \to C$ on the group $R_{+}^{*}$ of positive real numbers, we define the Mellin transform to be the improper integral $$ L (f, s) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} (f (y) - f (\infty)) y ^ {s} \frac {d y}{y}, $$ provided the limit $f(\infty) = \lim_{y \to \infty} f(y)$ and the integral exist. The following theorem is of pivotal importance, also for later applications. We will often refer to it as the Mellin principle. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U007` | Theorem (1.4) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17743 | (1.4) Theorem. Let $f, g: \mathbb{R}_+^* \to \mathbb{C}$ be continuous functions such that $$ f (y) = a _ {0} + O (e ^ {- c y ^ {\alpha}}), \quad g (y) = b _ {0} + O (e ^ {- c y ^ {\alpha}}), $$ for $y \to \infty$ , with positive constants $c, \alpha$ . If these functions satisfy the equation $$ f \left(\frac {1}{y}\right) = C y ^ {k} g (y), $$ for some real number $k > 0$ and some complex number $C \neq 0$ , then one has: (i) The integrals $L(f, s)$ and $L(g, s)$ converge absolutely and uniformly if $s$ varies in an arbitrary compact domain contained in $\{s \in \mathbb{C} \mid \operatorname{Re}(s) > k\}$ . They are therefore holomorphic functions on $\{s \in \mathbb{C} \mid \operatorname{Re}(s) > k\}$ . They admit holomorphic continuations to $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{0, k\}$ . (ii) They have simple poles at $s = 0$ and $s = k$ with residues $$ \operatorname{Res} _ {s = 0} L (f, s) = - a _ {0}, \quad \operatorname{Res} _ {s = k} L (f, s) = C b _ {0}, \quad r e s p. $$ $$ \operatorname{Res} _ {s = 0} L (g, s) = - b _ {0}, \quad \operatorname{Res} _ {s = k} L (g, s) = C ^ {- 1} a _ {0}. $$ (iii) They satisfy the functional equation $$ L (f, s) = C L (g, k - s). $$ Remark 1: The symbol $\varphi(y) = O(\psi(y))$ means, as usual, that one has $\varphi(y) = c(y)\psi(y)$ , for some function $c(y)$ which stays bounded under the limit in question, so in our case, as $y \to \infty$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U008` | Proposition (1.5) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17827 | (1.5) Proposition. The series $$ \theta (z) = \sum_ {n \in \mathbb {Z}} e ^ {\pi i n ^ {2} z} $$ converges absolutely and uniformly in the domain $\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\operatorname{Im}(z)\geq\delta\}$ , for every $\delta>0$ . It therefore represents an analytic function on the upper half-plane $H=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\operatorname{Im}(z)>0\}$ , and satisfies the transformation formula $$ \theta (- 1 / z) = \sqrt {z / i} \theta (z). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U009` | Theorem (1.6) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17851 | (1.6) Theorem. The completed zeta function $$ Z (s) = \pi^ {- s / 2} \Gamma (s / 2) \zeta (s) $$ admits an analytic continuation to $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{0,1\}$ , has simple poles at $s = 0$ and $s = 1$ with residues $-1$ and $1$ , respectively, and satisfies the functional equation $$ Z (s) = Z (1 - s). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U010` | Corollary (1.7) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17901 | (1.7) Corollary. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s)$ admits an analytic continuation to $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{1\}$ , has a simple pole at $s = 1$ with residue 1 and satisfies the functional equation $$ \zeta (1 - s) = 2 (2 \pi) ^ {- s} \Gamma (s) \cos \left(\frac {\pi s}{2}\right) \zeta (s). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U011` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L17949 | These are explicit evaluations of the special values of the Riemann zeta function at the points $s = 2k$ , $k \in N$ . The phenomenon is explained via the functional equation by the fact that the values of the Riemann zeta function at the negative odd integers are given by Bernoulli numbers. These arise from the function $$ F (t) = \frac {t e ^ {t}}{e ^ {t} - 1} $$ and are defined by the series expansion $$ F (t) = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} B _ {k} \frac {t ^ {k}}{k !} \cdot $$ Their relation to the zeta function gives them a special arithmetic significance. The first Bernoulli numbers are $$ B _ {0} = 1, B _ {1} = \frac {1}{2}, B _ {2} = \frac {1}{6}, B _ {3} = 0, B _ {4} = - \frac {1}{3 0}, B _ {5} = 0, B _ {6} = \frac {1}{4 2}. $$ In general one has $B_{2\nu+1}=0$ for $\nu\geq1$ , because $F(-t)=F(t)-t$ . In the classical literature, it is usually the function $\frac{t}{e^{t}-1}$ , which serves for defining the Bernoulli numbers. As $F(t)=\frac{t}{e^{t}-1}+t$ , this does not change anything except for $B_{1}$ , where one finds $-\frac{1}{2}$ instead of $\frac{1}{2}$ . But the above definition is more natural and better suited for the further development of the theory. We now prove the remarkable | — |
| `FC09-C07-U012` | Theorem (1.8) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17969 | (1.8) Theorem. For every integer $k > 0$ one has $$ \zeta (1 - k) = - \frac {B _ {k}}{k} \cdot $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U013` | Lemma (1.9) | Chapter VII, §1, source L17985 | (1.9) Lemma. Let $U$ be an open subset of $\mathbb{C}$ that contains the path $C_{\varepsilon, a}$ and also the interior of $K_{\varepsilon}$ . Let $G(z)$ be a holomorphic function on $U \setminus \{0\}$ with a pole of order $m$ at $0$ , and let $G(t)t^{ns-1} (n \in \mathbb{N})$ , for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > \frac{m}{n}$ , be integrable on $(0, a)$ . Then one has $$ \int_ {C _ {\varepsilon , a}} G (z) z ^ {n s - 1} d z = (e ^ {2 \pi i n s} - 1) \int_ {0} ^ {a} G (t) t ^ {n s - 1} d t. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U014` | Corollary (1.10) | Chapter VII, §1, source L18123 | (1.10) Corollary. The values of $\zeta(s)$ at the positive even integers $s = 2k$ , $k = 1, 2, 3, \ldots$ , are given by $$ \zeta (2 k) = (- 1) ^ {k - 1} \frac {(2 \pi) ^ {2 k}}{2 (2 k) !} B _ {2 k}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U015` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L18135 | The image $R_{2k}$ of a nonzero element in $\tilde{K}_{4k-1}(\mathbb{Z}) \otimes_{\mathbb{Z}} \mathbb{Q}$ is called the 2k-th regulator. It is well-determined up to a rational factor, i.e., it is an element of $R^{*}/Q^{*}$ , and one has $$ \zeta (2 k - 1) \equiv R _ {2 k} \bmod \mathbb {Q} ^ {*}. $$ This discovery of the Swiss mathematician ARMAND BOREL has had a tremendous influence on further arithmetical research, and has opened up deep insights into the arithmetic nature of zeta functions and L-series of the most general kind. These insights are summarized within the comprehensive Beilinson conjecture (see [117]). In the meantime, the mathematicians SPENCER BLOCH and KAZUYA KATO have found a complete description of the special zeta values $\zeta(2k-1)$ (i.e., not just a description mod $Q^{*}$ ) via a new theory of the Tamagawa measure. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U016` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §1, source L18143 | The zeroes of the Riemann zeta function command special attention. Euler's identity (1.1) shows that one has $\zeta(s) \neq 0$ for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$ . The gamma function $\Gamma(s)$ is nowhere 0 and has simple poles at $s = 0, -1, -2, \ldots$ . The functional equation $Z(s) = Z(1 - s)$ , i.e., $$ \pi^ {- s / 2} \Gamma (s / 2) \zeta (s) = \pi^ {(s - 1) / 2} \Gamma ((1 - s) / 2) \zeta (1 - s), $$ therefore shows that the only zeroes of $\zeta(s)$ in the domain $\operatorname{Re}(s) < 0$ are the poles of $\Gamma(s/2)$ , i.e., the arguments $s = -2, -4, -6, \ldots$ . These are called the trivial zeroes of $\zeta(s)$ . Other zeroes have to lie in the critical strip $0 \leq \operatorname{Re}(s) \leq 1$ , since $\zeta(s) \neq 0$ for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$ . They are the subject of the famous, still unproven Riemann Hypothesis: The non-trivial zeroes of $\zeta(s)$ lie on the line $\operatorname{Re}(s) = \frac{1}{2}$ . | `FC09-C07-U001` |
| `FC09-C07-U017` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §2, source L18240 | The most immediate relatives of the Riemann zeta function are the Dirichlet L-series. They are defined as follows. Let m be a natural number. A Dirichlet character mod m is by definition a character $$ \chi : (\mathbb {Z} / m \mathbb {Z}) ^ {*} \longrightarrow S ^ {1} = \left\{z \in \mathbb {C} \mid \| z \| = 1 \right\}. $$ It is called primitive if it does not arise as the composite $$ (\mathbb {Z} / m \mathbb {Z}) ^ {*} \longrightarrow (\mathbb {Z} / m ^ {\prime} \mathbb {Z}) ^ {*} \stackrel {\chi^ {\prime}} {\longrightarrow} S ^ {1} $$ of a Dirichlet character $\chi'$ mod $m'$ for any proper divisor $m'\|m$ . In the general case, the gcd of all such divisors is called the conductor $f$ of $\chi$ . So $\chi$ is always induced from a primitive character $\chi'$ mod $f$ . Given $\chi$ , we define the multiplicative function $\chi: \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{C}$ by $$ \chi (n) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \chi (n \bmod m) & \text { for } (n, m) = 1, \\ 0 & \text { for } (n, m) \neq 1. \end{array} \right. $$ The trivial character $\chi^{0} \mod m$ , $\chi^{0}(n)=1$ for $(n,m)=1$ , $\chi^{0}(n)=0$ for $(n,m)\neq1$ , plays a special role. When read mod 1, we denote it by $\chi=1$ . It is also called the principal character. Considering it in the theory to be developed now has the effect of subsuming here everything we have done in the last section. For a Dirichlet character $\chi$ , we form the Dirichlet L-series | — |
| `FC09-C07-U018` | Proposition (2.1) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18268 | (2.1) Proposition. The series $L(\chi, s)$ converges absolutely and uniformly in the domain $\operatorname{Re}(s) \geq 1 + \delta$ , for any $\delta > 0$ . It therefore represents an analytic function on the half-plane $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$ . We have Euler's identity $$ L (\chi , s) = \prod_ {p} {\frac {1}{1 - \chi (p) p ^ {- s}}}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U019` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §2, source L18278 | The proof again hinges on an integral representation of the function $L(\chi, s)$ which has the effect of realizing it as the Mellin transform of a theta series. We do, however, have to distinguish now between even and odd Dirichlet characters $\chi \mod m$ . This phenomenon will become increasingly important when we generalize further. We define the exponent $p \in \{0, 1\}$ of $\chi$ by $$ \chi (- 1) = (- 1) ^ {p} \chi (1). $$ Then the rule $$ \chi ((n)) = \chi (n) \left(\frac {n}{\| n \|}\right) ^ {p} $$ defines a multiplicative function on the semigroup of all ideals $(n)$ which are relatively prime to m. This function is called a Größencharakter mod m. These Größencharaktere are capable of substantial generalization and will play the leading part when we consider higher algebraic number fields (see §7). | — |
| `FC09-C07-U020` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §2, source L18340 | We view the factor $$ L _ {\infty} (\chi , s) = \left(\frac {m}{\pi}\right) ^ {s / 2} \Gamma (\chi , s) $$ in (\*) as the “Euler factor” at the infinite prime. It joins with the Euler factors $L_{p}(s)=1/(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})$ of the product representation (2.1) of $L(\chi,s)$ to define the completed L-series of the character $\chi$ : $$ \Lambda (\chi , s) = L _ {\infty} (\chi , s) L (\chi , s), \quad \operatorname{Re} (s) > 1. $$ | `FC09-C07-U018` |
| `FC09-C07-U021` | Proposition (2.2) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18354 | (2.2) Proposition. The function $\Lambda(\chi, s)$ admits the integral representation $$ \Lambda (\chi , s) = \frac {c (\chi)}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \left(\theta (\chi , i y) - \chi (0)\right) y ^ {(s + p) / 2} \frac {d y}{y}, $$ where $c(\chi) = (\frac{\pi}{m})^{p / 2}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U022` | Proposition (2.3) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18366 | (2.3) Proposition. Let $a, b, \mu$ be real numbers, $\mu > 0$ . Then the series $$ \theta_ {\mu} (a, b, z) = \sum_ {g \in \mu \mathbb {Z}} e ^ {\pi i (a + g) ^ {2} z + 2 \pi i b g} $$ converges absolutely and uniformly in the domain $\operatorname{Im}(z) \geq \delta$ , for every $\delta > 0$ , and for $z \in \mathbb{H}$ , one has the transformation formula $$ \theta_ {\mu} (a, b, - 1 / z) = e ^ {- 2 \pi i a b} \frac {\sqrt {z / i}}{\mu} \theta_ {1 / \mu} (- b, a, z). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U023` | Corollary (2.4) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18398 | (2.4) Corollary. For $a, b, \mu \in \mathbb{R}$ , $\mu > 0$ , one has the transformation formula $$ \theta_ {\mu} ^ {p} (a, b, - 1 / z) = \left[ i ^ {p} e ^ {2 \pi i a b} \mu \right] ^ {- 1} (z / i) ^ {p + \frac {1}{2}} \theta_ {1 / \mu} ^ {p} (- b, a, z). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U024` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §2, source L18404 | This corollary gives us the required transformation formula for the theta series $\theta (\chi ,a)$ , if we introduce the Gauss sums which are defined as follows. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U025` | Definition (2.5) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18406 | (2.5) Definition. For $n \in Z$ , the Gauss sum $\tau(\chi, n)$ associated to the Dirichlet character $\chi \mod m$ is defined to be the complex number $$ \tau (\chi , n) = \sum_ {\nu = 0} ^ {m - 1} \chi (\nu) e ^ {2 \pi i \nu n / m}. $$ For $n = 1$ , we write $\tau(\chi) = \tau(\chi, 1)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U026` | Proposition (2.6) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18414 | (2.6) Proposition. For a primitive Dirichlet character $\chi \bmod m$ , one has $$ \tau (\chi , n) = \overline {{{{\chi}}}} (n) \tau (\chi) \quad a n d \quad \| \tau (\chi) \| = \sqrt {m}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U027` | Proposition (2.7) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18438 | (2.7) Proposition. If $\chi$ is a primitive Dirichlet character mod $m$ , then we have the transformation formula $$ \theta (\chi , - 1 / z) = \frac {\tau (\chi)}{i ^ {p} \sqrt {m}} (z / i) ^ {p + \frac {1}{2}} \theta (\overline {{\chi}}, z), $$ where $\overline{\chi}$ is the complex conjugate character to $\chi$ , i.e., its inverse. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U028` | Theorem (2.8) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18484 | (2.8) Theorem. If $\chi$ is a nontrivial primitive Dirichlet character, then the completed L-series $\Lambda(\chi,s)$ admits an analytic continuation to the whole complex plane C and satisfies the functional equation $$ \Lambda (\chi , s) = W (\chi) \Lambda (\overline {{{{\chi}}}}, 1 - s) $$ with the factor $W(\chi) = \frac{\tau(\chi)}{i^p\sqrt{m}}$ . This factor has absolute value 1. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U029` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §2, source L18524 | The behaviour of the special values at integer arguments of the Riemann zeta function generalizes to the Dirichlet L-series $L(\chi, s)$ if we introduce, for nontrivial primitive Dirichlet characters $\chi \mod m$ , the generalized Bernoulli numbers $B_{k, \chi}$ defined by the formula $$ F _ {\chi} (t) = \sum_ {a = 1} ^ {m} \chi (a) \frac {t e ^ {a t}}{e ^ {m t} - 1} = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} B _ {k, \chi} \frac {t ^ {k}}{k !}. $$ These are algebraic numbers which lie in the field $\mathbb{Q}(\chi)$ generated by the values of $\chi$ . Since $$ F _ {\chi} (- t) = \sum_ {a = 1} ^ {m} \chi (- 1) \chi (m - a) \frac {t e ^ {(m - a) t}}{e ^ {m t} - 1} = \chi (- 1) F _ {\chi} (t), $$ we find $(-1)^{k}B_{k,\chi} = \chi (-1)B_{k,\chi}$ , so that | — |
| `FC09-C07-U030` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §2, source L18542 | $p \in \{0,1\}$ is defined by $\chi(-1) = (-1)^p \chi(1)$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U031` | Theorem (2.9) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18544 | (2.9) Theorem. For any integer $k \geq 1$ , one has $$ L (\chi , 1 - k) = - \frac {B _ {k , \chi}}{k}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U032` | Corollary (2.10) | Chapter VII, §2, source L18596 | (2.10) Corollary. For $k \equiv p \mod 2$ , $k \geq 1$ , one has $$ L (\chi , k) = (- 1) ^ {1 + (k - p) / 2} \frac {\tau (\chi)}{2 i ^ {p}} \Bigl (\frac {2 \pi}{m} \Bigr) ^ {k} \frac {B _ {k , \overline {{\chi}}}}{k !}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U033` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §3, source L18634 | The familiar objects $\mathbb{C}$ , $\mathbb{R}$ , $\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}$ , $\mathbb{H}$ , $\|\cdot\|$ , log, find their higher dimensional analogues as follows. Let $X$ be a finite $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set, i.e., a finite set with an involution $\tau \mapsto \overline{\tau}$ ( $\tau \in X$ ), and let $n = \# X$ . We consider the $n$ -dimensional $\mathbb{C}$ -algebra $$ \mathbf {C} = \prod_ {\tau \in X} \mathbb {C} $$ of all tuples $z = (z_{\tau})_{\tau \in X}$ , $z_{\tau} \in C$ , with componentwise addition and multiplication. If $z = (z_{\tau}) \in \mathbf{C}$ , then the element $\bar{z} \in C$ is defined to have the following components: $$ (\bar {z}) _ {\tau} = \bar {z} _ {\overline {{\tau}}}. $$ We call the involution $z \mapsto \bar{z}$ the conjugation on C. In addition, we have the involutions $z \mapsto z^{*}$ and $z \mapsto ^{*}z$ given by $$ z _ {\tau} ^ {*} = z _ {\overline {{{\tau}}}}, \quad \text {resp.} \quad {} ^ {*} z _ {\tau} = \bar {z} _ {\tau}. $$ One clearly has $\bar{z} = {}^{*}z^{*}$ . The set $$ \mathbf {R} = \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {C} \right] ^ {+} = \left\{z \in \mathbf {C} \mid z = \bar {z} \right\} $$ forms an $n$ -dimensional commutative $\mathbb{R}$ -algebra, and $\mathbf{C} = \mathbf{R} \otimes_{\mathbb{R}} \mathbb{C}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U034` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §3, source L18662 | For the additive, resp. multiplicative, group C, resp. $C^{*}$ , we have the homomorphism $$ \begin{array}{l} T r: \mathbf {C} \to \mathbb {C}, \quad T r (z) = \sum_ {\tau} z _ {\tau}, \quad \text {resp.} \\ N: \mathbf {C} ^ {*} \to \mathbb {C} ^ {*}, N (z) = \prod_ {\tau} z _ {\tau}. \end{array} $$ Here $Tr(z)$ , resp. $N(z)$ , denotes the trace, resp. the determinant, of the endomorphism $C \rightarrow C$ , $x \mapsto zx$ . Furthermore we have on C the hermitian scalar product $$ \langle x, y \rangle = \sum_ {\tau} x _ {\tau} \bar {y} _ {\tau} = T r (x ^ {*} y). $$ It is invariant under conjugation, $\overline{\langle x,y\rangle}=\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle$ , and restricting it yields a scalar product $\langle,\rangle$ , i.e., a euclidean metric, on the R-vector space R. If $z\in C$ , then $^{*}z$ is the adjoint element with respect to $\langle,\rangle$ , i.e., $$ \langle x z, y \rangle = \langle x, ^ {*} z y \rangle . $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U035` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §3, source L18680 | In $\mathbf{R}$ , we consider the subspace $$ \mathbf {R} _ {\pm} = \left\{x \in \mathbf {R} \mid x = x ^ {*} \right\} = \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {R} \right] ^ {+}. $$ Thus we find for the components of $x = (x_{\tau}) \in \mathbf{R}_{\pm}$ that $x_{\overline{\tau}} = x_{\tau} \in R$ . If $\delta \in R$ , we simply write $x > \delta$ to signify that $x_{\tau} > \delta$ for all $\tau$ . The multiplicative group $$ \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} = \left\{x \in \mathbf {R} _ {\pm} \mid x > 0 \right\} = \left[ \prod_ {\tau} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \right] ^ {+} $$ will play a particularly important part. It consists of the tuples $x = (x_{\tau})$ of positive real numbers $x_{\tau}$ such that $x_{\overline{\tau}} = x_{\tau}$ , and it occurs in the two homomorphisms $$ \| \quad \|: \mathbf {R} ^ {*} \longrightarrow \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \quad x = (x _ {\tau}) \longmapsto \| x \| = (\| x _ {\tau} \|), $$ $$ \log : \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbf {R} _ {\pm}, \quad x = (x _ {\tau}) \longmapsto \log x = (\log x _ {\tau}). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U036` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §3, source L18702 | We finally define the upper half-space associated to the $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set X by $$ \mathbf {H} = \mathbf {R} _ {\pm} + i \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}. $$ Putting $\operatorname{Re}(z) = \frac{1}{2} (z + \bar{z})$ , $\operatorname{Im}(z) = \frac{1}{2i} (z - \bar{z})$ , we may also write $$ \mathbf {H} = \left\{z \in \mathbf {C} \mid z = z ^ {*}, \operatorname{Im} (z) > 0 \right\}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U037` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §3, source L18716 | For two tuples $z = (z_{\tau})$ , $p = (p_{\tau}) \in \mathbf{C}$ , the power $$ z ^ {p} = (z _ {\tau} ^ {p _ {\tau}}) \in \mathbf {C} $$ is well-defined by $$ z _ {\tau} ^ {p _ {\tau}} = e ^ {p _ {\tau} \log z _ {\tau}}, $$ if we agree to take the principal branch of the logarithm and assume that the $z_{\tau}$ move only in the plane cut along the negative real axis. The table $$ \mathbb {H} \subseteq \mathbb {C} \supseteq \mathbb {R} = \mathbb {R} \supseteq \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \| \|: \mathbb {R} ^ {*} \rightarrow \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \log : \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbb {R}, $$ $$ \mathbf {H} \subseteq \mathbf {C} \supseteq \mathbf {R} \supseteq \mathbf {R} _ {\pm} \supseteq \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \quad \| \|: \mathbf {R} ^ {*} \rightarrow \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \quad \log : \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbf {R} _ {\pm}, $$ shows the analogy of the notions introduced with the familiar ones in the case n = 1. We recommend that the reader memorize them well, for they will be used constantly in what follows without special cross-reference. This also includes the notation $$ \bar {z}, z ^ {*}, ^ {*} z, T r, N, \langle , \rangle , x > \delta , z ^ {p}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U038` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §3, source L18744 | The functional equations we are envisaging originate in a general formula from functional analysis, the Poisson summation formula. It will be proved first. A Schwartz function (or rapidly decreasing function) on a euclidean vector space R is by definition a $C^{\infty}$ -function $f: R \to C$ which tends to zero as $x \to \infty$ , even if multiplied by an arbitrary power $\\|x\\|^{m}, m \geq 0$ , and which shares this behaviour with all its derivatives. For every Schwartz function f, one forms the Fourier transform $$ \widehat {f} (y) = \int_ {\mathbf {R}} f (x) e ^ {- 2 \pi i \langle x, y \rangle} d x, $$ where dx is the Haar measure on R associated to $\langle\ ,\ \rangle$ which ascribes the volume 1 to the cube spanned by an orthonormal basis, i.e., it is the Haar measure which is selfdual with respect to $\langle\ ,\ \rangle$ . The improper integral converges absolutely and uniformly and gives again a Schwartz function $\widehat{f}$ . This is easily proved by elementary analytical techniques; we refer also to [98], chap. XIV. The prototype of a Schwartz function is the function $$ h (x) = e ^ {- \pi \langle x, x \rangle}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U039` | Proposition (3.1) | Chapter VII, §3, source L18758 | (3.1) Proposition. (i) The function $h(x) = e^{-\pi \langle x, x \rangle}$ is its own Fourier transform. (ii) If $f$ is an arbitrary Schwartz function and $A$ is a linear transformation of $\mathbf{R}$ , then the function $f_{A}(x) = f(Ax)$ has Fourier transform $$ \widehat {f} _ {A} (y) = \frac {1}{\| \det A \|} \widehat {f} (^ {t} A ^ {- 1} y), $$ where $^{t}A$ is the adjoint transformation of A. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U040` | Poisson Summation Formula (3.2) | Chapter VII, §3, source L18790 | (3.2) Poisson Summation Formula. Let $\Gamma$ be a complete lattice in $\mathbf{R}$ and let $$ \Gamma^ {\prime} = \left\{g ^ {\prime} \in \mathbf {R} \mid \langle g, g ^ {\prime} \rangle \in \mathbb {Z} \text { for all } g \in \Gamma \right\} $$ be the lattice dual to $\Gamma$ . Then for any Schwartz function f, one has: $$ \sum_ {g \in \Gamma} f (g) = \frac {1}{\operatorname{vol} (\Gamma)} \sum_ {g ^ {\prime} \in \Gamma^ {\prime}} \widehat {f} (g ^ {\prime}), $$ where $\operatorname{vol}(\Gamma)$ is the volume of a fundamental mesh of $\Gamma$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U041` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §3, source L18864 | We apply the Poisson summation formula to the functions $$ f _ {p} (a, b, x) = N \left((x + a) ^ {p}\right) e ^ {- \pi \langle a + x, a + x \rangle + 2 \pi i \langle b, x \rangle} $$ with the parameters $a, b \in R$ and a tuple $p = (p_{\tau})$ of nonnegative integers, such that $p_{\tau} \in \{0, 1\}$ if $\tau = \overline{\tau}$ , and $p_{\tau} p_{\overline{\tau}} = 0$ if $\tau \neq \overline{\tau}$ . Such an element $p \in \prod_{\tau} Z$ will henceforth be called admissible. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U042` | Proposition (3.3) | Chapter VII, §3, source L18872 | (3.3) Proposition. The function $f(x) = f_{p}(a, b, x)$ is a Schwartz function on $\mathbf{R}$ . Its Fourier transform is $$ \widehat {f} (y) = \left[ i ^ {T r (p)} e ^ {2 \pi i \langle a, b \rangle} \right] ^ {- 1} f _ {p} (- b, a, y). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U043` | Definition (3.4) | Chapter VII, §3, source L18958 | (3.4) Definition. For every complete lattice $\Gamma$ of $\mathbf{R}$ , we define the theta series $$ \theta_ {\Gamma} (z) = \sum_ {g \in \Gamma} e ^ {\pi i \langle g z, g \rangle}, \quad z \in \mathbf {H}. $$ More generally, for $a, b \in \mathbf{R}$ and any admissible $p \in \prod_{\tau} \mathbb{Z}$ , we put $$ \theta_ {\Gamma} ^ {p} (a, b, z) = \sum_ {g \in \Gamma} N \big ((a + g) ^ {p} \big) e ^ {\pi i \langle (a + g) z, a + g \rangle + 2 \pi i \langle b, g \rangle}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U044` | Proposition (3.5) | Chapter VII, §3, source L18970 | (3.5) Proposition. The series $\theta_{\Gamma}^{p}(a,b,z)$ converges absolutely and uniformly on every compact subset of $R \times R \times H$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U045` | Theta Transformation Formula (3.6) | Chapter VII, §3, source L19014 | (3.6) Theta Transformation Formula. One has $$ \theta_ {\Gamma} ^ {p} (a, b, - 1 / z) = \left[ i ^ {T r (p)} e ^ {2 \pi i \langle a, b \rangle} \mathrm{vol} (\Gamma) \right] ^ {- 1} N \Big ((z / i) ^ {p + \frac {1}{2}} \Big) \theta_ {\Gamma^ {\prime}} ^ {p} (- b, a, z). $$ In particular, one has for the function $\theta_{\Gamma}(z) = \theta_{\Gamma}^{0}(0,0,z)$ : $$ \theta_ {\Gamma} (- 1 / z) = \frac {\sqrt {N (z / i)}}{\operatorname{vol} (\Gamma)} \theta_ {\Gamma^ {\prime}} (z). $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U046` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §4, source L19108 | In order to generalize this process, we now introduce a higher-dimensional gamma function for every finite $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set X, building upon the notation of the last section. First we fix a Haar measure on the multiplicative group $R_{+}^{*}$ : Let $\mathfrak{p} = \{\tau, \overline{\tau}\}$ be the conjugation classes in $X$ . We call $\mathfrak{p}$ real or complex, depending whether $\# \mathfrak{p} = 1$ or $\# \mathfrak{p} = 2$ . We then have $$ \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathbf {R} _ {+ \mathfrak {p}} ^ {*}, $$ where $$ \mathbf {R} _ {+ \mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}, \quad \text { resp. } \quad \mathbf {R} _ {+ \mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} = \left[ \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \times \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \right] ^ {+} = \left\{(y, y) \mid y \in \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \right\}. $$ We define isomorphisms $$ \mathbf {R} _ {+ \mathfrak {p}} ^ {*} \stackrel {\sim} {\longrightarrow} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*} $$ by $y \mapsto y$ , resp. $(y, y) \mapsto y^2$ , and obtain an isomorphism $$ \varphi : \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathbb {R} _ {+} ^ {*}. $$ We now denote by $\frac{dy}{y}$ the Haar measure on $R_{+}^{*}$ which corresponds to the product measure $$ \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \frac {d t}{t}, $$ where $\frac{dt}{t}$ is the usual Haar measure on $\mathbb{R}_+^*$ . The Haar measure thus defined is called the canonical measure on $\mathbf{R}_+^*$ . Under the logarithm $$ \log : \mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \mathbf {R} _ {\pm}, $$ it is mapped to the Haar measure dx on $R_{\pm}$ which under the isomorphism $$ \mathbf {R} _ {\pm} = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathbf {R} _ {\pm \mathfrak {p}} \xrightarrow {\varphi} \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \mathbb {R}, $$ $x_{\mathfrak{p}} \mapsto x_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , resp. $(x_{\mathfrak{p}}, x_{\mathfrak{p}}) \mapsto 2x_{\mathfrak{p}}$ , corresponds to the Lebesgue measure on $\prod_{\mathfrak{p}} \mathbb{R}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U047` | Definition (4.1) | Chapter VII, §4, source L19154 | (4.1) Definition. For $\mathbf{s} = (s_{\tau}) \in \mathbf{C}$ such that $\operatorname{Re}(s_{\tau}) > 0$ , we define the gamma function associated to the $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set X by $$ \Gamma_ {X} (\mathbf {s}) = \int_ {\mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}} N (e ^ {- y} y ^ {\mathbf {s}}) \frac {d y}{y}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U048` | Proposition (4.2) | Chapter VII, §4, source L19162 | (4.2) Proposition. Decomposing the $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set $X$ into its conjugation classes $\mathfrak{p}$ , one has $$ \Gamma_ {X} (\mathbf {s}) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \Gamma_ {\mathfrak {p}} (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}), $$ where $\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}} = s_{\tau}$ for $\mathfrak{p} = \{\tau\}$ , resp. $\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}} = (s_{\tau}, s_{\overline{\tau}})$ for $\mathfrak{p} = \{\tau, \overline{\tau}\}$ , $\tau \neq \overline{\tau}$ . The factors are given explicitly by $$ \Gamma_ {\mathfrak {p}} (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \Gamma (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}), & \text {if \mathfrak {p} real}, \\ 2 ^ {1 - T r (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}})} \Gamma (T r (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}})), & \text {if \mathfrak {p} complex}, \end{array} \right. $$ where $Tr(\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}}) = s_{\tau} + s_{\overline{\tau}}$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U049` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §4, source L19202 | We call the function $$ L _ {X} (\mathbf {s}) = N (\pi^ {- \mathbf {s} / 2}) \Gamma_ {X} (\mathbf {s} / 2) $$ the $L$ -function of the $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set $X$ . Decomposing $X$ into the conjugation classes $\mathfrak{p}$ , yields $$ L _ {X} (\mathbf {s}) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} L _ {\mathfrak {p}} (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}), $$ where as before we write $\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}} = s_{\tau}$ for $\mathfrak{p} = \{\tau\}$ and $\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}} = (s_{\tau}, s_{\overline{\tau}})$ for $\mathfrak{p} = \{\tau, \overline{\tau}\}$ , $\tau \neq \overline{\tau}$ . The factors $L_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbf{s}_{\mathfrak{p}})$ are given explicitly, by (4.2), as $$ L _ {\mathfrak {p}} (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \pi^ {- \mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}} / 2} \Gamma (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}} / 2), & \text {if \mathfrak {p} real}, \\ 2 (2 \pi) ^ {- T r (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}) / 2} \Gamma (T r (\mathbf {s} _ {\mathfrak {p}}) / 2), & \text {if \mathfrak {p} complex}. \end{array} \right. $$ For a single complex variable $s \in \mathbb{C}$ , we put $$ \Gamma_ {X} (s) = \Gamma_ {X} (s {\bf 1}), $$ where $\mathbf{1} = (1, \ldots, 1)$ is the unit element of $\mathbf{C}$ . Denoting $r_1$ , resp. $r_2$ , the number of real, resp. complex, conjugation classes of $X$ , we find $$ \Gamma_ {X} (s) = 2 ^ {(1 - 2 s) r _ {2}} \Gamma (s) ^ {r _ {1}} \Gamma (2 s) ^ {r _ {2}}. $$ In the same way we put $$ L _ {X} (s) = L _ {X} (s \mathbf {1}) = \pi^ {- n s / 2} \Gamma_ {X} (s / 2), \quad n = \# X, $$ and in particular $$ L _ {\mathbb {R}} (s) = L _ {X} (s) = \pi^ {- s / 2} \Gamma (s / 2), \quad \mathrm{if} \quad X = \{\tau \}, $$ $$ L _ {\mathbb {C}} (s) = L _ {X} (s) = 2 (2 \pi) ^ {- s} \Gamma (s), \quad \text { if } \quad X = \{\tau , \overline {{\tau}} \}, \tau \neq \overline {{\tau}}. $$ Then we have, for an arbitrary $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set $X$ : $$ L _ {X} (s) = L _ {\mathbb {R}} (s) ^ {r _ {1}} L _ {\mathbb {C}} (s) ^ {r _ {2}}. $$ | `FC09-C07-U048` |
| `FC09-C07-U050` | Proposition (4.3) | Chapter VII, §4, source L19256 | (4.3) Proposition. (i) $L_{\mathbb{R}}(1) = 1, L_{\mathbb{C}}(1) = \frac{1}{\pi}$ . (ii) $L_{\mathbb{R}}(s + 2) = \frac{s}{2\pi} L_{\mathbb{R}}(s), L_{\mathbb{C}}(s + 1) = \frac{s}{2\pi} L_{\mathbb{C}}(s).$ $$ \text {(iii)} L _ {\mathbb {R}} (1 - s) L _ {\mathbb {R}} (1 + s) = \frac {1}{\cos \pi s / 2}, \quad L _ {\mathbb {C}} (s) L _ {\mathbb {C}} (1 - s) = \frac {2}{\sin \pi s}. $$ (iv) $L_{\mathbb{R}}(s)L_{\mathbb{R}}(s + 1) = L_{\mathbb{C}}(s)$ (Legendre's duplication formula). | — |
| `FC09-C07-U051` | Proposition (4.4) | Chapter VII, §4, source L19268 | (4.4) Proposition. $L_{X}(s) = A(s)L_{X}(1 - s)$ with the factor $$ A (s) = (\cos \pi s / 2) ^ {r _ {1} + r _ {2}} (\sin \pi s / 2) ^ {r _ {2}} L _ {\mathbb {C}} (s) ^ {n}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U052` | Definition (5.1) | Chapter VII, §5, source L19294 | (5.1) Definition. The Dedekind zeta function of the number field $K$ is defined by the series $$ \zeta_ {K} (s) = \sum_ {\mathfrak {a}} \frac {1}{\mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {a}) ^ {s}}, $$ where $\mathfrak{a}$ varies over the integral ideals of $K$ , and $\mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{a})$ denotes their absolute norm. | — |
| `FC09-C07-U053` | Proposition (5.2) | Chapter VII, §5, source L19302 | (5.2) Proposition. The series $\zeta_K(s)$ converge absolutely and uniformly in the domain $\operatorname{Re}(s) \geq 1 + \delta$ for every $\delta > 0$ , and one has $$ \zeta_ {K} (s) = \prod_ {\mathfrak {p}} \frac {1}{1 - \mathfrak {N} (\mathfrak {p}) ^ {- s}}, $$ where $\mathfrak{p}$ runs through the prime ideals of $K$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U054` | Convention / notation | Chapter VII, §5, source L19326 | The functional equation is then proved for the individual functions $\zeta(\mathfrak{K}, s)$ . The integral ideals in $\mathfrak{K}$ are described as follows. If $a$ is a fractional ideal, then the unit group $\mathcal{O}^*$ of $\mathcal{O}$ operates on the set $a^* = a \setminus \{0\}$ , and we denote by $a^*/\mathcal{O}^*$ the set of orbits, i.e., the set of classes of non-zero associated elements in $a$ . | — |
| `FC09-C07-U055` | Lemma (5.3) | Chapter VII, §5, source L19328 | (5.3) Lemma. Let $\mathfrak{a}$ be an integral ideal of $K$ and $\mathfrak{K}$ the class of the ideal $\mathfrak{a}^{-1}$ . Then there is a bijection $$ \mathfrak {a} ^ {*} / \mathcal {O} ^ {*} \stackrel {{\sim}} {{\longrightarrow}} \left\{\mathfrak {b} \in \mathfrak {K} \mid \mathfrak {b} \text { integral } \right\}, \quad \bar {a} \longmapsto \mathfrak {b} = a \mathfrak {a} ^ {- 1}. $$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U056` | Proposition (5.4) | Chapter VII, §5, source L19350 | (5.4) Proposition. $\zeta(\mathfrak{K},s) = \mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{a})^s\sum_{\overline{a}\in \mathfrak{a}^* /\mathcal{O}^*}\frac{1}{\|N(\overline{a})\|^s}.$ | — |
| `FC09-C07-U057` | Definition / construction | Chapter VII, §5, source L19352 | By chap. I, (5.2), the ideal $\mathfrak{a}$ forms a complete lattice in $\mathbf{R}$ whose fundamental mesh has volume $$ \operatorname{vol} (\mathfrak {a}) = \sqrt {d _ {\mathfrak {a}}}, $$ where $d_{\mathfrak{a}} = \mathfrak{N}(\mathfrak{a})^{2}\|d_{K}\|$ denotes the absolute value of the discriminant of $\mathfrak{a}$ , and $d_{K}$ is the discriminant of $K$ . To the series $\zeta(\mathfrak{K}, s)$ we associate the theta series $$ \theta (\mathfrak {a}, z) = \theta_ {\mathfrak {a}} (z / d _ {\mathfrak {a}} ^ {1 / n}) = \sum_ {a \in \mathfrak {a}} e ^ {\pi i \langle a z / d _ {\mathfrak {a}} ^ {1 / n}, a \rangle}. $$ It is related to $\zeta(\mathcal{K},s)$ via the gamma integral associated to the $G(\mathbb{C}\|\mathbb{R})$ -set $X=\operatorname{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})$ , $$ \Gamma_ {K} (s) = \Gamma_ {X} (s) = \int_ {\mathbf {R} _ {+} ^ {*}} N (e ^ {- y} y ^ {s}) \frac {d y}{y}, $$ | `FC09-C01-U048`, `FC09-C07-U053` |
